A Vindication of the Rights of Woman

Written in six weeks, three years after the Revolution: women are not born frivolous, they are made so by an education that gives them nothing else to be

by Mary Wollstonecraft

To Monsieur Talleyrand-Perigord, Late Bishop of Autun

Sir,

I have read with great pleasure the pamphlet you have lately published on national education, and I dedicate this volume to you. It is the first dedication I have ever written. I write it to persuade you to read the book carefully, and because I believe you will understand me — which is more than I expect of the many pert little wits who may ridicule arguments they are unable to answer. But, sir, my respect for your understanding goes further than that. It goes so far that I am confident you will not throw my work aside and conclude in a hurry that I am wrong simply because you did not see the subject in the same light yourself.

Forgive my frankness, but I must say that you treated it far too quickly, content to consider it as it had always been considered — back when the rights of man, let alone of woman, were dismissed as fantasy. So I call on you now to weigh what I have said about the rights of woman and about national education, and I call in the firm tone of one human being addressing another. My arguments, sir, come from no private interest: I plead for my sex, not for myself. I have long thought independence the great blessing of life and the basis of every virtue, and I will always secure my own by keeping my wants small, even if it meant living on a bare hillside.

It is an affection for the whole human race, then, that sends my pen racing along in support of what I believe to be the cause of virtue. The same motive makes me long to see women placed where they would advance those glorious principles that give morality some substance, instead of holding them back. My view of the rights and duties of women seems to me to follow so naturally from a few simple principles that I can hardly believe some of the broad minds who framed your admirable constitution will not agree with me.

There is certainly a wider spread of knowledge in France than anywhere else in Europe, and I attribute it largely to the long habit of social exchange between the sexes. I say plainly what I think, however: in France the very essence of sensuality has been distilled for the pleasure of the voluptuary, and a kind of sentimental lust has taken hold. That, together with the system of double-dealing taught by the whole tenor of French political and civil government, has given the national character a sinister sort of shrewdness — finesse is the proper word for it — and a polish of manners that damages the substance beneath by hunting sincerity out of society. And modesty, which is virtue's fairest dress, has been more grossly insulted in France than even in England, until French women have come to call it prudish to observe the decencies that animals attend to by instinct.

Manners and morals are so closely related that they are often confused; and though the first ought to be nothing but the natural reflection of the second, still, when various causes have produced artificial and corrupt manners — which are caught very early — morality becomes an empty word. That personal reserve, and that sacred respect for cleanliness and delicacy in family life, which French women almost despise, are the graceful pillars of modesty. Far from despising them, if the pure flame of patriotism has ever reached their hearts, they ought to be labouring to improve the morals of their fellow citizens by teaching men not only to respect modesty in women but to acquire it themselves, since that is the only way to deserve women's regard.

In arguing for the rights of women, my main case rests on this simple principle: that unless a woman is prepared by education to become the companion of a man, she will halt the progress of knowledge. Truth must be common to everyone, or it will have no real influence on how people actually behave. And how can a woman be expected to play her part unless she knows why she ought to be good? Unless freedom strengthens her reason until she understands her duty and sees how it connects with her own real welfare? If children are to be brought up to understand what love of country really means, their mother must love her country too; and a love of humankind, from which an orderly train of virtues springs, can only come from thinking about the moral and civil interests of humankind — and a woman's education and situation at present shut her out from any such enquiry.

In this book I have set out many arguments which seemed to me conclusive, to prove that the prevailing notion that women have a character of their own, distinct from human character, undermines morality. I have argued that if the human body and mind are to be improved, chastity must be far more general than it is; and that chastity will never be respected among men so long as a woman's person is idolised while she has little virtue or sense to give it the grand marks of mental beauty or the appealing simplicity of real affection.

Consider these observations calmly, sir. A glimpse of this truth seemed to open before you when you remarked "that to see one half of the human race excluded by the other from all participation of government, was a political phenomenon that, according to abstract principles, it was impossible to explain." If that is so, what does your constitution rest on? If the abstract rights of man will bear discussion and explanation, then by the same reasoning the rights of woman will not shrink from the same test — though a different opinion prevails in this country, founded on the very argument you use to justify the oppression of women: long custom.

Consider, and I address you as a legislator, whether it is not inconsistent and unjust, when men are contending for their own freedom and for the right to judge for themselves what will make them happy, to keep women in subjection — even though you firmly believe you are acting in the way best calculated to make them happy. Who made man the sole judge, if woman shares with him the gift of reason?

This is exactly how tyrants of every denomination argue, from the weak king down to the weak father of a family. They are all eager to crush reason, and they all insist that they have usurped its throne only in order to be useful. Do you not play a similar part when you force all women, by denying them civil and political rights, to stay shut up in their families groping in the dark? For surely, sir, you will not maintain that a duty can be binding which is not founded on reason? If domestic life really is what women are made for, then arguments can be drawn from reason to show it; and, so nobly supported, the more understanding women acquire the more attached to that duty they will be, because they will comprehend it. Unless they do comprehend it, unless their morals rest on the same unchanging principles as men's, no authority on earth can make them discharge it in any virtuous way. They may be convenient slaves, but slavery will always have its usual effect, degrading the master and the abject dependent alike.

But if women are to be excluded, without any voice of their own, from a share in the natural rights of humankind, then first prove — to fend off the charge of injustice and inconsistency — that they lack reason. Otherwise this flaw in your new constitution, the first constitution ever founded on reason, will always show that man must in some form act the tyrant; and tyranny, in whatever part of society it raises its brazen face, will always undermine morality.

I have said repeatedly, and produced what seem to me unanswerable arguments drawn from plain fact to prove it, that women cannot be confined by force to domestic concerns. However ignorant they are, they will meddle in weightier matters, neglecting their private duties only to disturb, by cunning tricks, the orderly plans of reason that rise above their understanding.

Besides, while women are trained only to acquire personal accomplishments, men will look for pleasure in variety, and unfaithful husbands will make unfaithful wives. Such ignorant creatures will indeed be very excusable when, never taught to respect the public good and never allowed a single civil right, they try to get justice for themselves by retaliation.

Once that box of mischief is open in a society, what is left to preserve private virtue, the only guarantee of public freedom and general happiness?

Let there be no coercion established in society, then; let the common law of gravity take its course, and the sexes will fall into their proper places. And now that more equitable laws are forming your citizens, marriage may become more sacred: your young men may choose their wives out of affection, and your young women may let love root out vanity.

The father of a family will not then weaken his constitution and debase his feelings by visiting the prostitute, nor forget, while obeying the call of appetite, the purpose for which that appetite was planted in him. And the mother will not neglect her children in order to practise the arts of coquetry, once good sense and modesty have secured her the friendship of her husband.

But until men become attentive to the duties of a father, it is useless to expect women to spend time in the nursery which, being "wise in their generation," they choose to spend at the mirror. This exercise of cunning is only an instinct of nature, a way of getting indirectly a little of the power they are unjustly refused a share in. If women are not allowed to enjoy legitimate rights, they will make both themselves and men vicious in order to obtain illegitimate ones.

I wish, sir, to set enquiries of this kind afloat in France; and if they should end by confirming my principles, then when your constitution comes to be revised, the rights of woman may be respected — if it is fully proved that reason calls for that respect, and loudly demands justice for one half of the human race.

I am, sir, yours respectfully,

M. W.

Introduction

I have looked back over the pages of history and watched the living world with anxious care, and the result has been the most melancholy kind of sorrowful indignation. It has weighed my spirits down. I have sighed as I was forced to admit that either nature has made a great difference between one human being and another, or else the civilization the world has achieved so far has been very partial indeed. I have turned over book after book written on education, and patiently observed how parents behave and how schools are run. And what has come of it? A deep conviction that the neglected education of my fellow creatures is the great source of the misery I am deploring — and that women in particular are made weak and wretched by a whole set of causes working together, all of them springing from one hasty conclusion.

The conduct and manners of women are proof enough that their minds are not in a healthy state. Like flowers planted in soil that is too rich, they have had strength and usefulness sacrificed to beauty, and the showy leaves, having pleased a fastidious eye, fade unnoticed on the stalk long before the season when they should have come to maturity. One cause of this barren blooming I put down to a false system of education, gathered out of books written on the subject by men who, treating women as women rather than as human creatures, have been more anxious to make them alluring mistresses than rational wives. Women's understanding has been so taken in by this plausible flattery that the civilized women of this century, with a few exceptions, are anxious only to inspire love, when they ought to be cherishing a nobler ambition and demanding respect for their abilities and their virtues.

In a book about the rights and manners of women, then, the works written specifically to improve them cannot be passed over — particularly when the charge is stated in so many words: that women's minds are weakened by false refinement; that books of instruction written by men of genius have had the same tendency as far more frivolous productions; and that, in the true Muslim style, women are considered only as females and not as part of the human species, at a time when improvable reason is allowed to be the dignified distinction that raises men above the animals and puts a natural sceptre into a feeble hand.

Still, because I am a woman, I would not have my readers suppose that I mean to stir up the disputed question of the equality or inferiority of the sexes. But the subject lies in my path, and I cannot step over it without laying the main drift of my reasoning open to misunderstanding, so I shall stop a moment to give my opinion in a few words. In the government of the physical world it is observable that the female is generally inferior to the male. The male pursues, the female yields — that is the law of nature, and it does not appear to have been suspended or repealed in favour of woman. This physical superiority cannot be denied, and it is a noble prerogative! But men, not content with this natural pre-eminence, work to sink us lower still, merely to make us alluring objects for a moment; and women, intoxicated by the adoration that men pay them under the influence of their senses, do not try to gain any lasting hold on their hearts, or to become the friends of the fellow creatures who find them amusing company.

I am aware of the obvious retort. From every quarter I have heard people exclaiming against masculine women — but where are they to be found? If by that name men mean to attack women's enthusiasm for hunting, shooting and gambling, I shall join in the cry most heartily. But if it is meant against women imitating manly virtues — or, to put it more properly, acquiring those talents and virtues whose exercise ennobles the human character, and which raise females in the scale of living beings when they are given the comprehensive name of mankind — then everyone who looks at the matter with a philosophical eye must, I should think, wish with me that they may every day grow more and more masculine.

This discussion divides the subject naturally. I shall first consider women in the grand light of human creatures who, like men, are placed on this earth to unfold their faculties; and afterwards I shall point out more particularly what is peculiar to them.

I want also to steer clear of a mistake that many respectable writers have fallen into. The instruction addressed to women so far has really been meant for ladies — with the exception of the little indirect advice scattered through Sandford and Merton. Speaking to my own sex in a firmer tone, I pay particular attention to those in the middle class, because they seem to me to be in the most natural condition. Perhaps the seeds of false refinement, immorality and vanity have always been scattered by the great. Weak, artificial beings, raised above the common wants and affections of their kind in a premature and unnatural way, undermine the very foundation of virtue and spread corruption through the whole mass of society. As a class they have the strongest claim on our pity: the education of the rich tends to make them vain and helpless, and the unfolding mind is not strengthened by practising the duties that give the human character its dignity. They live only to amuse themselves, and by the same law that produces certain effects everywhere in nature, they very soon become merely barren amusement.

But since I intend to look separately at the different ranks of society and at the moral character of women in each, this hint is enough for the present. I have touched on the subject only because it seems to me the very essence of an introduction to give some quick account of what the book it introduces contains.

My own sex, I hope, will forgive me if I treat them as rational creatures instead of flattering their fascinating graces and viewing them as though they were in a state of perpetual childhood, unable to stand alone. I want in earnest to point out where true dignity and human happiness lie. I want to persuade women to try to acquire strength, of mind and of body, and to convince them that the soft phrases — susceptibility of heart, delicacy of sentiment, refinement of taste — are very nearly synonyms for weakness, and that beings who are only objects of pity, and of the kind of love that has been called pity's sister, will soon become objects of contempt.

Dismissing, then, those pretty feminine phrases that men use so condescendingly to soften our slavish dependence, and despising that weak elegance of mind, that exquisite sensibility and sweet docility of manners supposed to be the marks of the weaker vessel, I want to show that elegance is inferior to virtue; that the first object of laudable ambition is to obtain a character as a human being, whatever one's sex; and that every secondary aim should be brought to that simple test.

This is a rough sketch of my plan. And if I express my conviction with the energy I feel whenever I think about the subject, then what experience and reflection have taught me will be felt by some of my readers. With that important object in view, I shall not stoop to pick over my phrases or polish my style. I aim at being useful, and sincerity will keep me unaffected. Wishing to persuade by the force of my arguments rather than dazzle by the elegance of my language, I shall not waste my time rounding off periods, or manufacturing the swollen bombast of artificial feelings, which comes from the head and never reaches the heart. I shall be employed about things, not words! And, anxious to make my sex more respectable members of society, I shall try to avoid that flowery diction which has slid out of essays into novels, and out of novels into ordinary letters and conversation.

These pretty nothings, these caricatures of the real beauty of feeling, drop so glibly off the tongue that they spoil the taste and create a kind of sickly delicacy which turns away from plain unadorned truth. A flood of false sentiment and overstretched feeling stifles the natural emotions of the heart and makes insipid those domestic pleasures which ought to sweeten the exercise of the severe duties that educate a rational and immortal being for a nobler field of action.

The education of women has been more attended to lately than it used to be; yet they are still reckoned a frivolous sex, and are ridiculed or pitied by the very writers who try, by satire or by instruction, to improve them. Everyone admits that they spend the first years of their lives acquiring a smattering of accomplishments — a little music, a little drawing, a little French, a little needlework. Meanwhile strength of body and mind is sacrificed to libertine notions of beauty and to the desire of establishing themselves in the only way a woman can rise in the world, which is by marriage. And this desire makes mere animals of them, so that when they do marry they behave as such children may be expected to behave: they dress; they paint, and give God's creatures new names. Surely such weak beings are fit for nothing but the harem! Can they govern a household, or take care of the poor babies they bring into the world?

If, then, it can be fairly concluded from the present conduct of my sex — from the prevailing fondness for pleasure, which takes the place of ambition and of those nobler passions that open and enlarge the soul — that the instruction women have received has only worked, along with the constitution of civil society, to render them insignificant objects of desire, mere breeders of fools; if it can be proved that in aiming to give them accomplishments without cultivating their understandings we take them out of their proper sphere of duties and make them ridiculous and useless once the short-lived bloom of beauty is over, then I presume that rational men will excuse me for trying to persuade women to become more masculine and respectable.

Footnote: A lively writer, whose name I cannot recall, asks what business women over forty have in the world at all.

Indeed the word masculine is only a bogey. There is little reason to fear that women will acquire too much courage or fortitude. Their apparent inferiority in bodily strength must make them to some degree dependent on men in the various relations of life; but why should that dependence be increased by prejudices which give a sex to virtue and confuse simple truths with sensual daydreams?

Women have in fact been so degraded by mistaken notions of female excellence that I am not adding a paradox when I say that this artificial weakness produces a tendency to tyrannize, and gives birth to cunning, the natural opponent of strength, which leads them to play off those contemptible childish airs that undermine esteem even while they excite desire. Stop fostering these prejudices and women will fall naturally into their subordinate, but still respectable, place in life.

It hardly needs saying that I am speaking here of the sex in general. Many individual women have more sense than their male relatives; and since nothing outweighs anything else in a constant struggle for balance unless it naturally has more gravity, some women govern their husbands without degrading themselves, because intellect will always govern.

Chapter One: The Rights of Human Beings, and the Duties That Come with Them

In the present state of society it seems necessary to go back to first principles in search of the simplest truths, and to fight some prevailing prejudice for every inch of ground. To clear my way I must be allowed to ask a few plain questions. The answers will probably look as unarguable as the axioms that reasoning is built on — though once they get tangled up with the various motives people act from, they are flatly contradicted, either by men's words or by their conduct.

What does man's pre-eminence over the animals consist in? The answer is as clear as that a half is less than a whole: in reason.

What quality raises one being above another? Virtue, we answer without hesitating.

What were the passions planted in us for? So that man, by struggling with them, might reach a degree of knowledge denied to the animals — that is what experience whispers.

It follows that the perfection of our nature, and our capacity for happiness, must be measured by the degree of reason, virtue and knowledge that marks the individual and shapes the laws binding society together. And that knowledge and virtue flow naturally from the exercise of reason is equally undeniable, if you look at humankind as a whole.

Once the rights and duties of man are simplified in this way, it seems almost impertinent to try to illustrate truths that appear so incontestable. Yet prejudices are so deeply rooted, and have so clouded reason, and so many counterfeit qualities have taken the name of virtues, that it is necessary to follow the course of reason through all the confusion and error that accidental circumstances have led it into, comparing the simple axiom against the casual departures from it.

Men in general seem to use their reason to justify prejudices they have absorbed without knowing how, rather than to root them out. A mind must be strong to form its own principles resolutely, for there is a kind of intellectual cowardice about that makes many men shrink from the job, or do it only by halves. And the imperfect conclusions they draw are often very plausible, because they are built on partial experience, on views that are sound as far as they go but narrow.

Go back to first principles, and vice slinks away from close examination with all its native ugliness showing. But a set of shallow reasoners are forever exclaiming that these arguments prove too much, and that a measure rotten at its core may still be expedient. So expediency is set against simple principle again and again, until truth is lost in a fog of words, virtue in forms, and knowledge becomes a sounding nothing, drowned out by the plausible prejudices that borrow its name.

That the society is most wisely built whose constitution is founded on human nature strikes every thinking being so forcibly, in the abstract, that it looks like presumption to offer proof. Yet proof must be offered, or reason will never storm the stronghold of long custom. And to urge long custom as an argument for depriving men, or women, of their natural rights is one of those absurd sophistries that insult common sense daily.

The civilization of most of the people of Europe is very partial. Indeed one might ask whether they have gained any virtues in exchange for innocence worth the misery produced by the vices that have been plastered over their unsightly ignorance, and by the freedom they have bartered away for a splendid slavery. The desire to dazzle by wealth — the surest kind of pre-eminence a man can get — the pleasure of commanding flattering hangers-on, and a hundred other tangled, low calculations of doting self-love have all helped to overwhelm the mass of humanity and make liberty a convenient handle for mock patriotism. For as long as rank and titles are held to be of the utmost importance, before which genius "must hide its diminished head," it is, with a few exceptions, very unlucky for a nation when a man of ability without rank or property pushes himself into notice. What unheard-of misery have thousands suffered to buy a cardinal's hat for some scheming nobody who longed to be ranked with princes, or to lord it over them by seizing the papal crown!

So much wretchedness has flowed from hereditary honours, riches and monarchy that men of strong feeling have come close to blasphemy in the effort to justify the ways of providence. Man has been presented as independent of the power that made him, or as a lawless planet shooting out of its orbit to steal the heavenly fire of reason — and the vengeance of heaven, lurking in that subtle flame, punished his rashness quite sufficiently by bringing evil into the world.

Struck by this view of the misery and disorder running through society, and worn out with jostling against artificial fools, Rousseau fell in love with solitude; and being at the same time an optimist, he labours with uncommon eloquence to prove that man is naturally a solitary animal. Misled by his respect for the goodness of God — who certainly, for what man of sense and feeling can doubt it, gave life only in order to share out happiness — he treats evil as something positive, and as the work of man. He did not notice that he was exalting one divine attribute at the expense of another just as necessary to divine perfection.

Built on a false hypothesis, his arguments for a state of nature are plausible but unsound. Unsound, I say: because to assert that a state of nature is preferable to civilization in its highest possible perfection is, in other words, to put supreme wisdom on trial. And the paradox that God has made all things right and that evil was introduced by the creature he formed — knowing what he was forming — is as unphilosophical as it is impious.

When that wise Being who created us and placed us here saw the fair idea, he willed, by allowing it to be so, that the passions should unfold our reason, because he could see that present evil would produce future good. Could the helpless creature he called out of nothing break loose from his providence and boldly learn to know good by practising evil without his permission? No. How could that energetic champion of immortality argue so inconsistently? If humankind had stayed forever in the brutal state of nature — which even his magic pen cannot paint as a state where a single virtue took root — it would have been plain, though not to the sensitive unreflecting wanderer, that man was born to run the circle of life and death and to decorate God's garden for some purpose not easily squared with God's attributes.

But if, to crown the whole, rational creatures were to be produced, allowed to rise in excellence by exercising the powers planted in them for that very purpose; if kindness itself thought fit to call into existence a creature above the animals, able to think and to improve himself — then why should that priceless gift, and a gift it was if man was created with the capacity to rise above a state in which sensation produced nothing but animal ease, be called in so many words a curse? A curse it might be reckoned if all our existence were bounded by our stay in this world. For why should the gracious fountain of life give us passions and the power of reflection only to embitter our days and fill us with mistaken notions of dignity? Why should he lead us up from love of ourselves to those sublime emotions that the discovery of his wisdom and goodness excites, if those feelings were not set going to improve our nature, of which they form a part, and to make us capable of enjoying a more godlike share of happiness? Firmly persuaded that no evil exists in the world which God did not intend to take place, I build my belief on the perfection of God.

Rousseau exerts himself to prove that everything was right at the beginning; a crowd of authors, that everything is right now; and I, that everything will be right.

But, faithful to his first position, Rousseau ranks barbarism next after the state of nature; and calling on the ghost of Fabricius, he forgets that in conquering the world the Romans never dreamed of founding their own liberty on any solid basis, or of extending the reign of virtue. Eager to prop up his system, he brands every effort of genius as vicious, and, pronouncing savage virtues divine, raises to demigods men who were barely human — the brutal Spartans, who in defiance of justice and gratitude killed in cold blood the slaves who had proved themselves men by rescuing their oppressors.

Disgusted with artificial manners and artificial virtues, the citizen of Geneva threw out the wheat with the chaff instead of properly sifting the subject, never stopping to ask whether the evils his ardent soul turned from in indignation were the consequences of civilization or the leftovers of barbarism. He saw vice trampling on virtue, and the appearance of goodness taking the place of the real thing; he saw talents bent by power to sinister ends, and it never occurred to him to trace the enormous mischief back to arbitrary power, back to the hereditary distinctions that clash with the mental superiority which naturally raises one man above his fellows. He did not see that royal power in a few generations breeds idiocy into the noble stock, and holds out temptations that make thousands idle and vicious.

Nothing shows up the character of kingship more contemptibly than the various crimes that have raised men to that supreme dignity. Vile intrigues, unnatural crimes and every vice that degrades our nature have been the steps to that eminence; and yet millions of men have lain back and let the nerveless limbs of the descendants of such rapacious prowlers rest quietly on their bloodstained thrones.

What but a diseased vapour can hang over a society whose chief director has been educated in nothing but the invention of crimes, or the stupid routine of childish ceremonies? Will men never be wise? Will they never stop expecting corn from weeds, and figs from thistles?

Even when the most favourable circumstances come together, it is impossible for any man to acquire enough knowledge and strength of mind to discharge the duties of a king entrusted with uncontrolled power. How much worse, then, must those duties be violated when his very elevation is an insuperable bar to acquiring either wisdom or virtue; when every human feeling in him is stifled by flattery and every chance of reflection shut out by pleasure! Surely it is madness to make the fate of thousands depend on the whim of a weak fellow creature whose very station sinks him necessarily below the meanest of his subjects. But one power should not be pulled down in order to raise another, for all power intoxicates weak men; and the abuse of it proves that the more equality is established among men, the more virtue and happiness will reign in society. Yet this, and any similar maxim drawn from plain reason, raises an outcry. The church or the state is in danger, we are told, if faith in the wisdom of antiquity is anything less than total; and those who, roused by the sight of human misery, dare to attack human authority are abused as despisers of God and enemies of man. These are bitter slanders, and yet they reached one of the best of men, whose ashes still preach peace, and whose memory deserves a respectful pause whenever subjects so near his heart are being discussed.

Footnote: Dr. Price.

Having attacked the sacred majesty of kings, I shall hardly cause surprise by adding my firm conviction that every profession whose power rests on a great subordination of rank does real damage to morality.

A standing army, for instance, is incompatible with freedom, because subordination and severity are the very sinews of military discipline, and despotism is needed to give force to enterprises directed by a single will. A spirit fed on romantic notions of honour, a kind of morality founded on the fashion of the age, can only be felt by a few officers, while the main body has to be moved by command, like waves of the sea; for the strong wind of authority drives the crowd of junior officers forward with headlong fury, hardly knowing or caring why.

Besides, nothing can be so damaging to the morals of a country town as the occasional residence of a set of idle, superficial young men whose only occupation is flirtation, and whose polished manners make vice more dangerous by hiding its deformity under gay ornamental drapery. An air of fashion, which is really a badge of slavery and proves that the soul has no strong character of its own, overawes simple country people into imitating the vices when they cannot catch the slippery graces of politeness. Every regiment is a chain of little despots who, submitting and tyrannizing without ever exercising their reason, become dead weights of vice and folly on the community. A man of rank or fortune, certain of rising through connections, has nothing to do but pursue some extravagant whim, while the needy gentleman, who is to rise, as the phrase goes, by his merit, turns into a servile hanger-on or a contemptible pimp.

Sailors — the naval gentlemen — come under the same description, except that their vices take a different and coarser cast. They are more positively idle when they are not performing the ceremonies of their station, whereas the insignificant fluttering of soldiers might be called active idleness. Being more confined to the company of men, sailors develop a taste for humour and mischievous practical jokes, while soldiers, mixing often with well-bred women, pick up a sentimental cant. But the mind is equally out of the question, whether they indulge in the horse laugh or the polite simper.

May I extend the comparison to a profession where there is certainly more mind to be found? For the clergy have better opportunities of improvement, though subordination cramps their faculties almost as much. The blind submission to forms of belief that is imposed at college serves as an apprenticeship for the curate, who will most obsequiously respect the opinion of his rector or his patron if he means to get on in his profession. Perhaps there is no more forcible contrast anywhere than between the servile, dependent walk of a poor curate and the courtly bearing of a bishop — and the respect the one inspires and the contempt the other does make both of them equally useless at their separate jobs.

It is very important to notice that every man's character is shaped to some degree by his profession. A man of sense may have no more than a cast of expression, which wears off as you come to know him as an individual; whereas the weak, ordinary man has hardly any character except what belongs to his body — or at least all his opinions have been steeped so long in the vat consecrated by authority that you cannot taste the faint spirit yielded by the grapes of his own vine.

Society, then, as it becomes more enlightened, ought to be very careful not to establish bodies of men who must inevitably be made foolish or vicious by the very constitution of their profession.

In the infancy of society, when men were only just emerging from barbarism, chiefs and priests, touching the most powerful springs of savage conduct — hope and fear — must have had unlimited sway. An aristocracy is naturally the first form of government. But clashing interests soon lose their balance, and a monarchy and a priesthood break out of the confusion of ambitious struggles, both of them then secured on a foundation of feudal tenures. This appears to be the origin of royal and priestly power, and the dawn of civilization. But such combustible materials cannot be shut up for long; and finding a vent in foreign wars and internal rebellions, the people acquire some power in the tumult, which forces their rulers to gloss over their oppression with a show of right. So as war, agriculture, trade and literature expand the mind, despots are driven to use covert corruption to keep a firm grip on the power that used to be snatched by open force. And this lurking gangrene is spread quickest by luxury and superstition, the certain dregs of ambition. The idle puppet of a court first becomes a luxurious monster or a fastidious sensualist, and then turns the contagion his unnatural condition spreads into an instrument of tyranny.

Footnote: Men of ability scatter seeds that grow up and have a great influence on opinion as it forms; and once public opinion has come down on the side of reason, the overthrow of arbitrary power is not far off.

It is the plague-carrying purple of royalty that turns the progress of civilization into a curse and warps the understanding, until men of feeling begin to doubt whether the expansion of intellect produces more happiness or more misery. But the nature of the poison points to the antidote; and if Rousseau had climbed one step higher in his enquiry, or if his eye could have pierced the foggy atmosphere he almost disdained to breathe, his active mind would have darted forward to contemplate the perfection of man in the establishment of a true civilization, instead of taking its ferocious flight back into the night of sensual ignorance.

Chapter Two: The Common Opinion That Each Sex Has a Character of Its Own

To account for the tyranny of men, and to excuse it, a great many ingenious arguments have been produced to prove that in acquiring virtue the two sexes ought to be aiming at quite different characters — or, to say it plainly, that women are not allowed enough strength of mind to acquire anything that really deserves the name of virtue. Yet if we grant that women have souls, it would seem that providence has appointed only one road by which mankind can be led to either virtue or happiness.

If women are not a swarm of mayflies born to trifle, then why should they be kept ignorant under the plausible name of innocence? Men complain, and with reason, of the follies and caprices of our sex, when they are not busy sharply satirizing our headstrong passions and grovelling vices. Look, I would answer, at the natural effect of ignorance! A mind will always be unstable that has nothing but prejudices to rest on, and the current will run with destructive fury when there is nothing to break its force. Women are told from infancy, and taught by their mothers' example, that a little knowledge of human weakness — justly called cunning — a soft temper, outward obedience and a scrupulous attention to a childish kind of propriety will win them the protection of a man; and that if they happen to be beautiful, nothing else is needed for at least twenty years of their lives.

This is how Milton describes our first frail mother. Though when he tells us that women are formed for softness and sweet attractive grace, I cannot make out what he means, unless, in the true Muslim strain, he meant to deprive us of souls and to hint that we are beings designed only by sweet attractive grace and docile blind obedience to gratify a man's senses when he can no longer soar on the wing of contemplation.

How grossly they insult us, those who advise us to make ourselves nothing but gentle domestic brutes! Take that winning softness so warmly and so often recommended, which governs by obeying. What childish language, and how insignificant a creature — can it possibly be an immortal one? — who will stoop to govern by such underhand methods. "Certainly," says Lord Bacon, "man is of kin to the beasts by his body: and if he be not of kin to God by his spirit, he is a base and ignoble creature!" Men seem to me to behave in a very unphilosophical way when they try to secure women's good conduct by keeping them permanently in a state of childhood. Rousseau was more consistent when he wished to stop the progress of reason in both sexes; for if men eat of the tree of knowledge, women will get a taste of it too — though from the imperfect cultivation their understandings receive at present, all they arrive at is a knowledge of evil.

Children, I grant, should be innocent; but when the word is applied to a grown man or woman it is only a polite term for weakness. For if we allow that women were meant by providence to acquire human virtues, and by exercising their understandings that stability of character which is the firmest ground for our future hopes, then they must be allowed to turn towards the fountain of light, and not forced to steer by the twinkling of a mere satellite. Milton, I grant, was of a very different opinion, for he bows to the unchallengeable right of beauty — though it would be hard to make the two passages I am about to set side by side consistent with each other. But great men are often led into inconsistencies of just this kind by their senses.

	"To whom thus Eve with perfect beauty adorned:
	My author and disposer, what thou bidst
	Unargued I obey; so God ordains;
	God is thy law, thou mine; to know no more
	Is woman's happiest knowledge and her praise."

These are exactly the arguments I have used with children — except that I added: "Your reason is gaining strength now, and until it comes to some maturity you must look to me for advice. After that you ought to think, and rely on God alone."

Yet in the following lines Milton seems to agree with me, when he has Adam protest to his Maker:

	"Hast thou not made me here thy substitute,
	And these inferior far beneath me set?
	Among unequals what society
	Can sort, what harmony or delight?
	Which must be mutual, in proportion due
	Given and received; but in disparity
	The one intense, the other still remiss
	Cannot well suit with either, but soon prove
	Tedious alike: of fellowship I speak
	Such as I seek fit to participate
	All rational delight."

In discussing the manners of women, then, let us set the sensual arguments aside and trace out what we ought to be trying to make of them, so that they may cooperate — if that is not too bold a way of putting it — with the Supreme Being.

By individual education I mean — since the sense of the word is not precisely fixed — such attention to a child as will slowly sharpen the senses, form the temper, regulate the passions as they begin to ferment, and set the understanding to work before the body reaches maturity; so that the adult has only to carry on the important business of learning to think and reason, not to begin it.

To prevent any misunderstanding I must add that I do not believe a private education can work the wonders some hopeful writers have attributed to it. Men and women are educated in great part by the opinions and manners of the society they live in. In every age there has been a stream of popular opinion that carries everything before it and gives a kind of family character to the century. It may fairly be concluded, then, that until society is differently constituted, not much can be expected from education. For my present purpose, however, it is enough to assert that whatever effect circumstances may have on our abilities, every being can become virtuous by exercising its own reason. For if a single being were created with vicious inclinations — that is, positively bad — what would save us from atheism? Or, if we worship a God, is that God not a devil?

The most perfect education, in my opinion, is therefore the exercise of the understanding best calculated to strengthen the body and form the heart; or, in other words, to enable a person to acquire such habits of virtue as will make them independent. In fact it is a farce to call any being virtuous whose virtues are not the result of exercising its own reason. This was Rousseau's opinion about men. I extend it to women, and confidently assert that they have been drawn out of their proper sphere by false refinement, and not by any attempt to acquire masculine qualities. Still, the royal homage they receive is so intoxicating that until the manners of the age are changed and rebuilt on more reasonable principles it may be impossible to convince them that the illegitimate power they get by degrading themselves is a curse, and that they must return to nature and equality if they want the calm satisfaction that unspoiled affections bring. But for that day we must wait — wait, perhaps, until kings and nobles, enlightened by reason and preferring the real dignity of a human being to childish pomp, throw off their gaudy hereditary trappings. And if women do not then give up the arbitrary power of beauty, they will have proved that they have less mind than men.

I may be accused of arrogance; still I must declare what I firmly believe, which is that every writer on the education and manners of women, from Rousseau to Dr. Gregory, has helped to make women more artificial and weaker characters than they would otherwise have been, and consequently more useless members of society. I might have put this conviction in a lower key, but I am afraid that would have been the whine of affectation rather than the faithful expression of what I feel — of the clear conclusion that experience and reflection have brought me to. When I reach that division of the subject I shall point out the particular passages I object to in the works of the authors I have just named; but first I must say that my objection extends to the whole drift of those books, which tend, in my view, to degrade one half of the human species and to make women pleasing at the cost of every solid virtue.

To reason on Rousseau's ground for a moment: if a man really did reach some perfection of mind at the same time his body reached maturity, then it might be quite proper, in order to make a husband and wife one, that she should rely entirely on his understanding; and the graceful ivy clasping the oak that supports it would make a whole in which strength and beauty were equally conspicuous. But husbands, alas, as well as their helpmates, are often only overgrown children — indeed, thanks to early debauchery, scarcely men even in their outward form. And if the blind lead the blind, nobody needs to come from heaven to tell us what follows.

Many causes, in the present corrupt state of society, help to enslave women by cramping their understandings and sharpening their senses. One that quietly does more mischief than all the rest, perhaps, is their disregard of order.

To do everything in an orderly way is a most important rule, and women, who generally receive only a disorderly sort of education, rarely observe it with the exactness that men do, having been broken into method from infancy. This careless guesswork — and what else can you call the random exertions of a sort of instinctive common sense that is never brought to the test of reason? — prevents them from generalizing from facts, so that they do today what they did yesterday, merely because they did it yesterday.

This contempt for the understanding in early life has worse consequences than is commonly supposed. The little knowledge that women of strong mind do acquire is, for various reasons, of a more scattered kind than men's, and is got more by sheer observation of real life than by comparing what they have individually observed with the results of experience generalized by reflection. Their dependent situation and their domestic employments lead them more into company, so that what they learn comes in snatches; and since learning is generally only a secondary thing with them, they never pursue any one branch with the persevering ardour that gives vigour to the faculties and clearness to the judgment. In the present state of society a little learning is required to keep up the character of a gentleman, and boys are obliged to submit to a few years of discipline. But in a woman's education the cultivation of the understanding is always subordinate to acquiring some bodily accomplishment — and even that fails, since a body weakened by confinement and by false notions of modesty never attains the grace and beauty that slack, half-formed limbs cannot show. Besides, in youth their faculties are not drawn out by competition; and having no serious scientific study, if they have natural shrewdness it is turned far too early onto life and manners. They dwell on effects and variations without tracing them back to causes; and complicated rules for regulating behaviour are a poor substitute for simple principles.

As proof that it is education that gives women this appearance of weakness, take the example of military men, who like women are sent out into the world before their minds have been stocked with knowledge or fortified with principles. The consequences are similar. Soldiers pick up a little superficial knowledge snatched from the muddy current of conversation, and from mixing constantly in society they acquire what is called a knowledge of the world; and this acquaintance with manners and customs has often been confused with a knowledge of the human heart. But can the crude fruit of casual observation, never brought to the test of a judgment formed by comparing reflection with experience, deserve such a name? Soldiers, like women, practise the minor virtues with punctilious politeness. Where is the difference of sex, then, when the education has been the same? All the difference I can see comes from the superior advantage of liberty, which lets the soldier see more of life.

It is wandering from my subject, perhaps, to make a political remark; but since the train of my reflections produced it naturally, I shall not pass it over in silence.

Standing armies can never be made up of resolute, robust men. They may be well-disciplined machines, but they will seldom contain men under the influence of strong passions or possessed of very vigorous faculties. And as for depth of understanding, I will venture to say that it is as rarely found in the army as it is among women — and the cause, I maintain, is the same. It may be added that officers are also particularly attentive to their persons, fond of dancing, crowded rooms, adventures and ridicule. Like the fair sex, the business of their lives is gallantry. They were taught to please, and they live only to please. Yet they do not lose their rank in the distinction of the sexes, for they are still reckoned superior to women — though what their superiority consists in, beyond what I have just mentioned, is difficult to discover.

The great misfortune is this: that both acquire manners before morals, and a knowledge of life before reflection has given them any acquaintance with the grand ideal outline of human nature. The consequence follows naturally. Satisfied with common nature, they fall prey to prejudices, and, taking all their opinions on credit, submit blindly to authority. So that if they have any sense, it is a kind of instinctive glance that catches proportions and decides questions of manners, but fails as soon as an argument has to be followed below the surface or an opinion taken apart.

May the same remark not be applied to women? Indeed the argument can be carried further, for both are thrown out of any useful station by the unnatural distinctions established in civilized life. Riches and hereditary honours have made ciphers of women in order to give consequence to the figure they stand beside; and idleness has produced a mixture of gallantry and despotism in society which leads the very men who are the slaves of their mistresses to tyrannize over their sisters, wives and daughters. It is only keeping them in rank and file, of course. Strengthen the female mind by enlarging it, and there will be an end to blind obedience; but since blind obedience is exactly what power always wants, tyrants and sensualists are quite right to try to keep women in the dark, the first because they only want slaves and the second because they only want a plaything. The sensualist has been the most dangerous tyrant of the two, and women have been duped by their lovers as princes are by their ministers, all the while dreaming that they reigned over them.

I am chiefly referring here to Rousseau, whose character of Sophia is undoubtedly a captivating one, though it seems to me grossly unnatural. It is not the superstructure I mean to attack, however, but the foundation of her character, the principles her education was built on. Warmly as I admire the genius of that able writer, whose opinions I shall often have occasion to quote, indignation always takes the place of admiration in me, and the rigid frown of insulted virtue wipes away the smile of pleasure his eloquent sentences usually raise, when I come to his voluptuous daydreams. Is this the man who in his ardour for virtue would banish all the soft arts of peace and carry us nearly back to Spartan discipline? Is this the man who loves to paint the useful struggles of passion, the triumphs of a good disposition, and the heroic flights that carry a glowing soul out of itself? How those mighty sentiments come down in the world when he is describing the pretty foot and enticing airs of his little favourite! But I shall leave the subject for now; and instead of severely rebuking the passing outpourings of an overweening sensibility, I shall only observe that anyone who has ever looked kindly at society must often have been pleased by the sight of a humble mutual love, not dignified by any sentiment nor strengthened by any shared intellectual pursuit. The domestic trifles of the day have supplied matter for cheerful conversation, and innocent caresses have eased labours that never called for much exercise of mind or stretch of thought. And yet has the sight of that moderate happiness not stirred more tenderness than respect? Something like what we feel watching children at play, or animals at their games — whereas the contemplation of the noble struggles of suffering merit raises admiration, and carries our thoughts to that world where sensation will give place to reason.

Women, then, are to be considered either as moral beings, or as creatures so weak that they must be entirely subject to the superior faculties of men.

Let us examine the question. Rousseau declares that a woman should never for a moment feel herself independent; that she should be governed by fear so as to exercise her natural cunning, and made into a coquettish slave in order to be a more alluring object of desire, a sweeter companion whenever a man chooses to relax. He carries the arguments he pretends to draw from the indications of nature further still, and hints that truth and fortitude, the cornerstones of all human virtue, are to be cultivated in a woman only within certain limits, because for the female character obedience is the great lesson, to be driven home with unrelenting rigour.

What nonsense! When will a great man arise with strength of mind enough to blow away the fumes that pride and sensuality have spread over this subject? If women are by nature inferior to men, their virtues must still be the same in kind, if not in degree — otherwise virtue is a merely relative idea. And consequently their conduct should rest on the same principles and have the same aim.

Connected with men as daughters, wives and mothers, their moral character may be measured by how they fulfil those simple duties; but the end, the great end of their efforts, should be to unfold their own faculties and acquire the dignity of a virtue they are conscious of. They may try to make their road a pleasant one, but they should never forget, any more than men should, that life does not yield the happiness that can satisfy an immortal soul. I do not mean to suggest that either sex should get so lost in abstract reflections or distant prospects as to forget the affections and duties lying in front of them, which are in truth the appointed means of producing the fruit of life. On the contrary, I would warmly recommend those affections and duties, even while I insist that they give most satisfaction when they are seen in their true, subordinate light.

The prevailing opinion that woman was created for man probably had its origin in Moses's poetical story. But since very few people who have given the subject any serious thought have ever supposed that Eve was literally one of Adam's ribs, the inference must be allowed to fall to the ground — or be admitted only so far as it proves that man, from the remotest antiquity, found it convenient to use his strength to subjugate his companion and his ingenuity to show that she ought to have her neck bent under the yoke, because she, like the animals, was created to do his pleasure.

Let no one conclude that I want to turn the order of things upside down. I have already granted that from the constitution of their bodies men appear to be designed by providence to attain a greater degree of virtue. I am speaking of the sexes collectively. But I see not the shadow of a reason for concluding that their virtues should differ in nature. How could they, in fact, if virtue has only one eternal standard? So if I reason consistently I must maintain, as strenuously as I maintain that there is a God, that both sexes have the same simple direction.

It follows, then, that cunning should not be set against wisdom, nor little cares against great exertions, nor an insipid softness varnished over with the name of gentleness against the fortitude that only grand views can inspire.

I shall be told that women would then lose many of their peculiar graces, and a well-known poet may be quoted against my unqualified assertions. For Pope has said, in the name of the whole male sex,

	"Yet ne'er so sure our passions to create,
	As when she touch'd the brink of all we hate."

What light that sally throws on men and women I shall leave the judicious to decide. For myself I shall be content to observe that I cannot see why, unless women are merely mortal creatures with no souls at all, they should always be degraded by being made to serve love or lust.

To speak disrespectfully of love is, I know, high treason against sentiment and fine feeling; but I want to speak the plain language of truth, and to address the head rather than the heart. To try to reason love out of the world would be to out-Quixote Cervantes and would offend just as much against common sense. But to try to restrain this tumultuous passion, and to show that it should not be allowed to dethrone our higher powers or usurp the sceptre that the understanding ought always to wield coolly, seems a less wild undertaking.

Youth is the season for love in both sexes; but in those days of thoughtless enjoyment some provision ought to be made for the more important years of life, when reflection takes the place of sensation. Rousseau, though, and most of the male writers who have followed in his steps, have warmly insisted that the whole tendency of a woman's education should be directed to one point: making her pleasing.

Let me reason with the supporters of this opinion who know anything of human nature. Do they imagine that marriage can uproot the habits of a lifetime? The woman who has been taught nothing but to please will soon find that her charms are slanting sunbeams, and that they cannot have much effect on her husband's heart when he sees them every day and the summer is over. Will she then have enough native energy to look into herself for comfort and cultivate her sleeping faculties? Or is it not more reasonable to expect that she will try to please other men, and, in the excitement of hoping for new conquests, try to forget the humiliation her love or her pride has suffered? When the husband stops being a lover — and the time will inevitably come — her desire to please will grow languid, or turn into a source of bitterness; and love, perhaps the most fleeting of all the passions, gives place to jealousy or vanity.

I am speaking now of women restrained by principle or by prejudice. Such women, though they would shrink from an affair with genuine horror, still want the homage of gallantry to convince them that their husbands are cruelly neglecting them; or else they spend days and weeks dreaming of the happiness enjoyed by kindred souls, until their health is undermined and their spirits broken by discontent. How can the great art of pleasing be such a necessary study, then? It is only useful to a mistress. The chaste wife and the serious mother should think of her power to please as no more than the polish on her virtues, and of her husband's affection as one of the comforts that make her task easier and her life happier. But whether she is loved or neglected, her first wish should be to make herself worthy of respect, and not to depend for all her happiness on a being subject to the same weaknesses as herself.

The amiable Dr. Gregory fell into a similar mistake. I respect his heart, but I entirely disapprove of his celebrated Legacy to His Daughters.

He advises his daughters to cultivate a fondness for dress, because a fondness for dress, he asserts, is natural to them. I cannot understand what either he or Rousseau means when they use that vague word so often. If they told me that in some pre-existent state the soul was fond of dress and brought the inclination with it into a new body, I should listen with a half smile, as I often do when I hear people ranting about innate elegance. But if he only meant that exercising the faculties will produce this fondness, I deny it. It is not natural. It arises, like false ambition in men, from a love of power.

Dr. Gregory goes much further. He actually recommends dissimulation, and advises an innocent girl to give her own feelings the lie and not dance with spirit, at a moment when a light heart would make her feet eloquent without making her movements immodest. In the name of truth and common sense, why should a woman not admit that she can take more exercise than another — or, in other words, that she has a sound constitution? Why, in order to damp an innocent liveliness, must she be darkly told that men will draw conclusions she never dreamed of? Let the libertine draw whatever inference he pleases; but I hope no sensible mother will cramp the natural frankness of youth by instilling such indecent cautions. Out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaks; and one wiser than Solomon said that it is the heart that should be made clean, and not the trivial ceremonies that are observed — which are not very hard to fulfil with scrupulous exactness while vice reigns inside.

Women ought to try to purify their hearts. But how can they, when their uncultivated understandings leave them entirely dependent on their senses for occupation and amusement, when no noble pursuit lifts them above the little vanities of the day, or lets them curb the wild emotions that shake a reed which every passing breeze has power over? To gain the affection of a virtuous man, is affectation really necessary?

Nature has given woman a weaker frame than man. But to secure her husband's affection, must a wife who has exercised her mind and body while discharging the duties of a daughter, a wife and a mother, and has kept her constitution's natural strength and her nerves in healthy tone — must she, I say, stoop to use artifice and feign a sickly delicacy? Weakness may excite tenderness and gratify the arrogant pride of a man, but the lordly caresses of a protector will not satisfy a noble mind that longs for respect and deserves it. Fondness is a poor substitute for friendship.

In a harem, I grant, all these arts are necessary; the epicure must have his palate tickled or he will sink into apathy. But have women so little ambition that such a condition satisfies them? Can they idly dream their lives away in the lap of pleasure, or in the dullness of boredom, rather than claim their right to pursue reasonable pleasures and make themselves conspicuous by practising the virtues that give humankind its dignity? Surely she has no immortal soul who can loiter her life away employed only in decorating her person, so as to amuse the languid hours and soften the cares of a fellow creature who is willing to be cheered up by her smiles and her tricks when the serious business of the day is over.

Besides, the woman who strengthens her body and exercises her mind will, by managing her family and practising various virtues, become her husband's friend rather than his humble dependent. And if she deserves his regard by possessing such substantial qualities, she will not find it necessary to hide her affection, or to feign an unnatural coldness of constitution to excite his passion. In fact, if we go back to history, we shall find that the women who have distinguished themselves have been neither the most beautiful nor the gentlest of their sex.

Nature — or, to speak with strict propriety, God — has made all things right; but man has sought out many inventions to spoil the work. I am referring now to that part of Dr. Gregory's treatise where he advises a wife never to let her husband know the full extent of her feeling or her affection. A voluptuary's precaution, and as ineffectual as it is absurd. Love, by its very nature, must be transitory. To look for a secret that would make it constant would be as wild a search as the hunt for the philosopher's stone or the universal cure, and the discovery would be as useless — or rather as harmful — to humankind. The most sacred bond of society is friendship. A shrewd satirist put it well when he said that rare as true love is, true friendship is rarer still.

This is an obvious truth, and since its cause does not lie deep, it will not escape a brief glance of enquiry.

Love — the common passion, in which chance and sensation take the place of choice and reason — is felt to some degree by most of humankind; there is no need to speak here of the emotions that rise above love or sink below it. This passion, naturally increased by suspense and difficulty, draws the mind out of its usual state and exalts the affections. But once the security of marriage lets the fever of love subside, a healthy temperature is thought insipid only by those who have not intellect enough to put the calm tenderness of friendship and the confidence of respect in the place of blind admiration and the sensual emotions of fondness.

This is, and must be, the course of nature: friendship or indifference inevitably follows love. And this arrangement seems to harmonize perfectly with the system of government that prevails in the moral world. The passions are spurs to action and they open the mind; but they sink into mere appetites, into a momentary personal gratification, once the object is gained and the satisfied mind settles down to enjoy it. The man who had some virtue while he was struggling for a crown often becomes a voluptuous tyrant once it sits on his head. And when the lover is not lost in the husband, the doting fool, prey to childish whims and fond jealousies, neglects the serious duties of life, and the caresses that ought to build confidence in his children are lavished on his overgrown child, his wife.

If they are to fulfil the duties of life, and to pursue with any vigour the various employments that form a moral character, the master and mistress of a family ought not to go on loving each other with passion. I mean that they ought not to indulge emotions that disturb the order of society and monopolize thoughts that should be otherwise employed. A mind that has never been absorbed by a single object lacks vigour; if it can stay absorbed for long, it is weak.

A mistaken education, a narrow and uncultivated mind, and a great many prejudices about the sexes all tend to make women more constant than men; but I shall not touch on that branch of the subject at present. I will go further, and assert, without dreaming that it is a paradox, that an unhappy marriage is often very advantageous to a family, and that the neglected wife is generally the best mother. And this would nearly always be the result if the female mind were more enlarged. For it seems to be the common dispensation of providence that whatever we gain in present enjoyment is deducted from that treasure of life which is experience, and that while we are gathering the flowers of the day and revelling in pleasure we cannot be picking the solid fruit of labour and wisdom at the same time. The road lies in front of us; we must turn right or left; and the man who spends his life bounding from one pleasure to another must not complain if he acquires neither wisdom nor a character anyone respects.

Suppose for a moment that the soul is not immortal, and that we were created only for this present scene. Then I think we should have reason to complain that love, that infant fondness, ever grows insipid and cloys the senses. Let us eat, drink and love, for tomorrow we die, would in fact be the language of reason and the morality of life; and who but a fool would give up a reality for a passing shadow? But if, awed by observing how far the mind can improve, we disdain to confine our wishes or our thoughts to so comparatively mean a field of action — one that only looks grand and important because it is connected with a boundless prospect and with sublime hopes — then what need is there for falsehood in our conduct? Why must the sacred majesty of truth be violated to hold on to a deceitful good that saps the very foundation of virtue? Why must a woman's mind be tainted with coquettish arts in order to gratify the sensualist, and to stop love from settling into friendship, or into a compassionate tenderness where there are no qualities for friendship to be built on? Let the honest heart show itself, and let reason teach passion to submit to necessity; or else let the dignified pursuit of virtue and knowledge raise the mind above emotions which, when they are not kept within bounds, embitter the cup of life rather than sweeten it.

I do not mean the romantic passion that goes along with genius. Who can clip its wings? But that grand passion, out of all proportion to the puny enjoyments of life, is true only to the sentiment, and feeds on itself. The passions celebrated for lasting have always been unlucky ones. They gained their strength from absence and from a constitutional melancholy. The fancy has hovered round a form of beauty only dimly seen — whereas familiarity might have turned admiration into disgust, or at least into indifference, and given the imagination leisure to start fresh game. Taking this view of things, Rousseau is perfectly right to have Eloisa, the mistress of his soul, love St. Preux when her life was fading; but that is no proof that the passion is immortal.

Of the same complexion is Dr. Gregory's advice about delicacy of sentiment, which he tells a woman not to acquire if she has decided to marry. That decision, however — perfectly consistent with his earlier advice — he calls indelicate, and he earnestly urges his daughters to conceal it even while it governs their conduct: as though it were indelicate to have the common appetites of human nature.

Noble morality! And quite consistent with the cautious prudence of a small soul that cannot see beyond the present minute division of existence. If all the faculties of a woman's mind are to be cultivated only so far as they bear on her dependence on a man; if, once she has got a husband, she has reached her goal and, meanly proud, is satisfied with such a paltry crown, then let her grovel contentedly, raised by her occupations hardly at all above the animals. But if she is struggling for the prize of her high calling, let her cultivate her understanding without stopping to consider what sort of character the husband she is destined for may have. Let her simply determine, without being too anxious about present happiness, to acquire the qualities that ennoble a rational being; and then a rough, inelegant husband may offend her taste without destroying her peace of mind. She will shape her soul not to fit her companion's weaknesses but to bear with them: his character may be a trial to her virtue, but it will not be an obstacle to it.

If Dr. Gregory had confined his remark to romantic expectations of unchanging love and kindred feeling, he should have remembered that experience will banish what advice can never stop us wishing for, so long as the imagination is kept alive at the expense of reason.

I admit it often happens that women who have nursed a romantic and unnatural delicacy of feeling waste their lives imagining how happy they would have been with a husband who could love them with a fervent, daily increasing affection, all day long. But they might as well pine married as single, and would be no more unhappy with a bad husband than they are longing for a good one. That a proper education — or, to be more precise, a well-stocked mind — would let a woman support a single life with dignity, I grant. But that she should avoid cultivating her taste in case her husband occasionally offends it, is giving up substance for shadow. To tell the truth, I do not know what use an improved taste is at all, if it does not make a person more independent of the accidents of life, if it does not open new sources of enjoyment that depend on the solitary operations of the mind. People of taste, married or single alike, will always be disgusted by a variety of things that less observant minds never notice. The argument must not be made to hinge on that. But taking enjoyment as a whole, is taste to be called a blessing?

The question is whether it brings more pain than pleasure. The answer will decide whether Dr. Gregory's advice is sound, and will show how absurd and tyrannical it is to lay down a system of slavery in this way, or to try to educate moral beings by any rules other than those drawn from pure reason, which apply to the whole species.

Gentleness of manner, forbearance and long-suffering are such lovable, godlike qualities that in sublime poetry the Deity himself has been clothed with them; and perhaps no picture of his goodness fastens on human affection so strongly as those that show him abundant in mercy and ready to pardon. Gentleness seen in that light bears every mark of grandeur on its face, combined with the winning graces of stooping from a height. But what a different aspect it takes on when it is the submissive bearing of dependence — the prop of a weakness that loves because it needs protection, and forbears because it must endure injuries in silence, smiling under a lash it dares not snarl at. Abject as that picture is, it is the portrait of an accomplished woman according to the received opinion of female excellence, which plausible reasoners have separated from human excellence altogether. Or else they kindly give back the rib and make one moral being out of a man and a woman — not forgetting to give her all the "submissive charms."

Footnote: See Rousseau, and Swedenborg.

How women are to exist in that state where there is to be neither marrying nor giving in marriage, we are not told. For although moralists agree that the whole tenor of life seems to prove that man is being prepared by various circumstances for a future state, they agree just as consistently in advising woman to provide for the present only. Gentleness, docility and a spaniel-like affection are recommended on this ground as the cardinal virtues of the sex; and, disregarding the arbitrary economy of nature, one writer has declared that it is masculine in a woman to be melancholy. She was created to be the toy of man, his rattle, and it must jingle in his ears whenever he dismisses reason and chooses to be amused.

To recommend gentleness on a broad basis is, of course, strictly philosophical. A frail creature should work at being gentle. But when forbearance confuses right with wrong it stops being a virtue; and however convenient it may be in a companion, that companion will always be considered an inferior, and will inspire nothing but a vapid tenderness that easily degenerates into contempt. Still, if advice really could make a person gentle whose natural disposition did not admit of such a fine polish, something would have been gained for the general order. But if — as could very quickly be shown — this indiscriminate counsel produces nothing but affectation, and throws a stumbling block in the way of gradual improvement and any real bettering of the temper, then women are not much benefited by sacrificing solid virtues to superficial graces, however many years of royal sway those graces may win the individual.

As a philosopher I read with indignation the plausible epithets men use to soften their insults; and as a moralist I ask what is meant by such mongrel phrases as fair defects and amiable weaknesses. If there is only one criterion of morals, only one archetype for humanity, then women appear to be suspended by destiny like Muhammad's coffin in the popular tale: they have neither the unerring instinct of animals, nor are they allowed to fix the eye of reason on a perfect model. They were made to be loved, and must not aim at being respected, in case they should be hunted out of society as masculine.

But let us look at the subject another way. Do passive, indolent women make the best wives? Confining the discussion to this present moment of existence, let us see how such weak creatures play their part. Do the women who have propped up the prevailing prejudice by acquiring a few superficial accomplishments really contribute to their husbands' happiness? Do they display their charms merely to amuse them? And have women who absorbed notions of passive obedience early in life enough character to manage a household or educate children? So far from it, that after surveying the history of women I cannot help agreeing with the harshest satirist, and considering my sex as the weakest as well as the most oppressed half of the species. What does history show but marks of inferiority — and how few women have freed themselves from the galling yoke of sovereign man? So few that the exceptions remind me of an ingenious guess about Newton: that he was probably a being of a superior order accidentally caged in a human body. In the same style I have found myself imagining that the few extraordinary women who have rushed off in eccentric directions out of the orbit prescribed to their sex were male spirits, shut up by mistake in a female frame.
