But if it is unphilosophical to think of sex at all when the soul is in question, then the inferiority must depend on the bodily organs — or else the heavenly fire that is to quicken the clay is not handed out in equal portions.

But avoiding, as I have so far, any direct comparison of the two sexes as wholes, and frankly admitting women's inferiority as things appear at present, I shall only insist that men have increased that inferiority until women have sunk almost below the standard of rational creatures. Give their faculties room to unfold and their virtues room to gain strength, and then decide where the whole sex must stand on the intellectual scale. Yet let it be remembered that I am not asking for a place on the strength of a small number of distinguished women.

It is difficult for us half-blind mortals to say what heights human discovery and improvement may reach once the gloom of despotism lifts that makes us stumble at every step. But when morality has been settled on a more solid basis, then, without any gift of prophecy, I will venture to predict that woman will be either the friend or the slave of man. We shall no longer be in doubt, as we are now, whether she is a moral agent or the link joining man to the animals. If it should then turn out that, like the animals, women were principally created for the use of men, then men will let them bite the bridle patiently and will not mock them with empty praise. And if their rationality is proved, men will not obstruct their improvement merely to gratify their own sensual appetites. They will not use all the graces of rhetoric to advise women to surrender their understandings entirely to a man's guidance. They will not, when writing about the education of women, assert that women ought never to have the free use of reason, nor recommend cunning and dissimulation to beings who are acquiring, exactly as they are themselves, the virtues of humanity.

Surely there can be only one rule of right, if morality has an eternal foundation; and whoever sacrifices virtue, strictly so called, to present convenience, or whose duty it is said to be to act that way, lives only for the passing day and cannot be an accountable creature.

The poet should have dropped his sneer, then, when he says,

	"If weak women go astray,
	The stars are more in fault than they."

For that women are bound by the adamantine chain of destiny is quite certain, if it is settled that they are never to exercise their own reason, never to be independent, never to rise above opinion, never to feel the dignity of a rational will that bows to God alone, and often forgets that the universe contains anything besides itself and the model of perfection its ardent gaze is fixed on — adoring attributes which, softened into virtues, may be imitated in kind, even though their degree overwhelms the enraptured mind.

If, I say — for I would not try to impress by declamation when reason offers her sober light — if women really are capable of acting like rational creatures, then let them not be treated like slaves, or like the animals that depend on human reason when they live alongside us. Cultivate their minds, give them the wholesome, sublime curb of principle, and let them attain a conscious dignity by feeling themselves dependent on God alone. Teach them, as men are taught, to submit to necessity, instead of giving morals a sex in order to make them more pleasing.

Further: if experience should prove that they cannot attain the same degree of strength of mind, perseverance and fortitude, then let their virtues be the same in kind even though they struggle in vain for the same degree. The superiority of men will be just as clear, or clearer; and truth, being a simple principle admitting of no modification, would be common to both. The order of society as it is now regulated would not even be overturned, for women would then have only the rank reason assigned them, and no arts could be practised to level the balance, much less to tip it.

These may be called Utopian dreams. Thanks be to the Being who impressed them on my soul and gave me strength of mind enough to dare to exercise my own reason, until, depending on him alone to support my virtue, I look with indignation at the mistaken notions that enslave my sex.

I love man as my fellow creature; but his sceptre, real or usurped, does not extend to me, unless the reason of a particular individual demands my homage — and even then the submission is to reason and not to the man. In fact the conduct of an accountable being must be regulated by the workings of its own reason; or on what foundation does the throne of God rest?

It seems necessary to dwell on these obvious truths, because women have been insulted, as it were, in this way: stripped of the virtues that ought to clothe humanity, they have been decked out instead with artificial graces that let them exercise a short-lived tyranny. Love takes the place in them of every nobler passion, so that their sole ambition is to be beautiful, to stir emotion instead of inspiring respect. And this ignoble desire, like the servility of absolute monarchies, destroys all strength of character. Liberty is the mother of virtue; and if women are by their very constitution slaves, and are not allowed to breathe the sharp invigorating air of freedom, they must always languish like hothouse plants and be reckoned beautiful flaws in nature — and let it be remembered too that they are nature's only flaw.

As for the argument drawn from the subjection women have always been held in, it recoils on men. The many have always been enslaved by the few, and monsters who have shown hardly any discernment of human excellence have tyrannized over thousands of their fellow creatures. Why have men of superior endowments submitted to such degradation? For is it not universally acknowledged that kings, taken collectively, have always been inferior in ability and virtue to the same number of men picked out of the common mass — and yet are they not still treated with a reverence that is an insult to reason? China is not the only country where a living man has been made into a god. Men have submitted to superior strength in order to enjoy the pleasure of the moment with impunity; women have only done the same. And so, until it is proved that the courtier who servilely surrenders his birthright as a man is not a moral agent, it cannot be shown that woman is essentially inferior to man because she has always been subjugated.

Brute force has governed the world so far, and that the science of politics is in its infancy is plain from the way philosophers hesitate to give the knowledge most useful to man that definite name.

I shall not pursue the argument beyond establishing one obvious inference: that as sound politics spread liberty, humankind, women included, will become wiser and more virtuous.

Chapter Three: The Same Subject Continued

Bodily strength, which used to be the mark of heroes, has now sunk into such undeserved contempt that men as well as women seem to think it unnecessary — women because it detracts from their feminine graces and from that lovely weakness which is the source of their undue power, and men because it looks incompatible with the character of a gentleman.

That both have run from one extreme into the other can easily be shown. But first it is worth noticing that a vulgar error has gained enough credit to give force to a false conclusion, in which an effect has been mistaken for a cause.

People of genius have very often damaged their constitutions by study, or by careless inattention to their health; and since the violence of their passions is proportioned to the vigour of their intellects, the sword wearing out the scabbard has become almost proverbial. From this superficial observers have inferred that men of genius usually have weak — or, to use the more fashionable word, delicate — constitutions. Yet I believe the opposite will turn out to be the fact. On careful enquiry I find that strength of mind has in most cases been accompanied by superior strength of body and a naturally sound constitution — not that robust tone of nerve and vigour of muscle which comes from bodily labour while the mind lies idle, or merely directs the hands.

Dr. Priestley has remarked, in the preface to his biographical chart, that most great men have lived beyond forty-five. And considering the thoughtless way they squandered their strength — investigating some favourite science, burning up the lamp of life and forgetting the midnight hour; or lost in poetic dreams, with fancy peopling the scene and the soul so disturbed by the passions that meditation had raised that it shook the constitution, while the objects of those passions, the baseless fabric of a vision, faded before the exhausted eye — considering all that, they must have had iron frames. Shakespeare never grasped the airy dagger with a nerveless hand, nor did Milton tremble when he led Satan far from the confines of his dreary prison. These were not the ravings of feebleness, the sickly outpourings of a disordered brain, but the exuberance of a fancy which, wandering "in a fine frenzy", was not continually reminded of its material shackles.

I am aware that this argument would carry me further than I may be supposed to want to go; but I follow truth, and, still holding to my first position, I will allow that bodily strength does seem to give men a natural superiority over women. That is the only solid basis on which the superiority of the sex can be built. But I still insist that not only the virtue but the knowledge of the two sexes should be the same in kind, if not in degree; and that women, considered not only as moral but as rational creatures, ought to try to acquire human virtues, or perfections, by the same means as men, instead of being educated as a fanciful sort of half being, one of Rousseau's wild chimeras.

But if strength of body is, with some show of reason, the boast of men, why are women so infatuated as to be proud of a defect? Rousseau has supplied them with a plausible excuse — one that could only have occurred to a man whose imagination had been allowed to run wild and to refine on the impressions made by exquisite senses. Its purpose, if you please, is to give men a pretext for yielding to a natural appetite without violating a romantic species of modesty which gratifies male pride and male libertinism.

Deluded by such sentiments, women sometimes boast of their weakness, cunningly obtaining power by playing on the weakness of men; and they may well glory in their illicit sway, for, like Turkish pashas, they have more real power than their masters. But virtue is sacrificed to momentary gratifications, and the respectability of a whole life to the triumph of an hour.

Women, like despots, have perhaps more power now than they would have if the world, divided and subdivided into kingdoms and families, were governed by laws drawn from the exercise of reason. But in getting that power — to carry the comparison on — their character is degraded, and licentiousness is spread through the whole of society. The many become a pedestal for the few. I will therefore venture to assert that until women are more rationally educated, the progress of human virtue and the improvement of knowledge must suffer continual checks. And if it is granted that woman was not created merely to gratify a man's appetite, nor to be the upper servant who provides his meals and looks after his linen, then it follows that the first care of any mother or father who really attends to a girl's education should be, if not to strengthen her body, at least not to destroy her constitution with mistaken notions of beauty and female excellence; and girls should never be allowed to absorb the pernicious idea that a defect can, by some chemical process of reasoning, be turned into an excellence. I am glad to find that the author of one of the most instructive books our country has produced for children agrees with me here, and I shall quote his apt remarks to lend the weight of his respectable authority to reason.

Footnote: A respectable old man gives the following sensible account of how he set about educating his daughter. "I tried to give both her mind and her body a degree of vigour that is seldom found in the female sex. As soon as she was strong enough for the lighter work of farming and gardening, I made her my constant companion. Selene — for that was her name — soon acquired a dexterity in all these country employments that I watched with equal pleasure and admiration. If women are generally feeble in body and mind alike, it comes less from nature than from education. We encourage a vicious idleness and inactivity which we falsely call delicacy; instead of hardening their minds with the sterner principles of reason and philosophy, we breed them up to useless arts that end in vanity and sensuality. In most of the countries I have visited they are taught nothing higher than a few modulations of the voice or a few useless postures of the body; their time is consumed in idleness or in trifles, and trifles become the only pursuits capable of interesting them. We seem to forget that our own domestic comfort, and the education of our children, must depend on the qualities of the female sex. And what comfort, or what education, is a race of beings corrupted from infancy and unacquainted with every duty of life fitted to give? To touch a musical instrument with useless skill, and to display their natural or affected graces to the eyes of idle, debauched young men who squander their fathers' estates in riotous and needless expense — these are the only arts women cultivate in most of the polished nations I have seen. And the consequences are uniformly what may be expected from such polluted sources: private misery, and public servitude. But Selene's education was regulated by different views and conducted on sterner principles — if that can be called sternness which opens the mind to a sense of moral and religious duty, and arms it most effectually against the inevitable evils of life." —Mr. Day's Sandford and Merton, Volume 3

But suppose it were proved that woman is naturally weaker than man. How does it follow that it is natural for her to labour at becoming weaker still than nature intended? Arguments of this cast are an insult to common sense and smell of passion. The divine right of husbands, like the divine right of kings, may, it is to be hoped, be disputed in this enlightened age without danger; and though conviction may not silence many boisterous disputants, still, when a prevailing prejudice is attacked, the wise will consider, and leave the narrow-minded to rail with thoughtless vehemence against innovation.

The mother who wishes to give her daughter a true dignity of character must, ignoring the sneers of the ignorant, proceed on a plan diametrically opposite to the one Rousseau has recommended with all the deluding charms of eloquence and philosophical sophistry. For his eloquence makes absurdities plausible, and his dogmatic conclusions puzzle without convincing anyone who has not the ability to refute them.

Throughout the whole animal kingdom every young creature needs almost continual exercise, and, in keeping with that hint, the infancy of children should be spent in harmless romping which exercises the feet and hands without needing much direction from the head or the constant attention of a nurse. In fact the care necessary for self-preservation is the first natural exercise of the understanding, just as little inventions to amuse the present moment unfold the imagination. But these wise designs of nature are frustrated by mistaken fondness or blind zeal. The child is never left a moment to its own direction — a girl least of all — and so is made dependent, and the dependence is then called natural.

To preserve personal beauty, woman's glory, the limbs and faculties are cramped with something worse than Chinese bandages; and the sedentary life girls are condemned to, while boys frolic in the open air, weakens the muscles and slackens the nerves. As for Rousseau's observations, since echoed by several writers, that girls have naturally — that is, from birth and independently of education — a fondness for dolls, for dressing up and for chattering, they are so childish that they do not deserve serious refutation. That a girl condemned to sit for hours together listening to the idle chat of feeble nurses, or attending her mother at her dressing table, should try to join in the conversation is indeed very natural. And that she should imitate her mother or her aunts, and amuse herself by adorning her lifeless doll as they adorn her, poor innocent baby, is undoubtedly the most natural consequence in the world. For men of the greatest abilities have seldom had strength enough to rise above the atmosphere around them; and if the page of genius has always been blotted by the prejudices of its age, some allowance should be made for a sex who, like kings, always see things through a false medium.

The fondness for dress so conspicuous in women can be easily accounted for in this way, without supposing it the result of any desire to please the sex they depend on. In short, the absurdity of supposing that a girl is naturally a coquette, and that a desire connected with nature's impulse to propagate the species should show itself even before an improper education has called it out prematurely by heating the imagination, is so unphilosophical that an observer as shrewd as Rousseau would never have adopted it, if he had not been in the habit of letting reason give way to his craving for singularity, and truth to a favourite paradox.

Yet giving a sex to the mind in this way was hardly consistent in a man who argued so warmly, and so well, for the immortality of the soul. But what a weak barrier truth is when it stands in the way of a hypothesis! Rousseau respected — almost adored — virtue, and yet let himself love with a sensual fondness. His imagination was constantly preparing inflammable fuel for his inflammable senses; and in order to reconcile that with his respect for self-denial, fortitude and the heroic virtues, which a mind like his could not admire coolly, he labours to invert the law of nature and puts forward a doctrine pregnant with mischief and insulting to the character of supreme wisdom.

His ridiculous stories, which are meant to prove that girls are naturally attentive to their persons, without laying any weight on daily example, are beneath contempt. And the tale of the little girl with such a correct taste that she gave up the pleasing amusement of making O's merely because she noticed it was an ungraceful attitude belongs with the anecdotes of the learned pig.

Footnote: "I once knew a young person who learned to write before she learned to read, and began to write with her needle before she could handle a pen. At first she took it into her head to make no letter but the O: she was constantly making it, in all sizes, and always the wrong way round. Unluckily, one day when she was intent on this employment, she happened to catch sight of herself in the looking glass; and, taking a dislike to the constrained attitude she sat in while writing, she threw away her pen like another Pallas and resolved never to make an O again. Her brother was equally averse to writing; but with him it was the confinement and not the constrained attitude that was most disgusting." —Rousseau's Emilius

I have probably had more opportunity of observing girls in their infancy than J. J. Rousseau. I can remember my own feelings, and I have looked steadily about me. So far from agreeing with him about the first dawn of the female character, I will venture to affirm that a girl whose spirits have not been damped by inactivity, and whose innocence has not been tainted by false shame, will always be a tomboy, and that a doll will never hold her attention unless confinement leaves her no alternative. In short, girls and boys would play harmlessly together if the distinction of sex were not drummed into them long before nature makes any difference. I will go further, and state it as an indisputable fact that most of the women within my own observation who have acted like rational creatures, or shown any vigour of intellect, have happened to be allowed to run wild — as some of the elegant improvers of the fair sex would put it.

The harmful consequences of neglecting health in infancy and youth reach further than is supposed: dependence of body naturally produces dependence of mind. How can a woman be a good wife or mother when most of her time is spent guarding against illness or enduring it? Nor can we expect a woman to work resolutely at strengthening her constitution and abstaining from enervating indulgences, if artificial notions of beauty and false descriptions of sensibility have been tangled up early with her motives for acting. Most men are obliged now and then to put up with bodily inconvenience and endure the weather; but genteel women are, quite literally, slaves to their bodies, and glory in the subjection.

I once knew a weak woman of fashion who was more than commonly proud of her delicacy and her sensibility. She thought a discriminating taste and a tiny appetite the height of human perfection, and acted accordingly. I have watched this feeble, artificial creature neglect every duty of life and then recline on a sofa in perfect self-satisfaction, boasting that she had no appetite as proof of a delicacy which extended to — or perhaps arose from — her exquisite sensibility; for it is hard to make such ridiculous jargon intelligible. And at that very moment I have seen her insult a worthy old gentlewoman whom unexpected misfortune had made dependent on her ostentatious charity, and who in better days had claims on her gratitude. Is it possible that a human creature could have become so weak and depraved if, like the Sybarites dissolved in luxury, everything resembling virtue had not been worn away, or had been impressed by mere precept — a poor substitute, it is true, for a cultivated mind, though it serves as a fence against vice?

Such a woman is no more an irrational monster than some of those Roman emperors who were depraved by lawless power. Yet since kings have been more under the restraint of law, and under the curb — however weak — of honour, the records of history are not filled with such unnatural instances of folly and cruelty; nor does the despotism that kills virtue and genius in the bud hang over Europe with the destructive blast that lays Turkey waste and leaves the men, like the soil, unfruitful.

Women are everywhere in this deplorable state. In order to preserve their innocence, as ignorance is politely called, truth is hidden from them, and they are made to assume an artificial character before their faculties have gained any strength. Taught from their infancy that beauty is woman's sceptre, the mind shapes itself to the body, and, roaming round its gilt cage, only seeks to adorn its prison. Men have a variety of employments and pursuits to engage their attention and give some character to the opening mind; but women, confined to one, and with their thoughts constantly directed to the most insignificant part of themselves, seldom look beyond the triumph of the hour. Yet if their understanding were once emancipated from the slavery that male pride and sensuality, and the short-sighted craving for immediate power that tyrants share, have subjected them to, we should probably read of their weaknesses with astonishment. I must be allowed to press the argument a little further.

Perhaps, if we allowed the existence of an evil being who, in the allegorical language of scripture, went about seeking whom he might devour, he could not degrade the human character more effectively than by giving a man absolute power.

This argument branches out in various directions. Birth, riches and every extrinsic advantage that raises a man above his fellows without any mental exertion really sinks him below them. In proportion to his weakness he is played upon by designing men, until the bloated monster has lost every trace of humanity. And that tribes of men, like flocks of sheep, should quietly follow such a leader is a contradiction that nothing but a desire for present enjoyment and a narrowness of understanding can explain. Brought up in slavish dependence, and enfeebled by luxury and sloth, where are we to find men who will stand forward to assert the rights of man, or claim the privilege of moral beings, who ought to have only one road to excellence? Slavery to monarchs and ministers, which the world will be long in freeing itself from, and whose deadly grip stops the progress of the human mind, is not yet abolished.

Let men, then, in the pride of power, not use the same arguments that tyrannical kings and venal ministers have used, and falsely assert that woman ought to be kept in subjection because she has always been so. When man, governed by reasonable laws, enjoys his natural freedom, let him despise woman if she does not share it with him; and until that glorious period arrives, when he holds forth on the folly of my sex let him not overlook his own.

Women who obtain power by unjust means, by practising vice or fostering it, do plainly lose the rank reason would assign them, and become either abject slaves or capricious tyrants. They lose all simplicity and all dignity of mind in acquiring power, and behave exactly as men behave when they have been raised by the same means.

It is time to effect a revolution in female manners, time to give women back their lost dignity, and to make them, as a part of the human species, work at reforming themselves in order to reform the world. It is time to separate unchangeable morals from local manners. If men are demigods, then let us serve them! And if the dignity of the female soul is as disputable as that of animals — if their reason gives too little light to direct their conduct while unerring instinct is denied them — then they are surely of all creatures the most miserable, and, bent beneath the iron hand of destiny, must submit to be a fair defect in creation. But to justify the ways of providence towards them by pointing out some unanswerable reason for making so large a portion of humankind accountable and not accountable at once would puzzle the subtlest casuist alive.

The only solid foundation for morality appears to be the character of the Supreme Being, whose harmony arises from a balance of attributes; and, to speak with reverence, one attribute seems to imply the necessity of another. He must be just, because he is wise; he must be good, because he is omnipotent. To exalt one attribute at the expense of another equally noble and necessary bears the stamp of man's warped reason, and is the homage of passion. Man, accustomed in his savage state to bow down before power, can seldom rid himself of that barbarous prejudice even when civilization has shown him how far mental strength is superior to bodily; and his reason stays clouded by these crude opinions even when he is thinking about the Deity. God's omnipotence is made to swallow up, or to preside over, all his other attributes, and anyone who thinks that power must be regulated by wisdom is supposed to be limiting it irreverently.

I disclaim the kind of humility which, having investigated nature, stops short at nature's author. The high and lofty One who inhabits eternity doubtless possesses many attributes we can form no conception of; but reason tells me that they cannot clash with those I adore, and I am compelled to listen to her voice.

It seems natural for man to search for excellence, and either to trace it in the object he worships or blindly to drape perfection over it like a garment. But what good effect can the second kind of worship have on the moral conduct of a rational being? He bows to power; he adores a dark cloud which may open on a bright prospect or burst in angry, lawless fury on his devoted head, and he does not know why. And if the Deity acts from the vague impulse of an undirected will, then man must follow his own will too, or else act by rules drawn from principles he has just declared it irreverent to apply. Both enthusiasts and cooler thinkers have fallen into this dilemma when they have laboured to free men from the wholesome restraints that a just conception of God's character imposes.

It is not impious to examine the attributes of the Almighty in this way; indeed, who that exercises his faculties at all can avoid it? For to love God as the fountain of wisdom, goodness and power appears to be the only worship of any use to a being who wants to acquire either virtue or knowledge. A blind, unsettled affection may, like the human passions, occupy the mind and warm the heart, while doing justice, loving mercy and walking humbly with our God is forgotten. I shall come back to this subject when I consider religion in a light quite opposite to the one Dr. Gregory recommends, who treats it as a matter of sentiment or taste.

To return from this apparent digression. It is to be wished that women would cherish an affection for their husbands founded on the same principle that devotion ought to rest on. There is no other firm base under heaven, and let them beware of the deceptive light of sentiment, too often used as a softer word for sensuality. It follows then, I think, that from infancy women should either be shut up like eastern princes, or educated in such a way as to be able to think and act for themselves.

Why do men halt between two opinions and expect impossibilities? Why do they expect virtue from a slave, or from a being whom the constitution of civil society has made weak, if not vicious?

Still, I know it will take a considerable time to root out the firmly planted prejudices that sensualists have sown. It will also take some time to convince women that they act against their own real interest, taken on any large scale, when they cherish or affect weakness under the name of delicacy; and to convince the world that the poisoned source of female vices and follies — if custom obliges me to use those words loosely as synonyms — has been the sensual homage paid to beauty. To beauty of feature, that is; for a German writer has shrewdly observed that a pretty woman, as an object of desire, is generally acknowledged so by men of every description, whereas a fine woman, who inspires more sublime emotions by displaying an intellectual beauty, may be overlooked, or noticed with indifference, by men who find their happiness in gratifying their appetites. I foresee the obvious retort: that while man remains as imperfect a being as he has so far shown himself, he will be more or less the slave of his appetites; and that since the women who gratify a dominant appetite obtain the most power, the sex is degraded by a physical if not by a moral necessity.

I grant that this objection has some force. But so long as so sublime a precept exists as "be pure as your heavenly father is pure", it would seem that man's virtues are not limited by the only Being who could limit them, and that he may press forward without asking whether so noble an ambition takes him out of his sphere. To the wild waves it was said, "this far you shall come, and no further; and here your proud waves shall be stopped." So they beat and foam in vain, restrained by the power that keeps the struggling planets in their orbits; matter yields to the great governing Spirit. But an immortal soul, not restrained by mechanical laws and struggling to free itself from the shackles of matter, adds to the order of creation instead of disturbing it, when, cooperating with the Father of spirits, it tries to govern itself by that unvarying rule which regulates the universe to a degree our imagination faints before.

Besides, if women are educated for dependence — that is, to act according to the will of another fallible being and to submit to power whether it is right or wrong — where are we to stop? Are they to be considered as deputies, allowed to reign over a small domain and answerable for their conduct to a higher tribunal, itself liable to error?

It will not be hard to prove that such deputies act like men who have been subdued by fear, and will make their children and servants endure their tyrannical oppression. Since they submit without reason, they will have no fixed rules to square their own conduct by, and will be kind or cruel just as the whim of the moment directs; and we ought not to be surprised if, galled sometimes by their own heavy yoke, they take a malignant pleasure in resting it on weaker shoulders.

But suppose a woman brought up to obedience marries a sensible man who directs her judgment without making her feel the servility of her position, so that she acts with as much propriety by this reflected light as can be expected when reason is taken at second hand. Even so she cannot guarantee the life of her protector. He may die and leave her with a large family.

A double duty then falls on her: to educate them in the character of both father and mother, to form their principles and secure their property. But she has never thought for herself, much less acted for herself. She has only learned to please men and to depend on them gracefully. Encumbered with children, how is she to get another protector — a husband to take the place of reason? A rational man, for we are not on romantic ground here, may think her a pleasing docile creature and still not choose to marry a family for love, when the world contains plenty more pretty creatures. What is to become of her, then? Either she falls an easy prey to some mean fortune hunter who defrauds her children of their father's inheritance and makes her miserable, or she becomes the victim of discontent and blind indulgence. Unable to educate her sons, or to impress them with respect — for it is no play on words to say that people are never respected, however important their station, if they are not respectable — she pines under the anguish of useless, helpless regret. The serpent's tooth enters into her very soul, and the vices of licentious youth bring her with sorrow, and perhaps with poverty as well, to the grave.

This is not an overdrawn picture. On the contrary, it is a very possible case, and something like it must have come under every attentive eye.

I have, however, assumed that she was well disposed, though experience shows that the blind can be led into a ditch as easily as along the beaten road. But suppose — and it is no very improbable guess — that a being taught only to please must go on finding her happiness in pleasing: what an example of folly, not to say of vice, she will be to her innocent daughters! The mother is lost in the coquette; and instead of making friends of her daughters she looks at them sideways, because they are rivals — crueller rivals than any others, because they invite comparison and drive her from the throne of beauty, having never once thought of a seat on the bench of reason.

It needs no lively pencil, no exaggerated outline of caricature, to sketch the domestic miseries and petty vices that such a mistress of a family spreads around her. And still she is only acting as a woman ought to act who has been brought up on Rousseau's system. She can never be reproached for being masculine or stepping out of her sphere. Indeed she may observe another of his grand rules, and, carefully keeping her reputation spotless, be reckoned a good sort of woman. Yet in what sense can she be called good? She abstains, it is true, and without much of a struggle, from committing gross crimes. But how does she fulfil her duties? Duties! In truth she has quite enough to think about in adorning her body and nursing a weak constitution.

As for religion, she never presumed to judge for herself, but conformed, as a dependent creature should, to the ceremonies of the church she was brought up in, piously believing that wiser heads than hers had settled that business, and that never doubting is her point of perfection. So she pays her tithe of mint and cumin, and thanks God that she is not as other women are. These are the blessed effects of a good education! These are the virtues of man's helpmate.

I must relieve myself by drawing a different picture.

Let fancy present a woman of tolerable understanding — I have no wish to leave the line of mediocrity — whose constitution, strengthened by exercise, has let her body reach its full vigour, while her mind has gradually expanded to take in the moral duties of life and to see what human virtue and dignity consist in. Formed in this way by the duties of her station, she marries from affection without losing sight of prudence; and, looking beyond marital bliss, she secures her husband's respect before there is any need to exert mean arts to please him and feed a dying flame which nature doomed to go out once its object became familiar, once friendship and forbearance take the place of a more ardent affection. That is the natural death of love, and domestic peace is not destroyed by struggles to prevent it. I am supposing the husband to be virtuous too; otherwise she needs independent principles even more.

Fate, however, breaks the tie. She is left a widow, perhaps without adequate provision — but she is not desolate. The pang of nature is felt; but after time has softened sorrow into a melancholy resignation, her heart turns to her children with redoubled fondness, and, anxious to provide for them, affection gives her maternal duties a sacred, heroic cast. She thinks that her virtuous efforts are seen not only by the eye from which all her comfort must now flow, and whose approval is life to her; her imagination, a little abstracted and exalted by grief, dwells also on the fond hope that the eyes her trembling hand closed may still see how she subdues every wayward passion to fulfil the double duty of being father as well as mother to her children. Raised to heroism by misfortune, she suppresses the first faint dawning of a natural inclination before it can ripen into love, and in the bloom of life forgets her sex — forgets the pleasure of an awakening passion that might have been inspired and returned again. She no longer thinks of pleasing, and a conscious dignity keeps her from priding herself on the praise her conduct earns. Her children have her love, and her brightest hopes lie beyond the grave, where her imagination often wanders.

I think I can see her surrounded by her children, reaping the reward of her care. The intelligent eye meets hers while health and innocence smile on their chubby cheeks; and as they grow up, the cares of life are lightened by their grateful attention. She lives to see the virtues she tried to plant on principle fixed into habits, and to see her children attain enough strength of character to endure adversity without forgetting their mother's example.

The task of life fulfilled in this way, she waits calmly for the sleep of death; and rising from the grave she may say: you gave me one talent, and here are five.

I wish to sum up what I have said in a few words, for here I throw down my gauntlet and deny the existence of sexual virtues, not excepting modesty. For men and women, truth, if I understand the meaning of the word, must be the same. Yet the fanciful female character so prettily drawn by poets and novelists demands the sacrifice of truth and sincerity; and so virtue becomes a relative idea with no other foundation than utility — and men claim the arbitrary right to judge that utility, shaping it to suit their own convenience.

Women, I allow, may have different duties to fulfil; but they are human duties, and the principles that should regulate the discharge of them, I stoutly maintain, must be the same.

To become respectable, they must exercise their understanding; there is no other foundation for independence of character. I mean explicitly to say that they must bow only to the authority of reason, instead of being the modest slaves of opinion.

In the higher ranks of life, how seldom do we meet a man of superior ability, or even of common attainments? The reason seems to me clear: the state they are born into was an unnatural one. Human character has always been formed by the employments an individual or a class pursues, and if the faculties are not sharpened by necessity they must stay blunt. The argument may fairly be extended to women; for, seldom occupied by any serious business, the pursuit of pleasure gives their character that insignificance which makes the society of the great so insipid. The same lack of firmness, produced by a similar cause, drives both to fly from themselves into noisy pleasures and artificial passions, until vanity takes the place of every social affection and the marks of humanity can hardly be made out at all. Such are the blessings of our civil governments as they are at present organized, that wealth and female softness tend equally to debase humankind, and both are produced by the same cause. But if women are rational creatures they should be spurred on to acquire virtues they can call their own; for how can a rational being be ennobled by anything that is not obtained by its own exertions?

Chapter Four: On the Degraded State to Which Various Causes Have Reduced Women

That woman is either naturally weak or has been degraded by a combination of circumstances seems to me clear. But I shall simply set that proposition beside a conclusion I have often heard sensible men draw in favour of aristocracy: that the mass of humankind cannot amount to anything, or else the obsequious slaves who patiently let themselves be penned up would feel their own importance and kick off their chains. Men submit to oppression everywhere, they add, when they have only to lift their heads to throw off the yoke; yet instead of asserting their birthright they quietly lick the dust and say, let us eat and drink, for tomorrow we die. Women, I argue by analogy, are degraded by the same tendency to enjoy the present moment, and end by despising a freedom they have not virtue enough to struggle for. But I must be more explicit.

As far as cultivating the heart goes, everyone agrees that sex does not come into it; but the line of subordination in mental powers is never to be crossed. Being merely "absolute in loveliness", the portion of rationality granted to women is a very scanty one indeed; for once you deny her genius and judgment it is hard to guess what is left to constitute an intellect at all.

The very stuff of immortality, if I may put it that way, is the perfectibility of human reason. If man had been created perfect, or if a flood of knowledge broke in on him at maturity that made error impossible, I should doubt whether his existence continued after the body dissolved. But as things are, every difficulty in morals that escapes human discussion, and that baffles profound thought and the lightning glance of genius alike, is an argument on which I build my belief in the immortality of the soul. Reason is accordingly the simple power of improvement — or, more properly, of discerning truth. In this respect every individual is a world in itself. More of it may show in one being than in another, but the nature of reason must be the same in all, if it is an emanation of divinity, the tie connecting the creature with the Creator. For can a soul be stamped with the heavenly image which is not perfected by exercising its own reason? Yet, outwardly ornamented with elaborate care and adorned to delight a man, "that with honour he may love", the soul of woman is not allowed this distinction; and, with a man always placed between her and reason, she is always represented as created only to see through a coarse medium and to take things on trust. But dismissing these fanciful theories, and considering woman as a whole — whatever that whole may be — rather than as a part of man, the question is whether she has reason or not. If she has, which I will assume for the moment, then she was not created merely to be man's comfort, and what belongs to her sex should not be allowed to destroy what belongs to her humanity.

Footnote: See Milton.

Men have probably been led into this error by looking at education in a false light: not as the first step in forming a being who is advancing gradually towards perfection, but only as a preparation for life. On this sensual error, for I must call it that, the whole false system of female manners has been raised, which robs women of their dignity and classes the dark and the fair alike with the smiling flowers that merely decorate the landscape. This has always been the language of men, and the fear of departing from a supposed character proper to their sex has made even women of superior sense adopt the same sentiments. So understanding, strictly speaking, has been denied to woman, and instinct, refined into wit and cunning for the purposes of life, has been put in its place.

Footnote: This word is not strictly the right one, but I cannot find a better.

The power of generalizing ideas, of drawing broad conclusions from individual observations, is the only acquirement for an immortal being that really deserves the name of knowledge. Merely to observe without trying to account for anything may serve, very incompletely, as the common sense of daily life; but where is the store being laid up that is to clothe the soul when it leaves the body?

This power has not only been denied to women; writers have insisted that, with a few exceptions, it is inconsistent with the character of their sex. Let men prove that, and I shall grant that woman exists only for man. First, though, I must remark that the power of generalizing ideas to any great extent is not very common among men or women. But this exercise is the true cultivation of the understanding — and everything conspires to make cultivating the understanding harder for women than for men.

That assertion leads me naturally to the main subject of this chapter, and I shall now try to point out some of the causes that degrade my sex and prevent women from generalizing their observations.

I shall not go back into remote antiquity to trace the history of woman. It is enough to grant that she has always been either a slave or a despot, and to remark that both conditions retard the progress of reason equally. The great source of female folly and vice has always seemed to me to be narrowness of mind; and the very constitution of civil governments has put nearly insuperable obstacles in the way of cultivating the female understanding — yet virtue can be built on no other foundation. The same obstacles are put in the way of the rich, and the same consequences follow.

Necessity has proverbially been called the mother of invention; the saying may be extended to virtue. Virtue is an acquirement, and one for which pleasure must be sacrificed — and who sacrifices pleasure when it lies within reach, if their mind has not been opened and strengthened by adversity, or their pursuit of knowledge goaded on by necessity? It is a happy thing when people have the cares of life to struggle with, for the struggle keeps them from falling prey to enervating vices out of sheer idleness. But if men and women are placed from birth in a tropical zone with the noonday sun of pleasure beating straight down on them, how can they brace their minds enough to discharge the duties of life, or even to relish the affections that carry them out of themselves?

Pleasure is the business of a woman's life as society is at present arranged, and while that goes on being true, little is to be expected of such weak beings. Inheriting in a direct line from the first fair defect in nature the sovereignty of beauty, they have, in order to keep that power, given up the natural rights that exercising their reason might have won them, choosing to be short-lived queens rather than labour for the sober pleasures that come from equality. Exalted by their inferiority — this sounds like a contradiction — they constantly demand homage as women, though experience should teach them that the men who pride themselves on paying this arbitrary, insolent respect to the sex with the most scrupulous exactness are the likeliest to tyrannize over and despise the very weakness they cherish. Often they repeat Mr. Hume's remark, where, comparing the French and Athenian character, he alludes to women. "But what is more singular in this whimsical nation, I say to the Athenians, is that a frolic of yours during the Saturnalia, when the slaves are waited on by their masters, is seriously kept up by them all year round, and all their lives, and with some further circumstances that make the absurdity and the ridicule greater still. Your sport only raises up for a few days those whom fortune has cast down, and whom she may in her own sport really raise above you forever. But this nation gravely exalts those whom nature has made subject to them, and whose inferiority and infirmities are absolutely incurable. The women, though without virtue, are their masters and sovereigns."

Why do women — I write with affectionate concern — condescend to accept from strangers a degree of attention and respect quite different from the reciprocal civility that humanity and the politeness of civilization allow between one man and another? And why do they not discover, while they are "in the noon of beauty's power", that they are treated like queens only so that hollow respect may delude them until they resign, or never claim, their natural prerogatives? Shut up in cages like birds, they have nothing to do but preen themselves and stalk with mock majesty from perch to perch. It is true they are provided with food and clothing for which they neither toil nor spin — but health, liberty and virtue are given in exchange. And where among humankind has there ever been found strength of mind enough to let a creature resign these adventitious privileges: someone who, rising with the calm dignity of reason above opinion, dared to be proud of the privileges inherent in being human? It is vain to expect it while hereditary power chokes the affections and nips reason in the bud.

The passions of men have thus set women on thrones; and until humankind grows more reasonable it is to be feared that women will avail themselves of the power that costs them least exertion and is least disputed. They will smile — yes, they will smile — even when they are told that

	"In beauty's empire is no mean,
	And woman either slave or queen,
	Is quickly scorn'd when not ador'd."

But the adoration comes first, and the scorn is not looked for.

Louis XIV in particular spread artificial manners and caught the whole nation in his toils by a plausible method: in establishing an artful chain of despotism, he made it every individual's interest to respect his station and support his power. And women, whom he flattered by a childish attention to the whole sex, obtained in his reign that prince-like distinction so fatal to reason and virtue.

A king is always a king, and a woman always a woman: his authority and her sex stand forever between them and rational conversation. With a lover, I grant, she should be a woman, and her sensibility will naturally lead her to try to stir emotion, not to gratify her vanity but her heart. That I do not call coquetry; it is the artless impulse of nature. I protest only against the sexual desire of conquest when the heart is not involved at all.

Footnote: And a wit is always a wit, one might add; the vain fooleries by which wits and beauties get attention and make conquests are much on a level.

This desire is not confined to women. "I have tried," says Lord Chesterfield, "to win the hearts of twenty women whose persons I would not have given a fig for." The libertine who in a gust of passion takes advantage of unsuspecting tenderness is a saint compared with this cold-hearted scoundrel — and I like to use words that mean something. Yet, taught only to please, women are always on the watch to please, and with truly heroic ardour set about winning hearts merely in order to give them up, or spurn them, once the victory is decided and conspicuous.

I must come down to the small details of the subject.

I am sorry to see women systematically degraded by receiving the trivial attentions that men think it manly to pay them, when in fact those attentions are an insulting way of propping up their own superiority. It is not condescension to bow to an inferior. Indeed these ceremonies look so ludicrous to me that I can hardly control my face when I see a man start up with eager and serious concern to pick up a handkerchief or shut a door which the lady could have managed herself if she had only moved a pace or two.

A wild wish has just flown from my heart to my head, and I will not stifle it, though it may raise a horse laugh. I earnestly wish to see the distinction of sex confounded in society, except where love animates the behaviour. For that distinction is, I am firmly persuaded, the foundation of the weakness of character attributed to women; it is why the understanding is neglected while accomplishments are acquired with painstaking care; and the same cause explains why women prefer the graceful virtues to the heroic ones.

Everybody, of every description, wishes to be loved and respected for something, and the common herd will always take the shortest road to what it wants. The respect paid to wealth and beauty is the most certain and unmistakable, and so it will always catch the vulgar eye of ordinary minds. Ability and virtue are absolutely necessary to raise a man out of the middle rank of life into notice, and the natural consequence is notorious: the middle rank contains most of the virtue and most of the ability. Men have, in that station at least, a chance of exerting themselves with dignity and of rising by exertions that really improve a rational creature. But women as a whole are, until their characters are formed, in the same condition as the rich: they are born — I am speaking of a state of civilization — with certain privileges of sex; and while those are granted them for nothing, few will ever think of doing more than is required in order to earn the esteem of a small number of superior people.

When do we hear of women who, starting out of obscurity, boldly claim respect for their great abilities or their daring virtues? Where are they to be found? "To be observed, to be attended to, to be taken notice of with sympathy, complacency and approval, is all the advantage they look for." True, my male readers will probably exclaim. But before they draw any conclusion, let them recollect that this was not originally written to describe women, but to describe the rich. In Dr. Smith's Theory of Moral Sentiments I have found a general character of people of rank and fortune which might, in my opinion, be applied with the greatest propriety to women. I refer the shrewd reader to the whole comparison, but I must be allowed to quote a passage in support of the argument I mean to press as the most conclusive of all against the notion of a character proper to each sex. For if, warriors excepted, no great men of any description have ever appeared among the nobility, may we not fairly infer that their situation swallowed up the man, and produced a character like that of women, who are localized, if I may use the word, by the rank they are placed in — by courtesy? Women, commonly called ladies, are not to be contradicted in company, are not allowed to exert any physical strength, and are expected to show only the negative virtues, when any virtues are expected of them at all: patience, docility, good humour and pliancy, virtues incompatible with any vigorous exertion of intellect. Besides, living more with each other and being seldom absolutely alone, they are more under the influence of sentiments than of passions. Solitude and reflection are needed to give wishes the force of passions, and to let the imagination enlarge an object until it becomes the most desirable thing in the world. The same may be said of the rich: they do not deal enough in general ideas, collected by dispassionate thinking or calm investigation, to acquire the strength of character on which great resolutions are built. But hear what an acute observer says of the great.

"Do the great seem unaware how cheaply they may acquire the public's admiration? Or do they imagine that for them, as for other men, it must be bought with sweat or with blood? By what important accomplishments is the young nobleman taught to support the dignity of his rank, and to make himself worthy of that superiority over his fellow citizens which the virtue of his ancestors won for them? Is it by knowledge, by industry, by patience, by self-denial, by any virtue at all? Since all his words and all his movements are attended to, he acquires a habitual regard for every circumstance of ordinary behaviour, and studies to perform all those small duties with the most exact propriety. Conscious of how closely he is watched, and how ready people are to indulge all his inclinations, he acts on the most trivial occasions with the freedom and elevation this naturally inspires. His air, his manner, his bearing all mark that elegant and graceful sense of his own superiority which those born to a lower station can hardly ever arrive at. These are the arts by which he means to make people submit more easily to his authority and govern their inclinations according to his own pleasure — and in this he is seldom disappointed. These arts, supported by rank and pre-eminence, are enough on ordinary occasions to govern the world. Louis XIV, during most of his reign, was regarded not only in France but all over Europe as the most perfect model of a great prince. But what were the talents and virtues by which he acquired that great reputation? Was it the scrupulous and inflexible justice of everything he undertook, the immense dangers and difficulties attending his enterprises, or the unwearied and unrelenting application with which he pursued them? Was it his extensive knowledge, his exquisite judgment, his heroic valour? It was none of these. He was, first of all, the most powerful prince in Europe and consequently held the highest rank among kings; and then, says his historian, 'he surpassed all his courtiers in the gracefulness of his figure and the majestic beauty of his features. The sound of his voice, noble and affecting, won the hearts that his presence intimidated. He had a step and a bearing that could suit nobody but him and his rank, and that would have been ridiculous in anyone else. The embarrassment he caused those who spoke to him flattered the secret satisfaction with which he felt his own superiority.' These frivolous accomplishments, supported by his rank and no doubt by some degree of other talents and virtues — which seem, however, to have been not much above mediocrity — established this prince in the esteem of his own age, and have drawn even from posterity a good deal of respect for his memory. Compared with these, in his own time and in his own presence, no other virtue seemed to have any merit at all. Knowledge, industry, valour and beneficence trembled, were abashed, and lost all their dignity before them."

Woman too, being thus "in herself complete" by possessing all these frivolous accomplishments, so changes the nature of things that

	—"That what she wills to do or say
	Seems wisest, virtuousest, discreetest, best;
	All higher knowledge in her presence falls
	Degraded. Wisdom in discourse with her
	Loses discountenanc'd, and like folly shows;
	Authority and reason on her wait."—

And all of it is built on her loveliness!

In the middle rank of life, to carry the comparison on, men are prepared in youth for a profession, and marriage is not considered the great event of their lives; whereas women have no other scheme to sharpen their faculties on. It is not business, or extensive plans, or any of the roving flights of ambition that engage their attention; their thoughts are not employed in raising such noble structures. To rise in the world and win the liberty of running from one pleasure to another, they must marry advantageously; and to that object their time is sacrificed and their persons often legally prostituted. A man entering a profession has his eye steadily fixed on some future advantage — and the mind gains great strength from having all its efforts directed to one point — and, full of his business, he thinks of pleasure as mere relaxation; whereas women look for pleasure as the main purpose of existence. In fact, from the education society gives them, the love of pleasure may be said to govern them all. But does that prove that there is a sex in souls? It would be just as reasonable to declare that the courtiers of France, when a destructive system of despotism had formed their character, were not men, because liberty, virtue and humanity had been sacrificed to pleasure and vanity. Fatal passions, which have always domineered over the whole race!

The same love of pleasure, fostered by the whole tendency of their education, gives a trifling turn to women's conduct in most situations. They are forever anxious about secondary things, and on the lookout for adventures instead of being occupied with duties.

A man setting out on a journey generally has the end of it in view; a woman thinks more about the incidental occurrences, the strange things that may happen on the road, the impression she may make on her fellow travellers — and above all she is anxiously intent on looking after the finery she has brought with her, which is more than ever a part of herself when she is going to appear in a new scene, when, to borrow an apt French turn of phrase, she is going to produce a sensation. Can dignity of mind survive such trivial cares?

In short, women in general, like the rich of both sexes, have acquired all the follies and vices of civilization and missed its useful fruit. I need not keep repeating that I am speaking of the condition of women as a whole and leaving exceptions out of the question. Their senses are inflamed and their understandings neglected; so they fall prey to their senses, delicately named sensibility, and are blown about by every momentary gust of feeling. They are therefore in a much worse condition than they would be in a state nearer to nature. Forever restless and anxious, their over-exercised sensibility makes them not only uncomfortable in themselves but troublesome, to use a soft phrase, to others. All their thoughts turn on things calculated to excite emotion; and, feeling where they should reason, their conduct is unstable and their opinions wavering — not the wavering produced by deliberation or by a widening view, but by contradictory emotions. By fits and starts they are warm about many pursuits, but that warmth, never concentrated into perseverance, soon exhausts itself, evaporated by its own heat, or else it meets some other fleeting passion to which reason has never given any weight, and neutrality follows. Miserable indeed must that being be whose cultivation of mind has only inflamed its passions! A distinction should be drawn between inflaming them and strengthening them. What can be expected to follow when the passions are pampered in this way while the judgment is left unformed? Undoubtedly a mixture of madness and folly.

This observation should not be confined to the fair sex; but for the present I mean to apply it only to them.

Novels, music, poetry and gallantry all tend to make women creatures of sensation, and their character is formed in this way during the years when they are acquiring accomplishments — the only improvement their station in society encourages them to seek. This over-stretched sensibility naturally slackens the other powers of the mind and stops the intellect from attaining the sovereignty it ought to have if a rational creature is to be useful to others and content in its own station; for exercising the understanding, as life goes on, is the only method nature points out for calming the passions.

Satiety has a very different effect, and I have often been forcibly struck by a vivid description of damnation in which the spirit is shown hovering forever with futile eagerness round the defiled body, unable to enjoy anything without the organs of sense. And yet women are made slaves to their senses, because it is through their sensibility that they get their present power.

And will moralists pretend to say that this is the condition in which one half of the human race should be encouraged to stay, in listless inactivity and stupid acquiescence? Kind instructors! What were we created for? To remain innocent, it may be said — by which they mean, in a state of childhood. We might as well never have been born, unless it were necessary that we should be created to enable man to acquire the noble privilege of reason, the power of telling good from evil, while we lie down in the dust we were taken from, never to rise again.

It would be an endless task to trace all the varieties of meanness, care and sorrow into which women are plunged by the prevailing opinion that they were created to feel rather than to reason, and that all the power they can get must be got by their charms and their weakness —

	"Fine by defect, and amiably weak!"

And, made entirely dependent by this amiable weakness — dependent on men for advice as well as protection, except for what they gain by illicit sway — is it surprising that, neglecting the duties reason alone points out and shrinking from any trial that might strengthen their minds, they exert themselves only to give their defects a graceful covering, which may heighten their charms in the voluptuary's eye though it sinks them below the scale of moral excellence?

Fragile in every sense of the word, they are obliged to look up to a man for every comfort. In the most trifling danger they cling to their support with parasitic tenacity, piteously begging for help; and their natural protector extends his arm or raises his voice to defend the lovely trembler — from what? Perhaps the frown of an old cow, or the jump of a mouse; a rat would be a serious danger. In the name of reason, and even of common sense, what can save such beings from contempt, however soft and fair they may be?

These fears, when they are not put on, may be very pretty; but they show a degree of feebleness that degrades a rational creature in a way women do not realise — for love and esteem are very different things.

I am fully persuaded we should hear nothing of these infantile airs if girls were allowed enough exercise and were not shut up in close rooms until their muscles are slack and their digestion ruined. To carry the remark further: if fear in girls, instead of being encouraged and perhaps even created, were treated the way cowardice in boys is treated, we should soon see women with more dignified faces. It is true they could not then be called with equal propriety the sweet flowers that smile along man's path; but they would be more respectable members of society and would discharge the important duties of life by the light of their own reason. "Educate women like men," says Rousseau, "and the more they resemble our sex the less power will they have over us." That is precisely the point I am aiming at. I do not wish them to have power over men, but over themselves.

I have heard men argue in exactly the same strain against instructing the poor, for aristocracy takes many forms. "Teach them to read and write," they say, "and you take them out of the station nature assigned them." An eloquent Frenchman has answered them, and I will borrow his sentiments. They do not know that when they make a man into a brute they may expect at any instant to see him transformed into a ferocious beast. Without knowledge there can be no morality.

Ignorance is a frail base for virtue! Yet that ignorance is the condition woman was made for has been insisted on by the very writers who have argued most vehemently for the superiority of man — a superiority not of degree but of essence; though, to soften the argument, they have laboured to prove with chivalrous generosity that the sexes ought not to be compared at all: man was made to reason, woman to feel, and together, flesh and spirit, they make the most perfect whole by happily blending reason and sensibility into one character.

And what is sensibility? "Quickness of sensation; quickness of perception; delicacy." So Dr. Johnson defines it, and the definition gives me no idea except of an exquisitely polished instinct. I can discern no trace of the image of God in either sensation or matter. Refined seventy times seven, they are still material; intellect does not live there; nor will fire ever turn lead into gold.

I come round to my old argument. If woman is allowed to have an immortal soul, she must have an understanding to improve as the employment of her life. And when, to make the present state more complete — though everything proves it to be only a fraction of a mighty sum — she is tempted by present gratification to forget her grand destination, then nature is thwarted, or else she was born only to breed and rot. Or, if we grant animals of every description a soul, though not a rational one, the exercise of instinct and sensibility may be the step they are to take in this life towards attaining reason in the next; so that through all eternity they will lag behind man, who, for reasons we cannot tell, was given the power of attaining reason in his first mode of existence.

When I come to the duties peculiar to women, as I should come to the duties peculiar to a citizen or a father, it will be seen that I do not mean to hint that women should be taken out of their families — speaking of the majority. "He who has a wife and children," says Lord Bacon, "has given hostages to fortune; for they are impediments to great enterprises, whether of virtue or of mischief. Certainly the best works, and those of greatest merit to the public, have come from unmarried or childless men." I say the same of women. But the welfare of society is not built on extraordinary exertions; and if society were more reasonably organized there would be even less need of great abilities or heroic virtues.

In the regulation of a family and the education of children, understanding in the plainest sense is particularly required, and strength of body and mind as well. Yet the men who have laboured hardest in their writings to keep women at home have tried, with arguments dictated by a coarse appetite that satiety had made fastidious, to weaken their bodies and cramp their minds. If even by these underhand methods they really did persuade women, by working on their feelings, to stay at home and fulfil the duties of a mother and mistress of a family, I should be cautious about opposing opinions that led women to right conduct, by getting them to make the discharge of a duty the business of life, even though reason was insulted in the process. Yet — and I appeal to experience — if by neglecting the understanding women are as much detached from those domestic duties as they could be by the most serious intellectual pursuit, and indeed more so, then, though it may be observed that most people will never vigorously pursue any intellectual object, I may be allowed to infer that reason is absolutely necessary to enable a woman to perform any duty properly. And I must repeat once more that sensibility is not reason.

The comparison with the rich keeps occurring to me; for when men neglect the duties of humanity, women will do the same, since a common stream hurries them both along with thoughtless speed. Riches and honours prevent a man from enlarging his understanding and enervate all his powers by reversing the order of nature, which has always made true pleasure the reward of labour. Pleasure — enervating pleasure — is likewise within a woman's reach without her earning it. But until hereditary possessions are spread more widely, how can we expect men to be proud of virtue? And until they are, women will govern them by the most direct means, neglecting their dull domestic duties to catch the pleasure that is on the wing of time.

"The power of a woman," some author says, "is her sensibility"; and men, not seeing the consequence, do everything they can to make that power swallow up every other. Those who constantly employ their sensibility will have most of it — poets, painters and composers, for example. Yet when sensibility is increased in this way at the expense of reason, and even of imagination, why do philosophical men complain of women's fickleness? The sexual attentions of men act particularly on female sensibility, and this sympathy has been exercised in them from their youth up. A husband cannot go on paying those attentions with the passion needed to excite lively emotions, and a heart used to lively emotions turns to a new lover, or pines in secret, the prey of virtue or of prudence. I mean where the heart really has been made susceptible and the taste formed; for from what I have seen of fashionable life I am inclined to conclude that the mode of education, and the intercourse between the sexes that I have been condemning, foster vanity oftener than sensibility, and that coquetry comes more often from vanity than from the inconstancy that overstrained sensibility naturally produces.

Another argument that has weighed a great deal with me must, I think, have some force with every considerate and benevolent heart. Girls who have been educated in this feeble way are often cruelly left by their parents with no provision at all, and so depend not only on their brothers' reason but on their brothers' generosity. Those brothers are, to put the case at its fairest, decent sorts of men, and give as a favour what children of the same parents had an equal right to. A docile woman may stay in this ambiguous, humiliating position for some time with a tolerable degree of comfort. But when the brother marries, as he probably will, she stops being regarded as the mistress of the house and is looked at sideways as an intruder, an unnecessary burden on the benevolence of its master and his new partner.

Who can count the misery that so many unfortunate beings, weak in mind and body alike, suffer in such a position — unable to work and ashamed to beg? The wife, a cold-hearted, narrow-minded woman — and this is not an unfair supposition, since the present mode of education does no more to enlarge the heart than the understanding — is jealous of the small kindnesses her husband shows his relations; and, her sensibility never rising as far as humanity, she resents seeing her children's property lavished on a helpless sister.

These are matters of fact that have come under my eye again and again. The consequence is obvious. The wife resorts to cunning to undermine an habitual affection she is afraid to oppose openly, and neither tears nor caresses are spared until the spy has been worked out of her home and thrown on a world she is unprepared for; or else sent off, as a great effort of generosity or out of some regard for propriety, with a small allowance and an uncultivated mind, into joyless solitude.

These two women may be much on a par as to reason and humanity, and if their situations were exchanged might have acted just the same selfish part. But if they had been educated differently the case would have been very different too. The wife would not have had that kind of sensibility whose centre is herself, and reason might have taught her not to expect her husband's affection — nor even to be flattered by it — if it led him to violate earlier duties. She would want to love him not merely because he loved her but on account of his virtues; and the sister might have been able to fight her own way instead of eating the bitter bread of dependence.

I am persuaded that cultivation opens the heart as well as the understanding, and that strengthening the bodily organs does too, though that may seem less obvious. I am not talking now about momentary flashes of feeling, but about the affections. And perhaps the hardest task in educating either sex is to adjust the instruction so that it does not narrow the understanding while the heart is being warmed by the generous juices of spring, just raised by the electric fermentation of the season; nor dry up the feelings by employing the mind on investigations remote from life.

When women do receive a careful education, they are made either into fine ladies, brimful of sensibility and teeming with capricious fancies, or into merely capable managing women. The latter are often friendly, honest creatures with a shrewd kind of good sense joined to worldly prudence, which often makes them more useful members of society than the fine sentimental lady, though they have neither greatness of mind nor taste. The intellectual world is shut against them. Take them out of their family or their neighbourhood and they come to a standstill; the mind finds nothing to do, since literature offers a fund of amusement that they have never tried to enjoy, and often try to despise. The sentiments and taste of more cultivated minds look ridiculous to them even in people whom chance and family connection have led them to love; and in mere acquaintances they think it all affectation.

A man of sense can only love such a woman on account of her sex, and respect her because she is a trustworthy servant. To keep his own peace he lets her scold the servants and go to church in clothes made of the very best material. A man of her own size of understanding would probably not get on with her so well, since he might want to trespass on her prerogative and manage some of the household himself. Yet women whose minds have not been enlarged by cultivation, or whose natural selfishness of feeling has not been expanded by reflection, are very unfit to manage a family; for by an undue stretch of power they are always tyrannizing to maintain a superiority that rests on nothing but the arbitrary distinction of money. Sometimes the evil is more serious, and the servants are deprived of innocent indulgences and made to work beyond their strength so that the capable woman can keep a better table and outshine her neighbours in finery and display. If she attends to her children, it is generally to dress them expensively; and whether that attention comes from vanity or from fondness, it is equally pernicious.

Besides, how many women of this description pass their days, or at least their evenings, in discontent. Their husbands acknowledge that they are good managers and chaste wives, and then leave home in search of more agreeable — may I be allowed a significant French word? — more piquant society. And the patient drudge who does her task like a blind horse in a mill is cheated of her proper reward, since the wages owed her are her husband's caresses; and women who have so few resources in themselves do not bear the loss of this natural right very patiently.

A fine lady, on the other hand, has been taught to look down with contempt on the vulgar employments of life, though she has only been encouraged to acquire accomplishments that rise a single degree above sense; for not even bodily accomplishments can be acquired with any precision unless the understanding has been strengthened by exercise.
