Without a foundation of principles, taste is superficial, and grace must come from something deeper than imitation. The imagination, however, is heated and the feelings made fastidious, if not sophisticated, when no counterweight of judgment has been acquired — while the heart still remains artless, even as it grows too tender.

These women are often lovable, and their hearts are really more open to general benevolence, more alive to the sentiments that civilize life, than the square-elbowed family drudge's. But, wanting a due proportion of reflection and self-government, they can only inspire love; they are their husbands' mistresses while they keep any hold on their affections, and the platonic friends of their husbands' male acquaintance. These are the fair defects of nature, the women who seem to have been created not to enjoy the fellowship of men but to save them from sinking into absolute brutality by rubbing off the rough corners of their characters, and by playful dalliance to give some dignity to the appetite that draws them there. Gracious Creator of the whole human race! Have you created such a being as woman, who can trace your wisdom in your works and feel that you alone are by your nature exalted above her — for no better purpose than this? Can she believe she was made only to submit to man, her equal, a being sent into the world as she was to acquire virtue? Can she consent to be occupied merely in pleasing him, merely in adorning the earth, when her soul is capable of rising to you? And can she rest supinely dependent on man for reason, when she ought to be climbing the steep paths of knowledge alongside him?

If love is the supreme good, then let women be educated for nothing but to inspire it, and let every charm be polished to intoxicate the senses. But if they are moral beings, let them have a chance of becoming intelligent; and let their love for men be only a part of that glowing flame of universal love which, after encircling humanity, rises as grateful incense to God.

Fulfilling domestic duties takes a great deal of resolution, and a serious kind of perseverance that needs a firmer support than emotions, however lively and true to nature they may be. To set an example of order, which is the soul of virtue, some austerity of behaviour must be adopted, and that is hardly to be expected from a being who has been made from infancy the weathercock of her own sensations. Anyone who rationally intends to be useful must have a plan of conduct; and in discharging the simplest duty we are often obliged to act against the immediate impulse of tenderness or compassion. Severity is often the surest as well as the most sublime proof of affection. It is the lack of this power over the feelings, and of that lofty, dignified affection which makes a person prefer the future good of the person they love to a present gratification, that leads so many fond mothers to spoil their children; and it has made it a real question whether neglect or indulgence does more harm. I am inclined to think indulgence has done most.

Everyone seems to agree that children should be left in the care of women during childhood. Now from all the observation I have been able to make, women of sensibility are the least fit for this task, because, carried away by their feelings, they will infallibly spoil a child's temper. The management of the temper, the first and most important branch of education, needs the sober steady eye of reason and a plan of conduct equally far from tyranny and from indulgence — and those are exactly the extremes people of sensibility fall into by turns, always shooting past the mark. I have followed this line of reasoning much further, until I concluded that a person of genius is the most unsuitable person imaginable to employ in education, public or private. Minds of that rare species see things too much in the mass, and seldom if ever have a good temper. That habitual cheerfulness we call good humour is perhaps as rarely combined with great mental powers as it is with strong feelings. And people who follow the flights of genius with interest and admiration, or who with cooler approval absorb the instruction a profound thinker has laboriously prepared for them, ought not to be disgusted if they find the first choleric and the second morose; for liveliness of fancy, and a tenacious grasp of mind, are scarcely compatible with the pliant urbanity that leads a man to bend at least a little to the opinions and prejudices of others instead of confronting them roughly.

But in discussing education or manners, minds of the superior class are not what we have to consider; they may be left to chance. It is the multitude of moderate abilities who need instruction and who take the colour of the atmosphere they breathe. This respectable crowd, men and women alike, should not have their sensations heightened in the hothouse of luxurious idleness at the expense of their understanding; for unless there is some ballast of understanding they will never become either virtuous or free, and an aristocracy founded on property or on sterling talents will always sweep the alternately timid and ferocious slaves of feeling before it.

To take another view of the subject: the arguments men have used to degrade women, morally and physically, are numberless, and they are all brought forward with a show of reason because they are supposed to be drawn from nature. I must notice a few.

The female understanding has often been spoken of with contempt for reaching maturity sooner than the male. I shall not answer this argument by pointing to the early proofs of reason, and of genius, in Cowley, Milton and Pope; I shall simply appeal to experience to decide whether young men who are introduced into company early — and examples abound now — do not acquire the same precocity. The fact is so notorious that merely mentioning it must bring before anyone who goes into the world at all the image of a number of swaggering apes of men, whose understandings have been narrowed by being brought into the society of men when they ought to have been spinning a top or trundling a hoop.

Footnote: Many other names might be added.

Some naturalists have also asserted that men do not reach their full growth and strength until thirty, but that women are mature by twenty. I think they are reasoning on false ground, led astray by the male prejudice that makes beauty the perfection of a woman — mere beauty of feature and complexion, in the vulgar sense of the word, while male beauty is allowed some connection with the mind. Strength of body, and that character in the face which the French call a physionomie, women no more acquire before thirty than men do. The little artless tricks of children are, it is true, particularly pleasing and attractive; yet once the pretty freshness of youth has worn off, those artless graces become studied airs and disgust everyone of taste. In a girl's face we look only for vivacity and a bashful modesty; but once the springtide of life is over we look for soberer sense in a face, and for the traces of passion rather than the dimples of animal spirits, expecting to see that individuality of character which is the only thing that fastens the affections. Then we want to converse, not to fondle; to give scope to our imaginations as well as to the sensations of our hearts.

At twenty the beauty of the two sexes is equal, but men's libertinism leads them to make the distinction, and superannuated coquettes commonly agree with them, for when they can no longer inspire love they pay for the vigour and vivacity of youth. The French, who let more mind into their notion of beauty, prefer women of thirty. What I mean is that they allow a woman to be at her most perfect when vivacity gives place to reason and to that majestic seriousness of character which marks maturity, the resting point. In youth, up to twenty, the body shoots out; until thirty the solid parts are gaining density, and the flexible muscles, growing more rigid every day, give the face its character — that is, they trace the workings of the mind with the iron pen of fate, and tell us not only what powers are within but how they have been used.

It is worth observing that the animals who arrive slowly at maturity live longest and are of the noblest species. Men cannot, however, claim any natural superiority from the grandeur of longevity, for in that respect nature has not distinguished the male.

Polygamy is another physical degradation, and a plausible argument for a custom that blights every domestic virtue is drawn from the well-attested fact that in the countries where it is established more girls are born than boys. This looks like an indication of nature, and apparently reasonable speculations must give way to nature. A further conclusion obviously offers itself: if polygamy is necessary, then woman must be inferior to man and made for him.

We are very ignorant about how the foetus is formed in the womb, but it seems to me likely that some accidental physical cause may account for this phenomenon and show that it is not a law of nature. I have met with some pertinent observations on the subject in Forster's Account of the Isles of the South Sea, which will explain what I mean. After observing that among animals the sex with the more vigorous and hotter constitution always prevails and reproduces its own kind, he adds: "If this is applied to the inhabitants of Africa, it is evident that the men there, being accustomed to polygamy, are enervated by the use of so many women and are therefore less vigorous; while the women, on the contrary, are of a hotter constitution, not only because of their more irritable nerves, more sensitive organization and livelier fancy, but also because in their marriages they are deprived of the share of physical love which in a monogamous condition would all be theirs. And so, for these reasons, most children are born female."

"In the greater part of Europe it has been proved by the most accurate lists of mortality that the proportion of men to women is nearly equal, or that, if there is any difference, more males are born, in the proportion of 105 to 100."

The necessity of polygamy, then, does not appear. Yet when a man seduces a woman, it ought, I think, to be called a left-handed marriage, and the man should be legally obliged to maintain the woman and her children, unless adultery, which is a natural divorce, cancelled the obligation. And this law should stay in force as long as women's weakness allows the word seduction to be used as an excuse for their frailty and want of principle — as long, that is, as they depend on men for a living instead of earning it by the exercise of their own hands or heads. But such women should not be called wives in the full meaning of the relationship, or the whole purpose of marriage would be subverted, and all those endearing charities that flow from personal fidelity and give the tie its sanctity, when neither love nor friendship unites the hearts, would melt away into selfishness. The woman who is faithful to the father of her children deserves respect and should not be treated like a prostitute — though I readily grant that if a man and a woman need to live together in order to bring up their children, nature never intended a man to have more than one wife.

Still, highly as I respect marriage as the foundation of almost every social virtue, I cannot help feeling the liveliest compassion for those unfortunate women who are cut off from society and torn by a single mistake from all the affections and relationships that improve the heart and mind. Often it does not even deserve the name of a mistake, for many innocent girls become the dupes of a sincere and affectionate heart, and still more are — as one may emphatically call it — ruined before they know the difference between virtue and vice; prepared for infamy by their education, they become infamous. Refuges and Magdalen hospitals are not the proper remedies for these abuses. It is justice, not charity, that the world is short of.

A woman who has lost her honour imagines she cannot fall lower; and as for recovering her former station, that is impossible, since no effort can wash the stain away. Losing every spur in this way, and having no other means of support, prostitution becomes her only refuge, and her character is quickly depraved by circumstances the poor wretch has little power over, unless she possesses an uncommon share of sense and loftiness of spirit. Necessity never makes prostitution the business of a man's life, though numberless women are made systematically vicious this way. And that comes in great part from the idleness in which women are educated, always taught to look up to a man for a living and to consider their persons the proper return for his exertions in supporting them. Meretricious airs and the whole science of wantonness then have a more powerful stimulus behind them than either appetite or vanity — which lends force to the prevailing opinion that with chastity a woman loses everything respectable about her. Her character depends on observing one virtue, though the only passion fostered in her heart is love. Indeed a woman's honour is not even made to depend on her own will.

When Richardson makes Clarissa tell Lovelace that he had robbed her of her honour, he must have had strange notions of honour and of virtue. For miserable beyond all names of misery is the condition of a being who could be degraded without its own consent. I have heard this excess of strictness defended as a salutary error. I shall answer in the words of Leibniz: "Errors are often useful, but usually to remedy other errors."

Most of the evils of life come from a desire for present enjoyment that outruns itself. The obedience required of women in marriage comes under this description. The mind, naturally weakened by depending on authority, never exerts its own powers, and the obedient wife is turned into a weak, indolent mother. Or, supposing that is not always the consequence, a future state of existence is hardly taken into the reckoning when nothing but negative virtues are cultivated. For in writing about morals, and particularly when women are the subject, writers have too often considered virtue in a very limited sense, and made its foundation solely worldly utility. Indeed a still more fragile base has been given to this stupendous structure, and the wayward, fluctuating feelings of men have been made the standard of virtue. Yes — virtue, like religion, has been made subject to the decisions of taste.

It would almost raise a smile of contempt, if the vain absurdities of men did not strike us on every side, to see how eager men are to degrade the very sex from whom they pretend to receive the chief pleasure of life; and I have often turned Pope's sarcasm back on them with complete conviction — or rather, it has seemed to me to apply to the whole human race. A love of pleasure or of power seems to divide humankind between them, and the husband who lords it in his little harem thinks only of his pleasure or his convenience. Indeed an intemperate love of pleasure carries some prudent men, or worn-out libertines who marry to have a safe companion, to such lengths that they seduce their own wives. Marriage banishes modesty, and chaste love takes flight.

Love, considered as an animal appetite, cannot feed on itself for long without expiring; and this extinction in its own flame may be called the violent death of love. But a wife who has been made licentious this way will probably try to fill the gap left when her husband's attentions stop, for she cannot contentedly become merely an upper servant after being treated like a goddess. She is still handsome; and instead of transferring her fondness to her children she dreams only of enjoying the sunshine of life. Besides, there are many husbands so devoid of sense and of parental affection that during the first effervescence of voluptuous fondness they refuse to let their wives nurse their own children. The wives are only to dress and live to please them — and love, even innocent love, soon sinks into lasciviousness when a duty is sacrificed to indulging it.

Personal attachment is a very happy foundation for friendship; yet when even two virtuous young people marry, it might be a good thing if some circumstance checked their passion — if the memory of a previous attachment or a disappointed affection made it, on one side at least, a match founded rather on esteem. In that case they would look beyond the present moment and try to make the whole of life respectable by forming a plan to regulate a friendship that only death ought to dissolve.

Friendship is a serious affection, the most sublime of all, because it is founded on principle and cemented by time. The exact opposite may be said of love. To a great extent love and friendship cannot exist in the same breast; even when they are inspired by different objects they weaken or destroy each other, and for the same object they can only be felt one after the other. The vain fears and fond jealousies that fan the flame of love, when they are judiciously or artfully managed, are both incompatible with the tender confidence and sincere respect of friendship.

Love as the glowing pen of genius has drawn it does not exist on earth, or exists only in those exalted, fervid imaginations that have sketched such dangerous pictures. Dangerous, because they not only offer a plausible excuse to the voluptuary who disguises sheer sensuality under a sentimental veil, but because they spread affectation and take away from the dignity of virtue. Virtue, as the very word implies, should look serious if not austere; and to try to dress her up in the garb of pleasure, because the word has been used as another name for beauty, is to set her on a quicksand — a most insidious attempt to hasten her fall under the appearance of respect. Virtue and pleasure are not in fact so closely allied in this life as some eloquent writers have laboured to prove. Pleasure prepares the fading wreath and mixes the intoxicating cup; but the fruit virtue gives is the reward of toil, and, seen gradually as it ripens, offers nothing but a calm satisfaction — indeed, appearing to be simply the result of the natural tendency of things, it is hardly noticed at all. Bread, the common food of life, is seldom thought of as a blessing, and yet it supports the constitution and preserves health; while feasts delight the heart of man, though disease and even death lurk in the cup or the dainty that raises the spirits or tickles the palate. In the same way the lively, heated imagination draws its picture of love as it draws every other picture, in the glowing colours that a daring hand will steal from the rainbow, when it is directed by a mind condemned in a world like this to prove its noble origin by panting after an unattainable perfection, forever pursuing what it admits to be a fleeting dream. An imagination of this vigorous cast can give existence to insubstantial forms and stability to the shadowy reveries the mind naturally falls into when realities are found flat. It can then paint love with heavenly charms and dote on the grand ideal object; it can imagine a degree of mutual affection that shall refine the soul and not expire once it has served as a "ladder to heavenly things"; and, like devotion, make the soul absorb every meaner affection and desire. In each other's arms, as in a temple whose summit is lost in the clouds, the world is to be shut out, and every thought and wish that does not nourish pure affection and permanent virtue. Permanent virtue! Alas, Rousseau, respectable visionary, your paradise would soon be violated by some unexpected guest walking in. Like Milton's, it could only hold angels, or men sunk below the dignity of rational creatures. Happiness is not material; it cannot be seen or handled. Yet the eager pursuit of a good that everyone shapes to his own fancy proclaims man the lord of this lower world, and an intelligent creature who is not to receive happiness but to acquire it. Those who complain of the delusions of passion, therefore, do not recollect that they are exclaiming against a strong proof of the immortality of the soul.

But leaving superior minds to correct themselves and pay dearly for their experience, I must point out that it is not against strong, persevering passions that I want to guard the female heart by exercising the understanding, but against romantic, wavering feelings; for these daydreams of paradise are more often the effect of idleness than of a lively fancy.

Women seldom have enough serious employment to silence their feelings. A round of little cares or vain pursuits frittering away all strength of mind and body, they naturally become objects of sense and nothing more. In short, the whole tenor of female education — the education society gives — tends to make the best-disposed romantic and inconstant, and the rest vain and mean. In the present state of society I am afraid this evil can hardly be remedied in the slightest degree; but should a more praiseworthy ambition ever gain ground, women may be brought nearer to nature and reason, and become more virtuous and useful as they grow more respectable.

But I will venture to assert that their reason will never gain strength enough to regulate their conduct while making an appearance in the world is the first wish of most of humankind. To this weak wish the natural affections and the most useful virtues are sacrificed. Girls marry merely to better themselves, to borrow a significant common phrase, and have such perfect power over their hearts as never to let themselves fall in love until a man with a superior fortune makes an offer. I mean to enlarge on this subject in a later chapter; a hint is enough at present, though women are so often degraded by letting the selfish prudence of age chill the ardour of youth.

From the same source flows the opinion that young girls ought to devote a great part of their time to needlework — an employment that contracts their faculties more than any other that could have been chosen for them, by confining their thoughts to their own persons. Men order their clothes to be made and have done with the subject; women make their own clothes, necessary or ornamental, and talk about them continually, and their thoughts follow their hands. It is not the making of necessities that weakens the mind, but the frippery of dress. When a woman in the lower ranks of life makes her husband's and children's clothes, she does her duty; that is part of her business. But when women work only to dress better than they could otherwise afford, it is worse than a sheer waste of time. To make the poor virtuous they must be employed; and women in the middle rank of life, if they did not ape the fashions of the nobility without catching their ease, might employ them, while they managed their own families, taught their own children, and exercised their own minds. Gardening, experimental science and literature would give them things to think about and matter for conversation that would exercise their understandings to some degree. The conversation of French women, who are not so rigidly nailed to their chairs twisting lappets and knotting ribbons, is often superficial; but I maintain it is not half so insipid as that of those English women whose time is spent making caps and bonnets and all the mischief of trimmings, not to mention shopping and bargain-hunting and the rest. And it is the decent, prudent women who are most degraded by these practices, since their motive is simply vanity. The wanton who exercises her taste to make herself alluring has something more in view.

All these observations branch out of a general one I have made before, and which cannot be insisted on too often: that whether we are speaking of men, of women or of professions, it will be found that the employment of the thoughts shapes the character, generally and individually. Women's thoughts hover forever around their own persons — and is it surprising that their persons are reckoned their most valuable possession? Yet some degree of liberty of mind is necessary even to form the person, and this may be one reason why some gentle wives have so few attractions besides sex. Add to this that sedentary employments make most women sickly, and that false notions of female excellence make them proud of this delicacy, though it is another fetter, and one that cramps the activity of the mind by calling attention continually to the body.

Women of quality seldom do any of the manual part of their dress, so only their taste is exercised; and, thinking less about the finery once the business of the dressing table is over, they acquire an ease that rarely appears in the bearing of women who dress merely for the sake of dressing. In fact the observation about the middle rank, the one where talents thrive best, does not extend to women; for those of the higher class, by catching at least a smattering of literature and talking with men on general topics, acquire more knowledge than the women who ape their fashions and their faults without sharing their advantages. As for virtue, in the widest sense of the word, I have seen most of it in low life. Many poor women maintain their children by the sweat of their brow and hold together families that the vices of the fathers would have scattered; but gentlewomen are too indolent to be actively virtuous, and are softened by civilization rather than refined by it. Indeed the good sense I have met with among poor women who have had few advantages of education, and who have yet acted heroically, has strongly confirmed me in the opinion that trifling employments have made women trifling. Men take the body, and leave the mind to rust; so that while physical love enervates man, being his favourite recreation, he will go on trying to enslave woman. And who can say how many generations may be needed to give vigour to the virtue and talents of the freed descendants of abject slaves?

Footnote: "I take her body," says Ranger.

Footnote: "Supposing that women are voluntary slaves — slavery of any kind is unfavourable to human happiness and improvement." —Knox's Essays

In tracing the causes that have, in my opinion, degraded women, I have confined myself to those that act universally on the morals and manners of the whole sex; and it seems clear to me that they all spring from a want of understanding. Whether that comes from a physical or an accidental weakness of the faculties, time alone can determine. For I shall lay no great weight on the example of a few women who, having received a masculine education, have acquired courage and resolution. I only maintain that the men who have been placed in similar situations have acquired a similar character — I am speaking of whole bodies of men — and that men of genius and talent have emerged from a class in which women have never yet been placed.

Footnote: Sappho, Eloisa, Mrs. Macaulay, the Empress of Russia, Madame d'Eon and the rest. These, and many more, may be counted exceptions; and are not all heroes, as well as heroines, exceptions to general rules? I wish to see women neither heroines nor brutes, but reasonable creatures.

Chapter Five: Criticisms of the Writers Who Have Made Women Objects of Pity, Bordering on Contempt

It remains now to examine the opinions plausibly maintained in certain modern publications on the female character and female education, which have set the tone for most of what is said, more casually, about women.

Section One: Rousseau

I shall begin with Rousseau, and give a sketch of his character of women in his own words, with comments and reflections in between. My comments, it is true, all spring from a few simple principles and might have been deduced from what I have already said; but the artificial structure has been raised with so much ingenuity that it seems necessary to attack it in more detail, and to make the application myself.

Sophia, says Rousseau, should be as perfect a woman as Emilius is a man; and to make her so, we must examine the character nature has given to her sex.

He then proceeds to prove that women ought to be weak and passive because a woman has less bodily strength than a man; from which he infers that she was formed to please him and to be subject to him, and that it is her duty to make herself agreeable to her master, this being the grand end of her existence.

If women were formed only to please men and be subject to them, the conclusion is sound: she ought to sacrifice every other consideration to making herself agreeable to him, and let this brutal instinct of self-preservation be the mainspring of everything she does, once it is proved to be the iron bed of fate, to fit which her character must be stretched or lopped, regardless of every moral or physical distinction. But if, as I think can be shown, the purposes even of this life, taken as a whole, are defeated by practical rules built on so ignoble a base, then I may be allowed to doubt whether woman was created for man. And though the cry of irreligion, or even of atheism, be raised against me, I will simply declare that if an angel from heaven told me that Moses's beautiful poetical cosmogony and his account of the fall of man were literally true, I could not believe what my reason told me was insulting to the character of the Supreme Being. Having no fear of the devil before my eyes, I venture to call this a suggestion of reason, rather than rest my weakness on the broad shoulders of the first seducer of my frail sex.

"Once it is demonstrated," Rousseau continues, "that man and woman are not, and ought not to be, constituted alike in temperament and character, it follows that they should not be educated in the same way. In following the directions of nature they ought certainly to act in concert, but they should not be engaged in the same employments. The end of their pursuits should be the same, but the means they take to accomplish it, and consequently their tastes and inclinations, should be different."

Footnote: Rousseau's Emilius, Volume 3, page 176.

"Girls are fond of dress from their earliest infancy. Not content with being pretty, they want to be thought so; all their little airs show that this thought occupies them, and they are hardly capable of understanding what is said to them before they can be governed by talk of what people will think of their behaviour. The same motive, used indiscreetly with boys, has no such effect: provided they are allowed to pursue their amusements as they please, they care very little what people think of them. It takes time and pains to make boys subject to this motive.

"Wherever girls get this first lesson from, it is a very good one. As the body is born, in a manner, before the soul, our first concern should be to cultivate the body; this order is common to both sexes, but the object of the cultivation differs. In one sex it is the development of bodily powers, in the other of personal charms — not that either strength or beauty ought to belong exclusively to one sex, but that the order of cultivating both is in this respect reversed. Women certainly need as much strength as will let them move and act gracefully, and men as much address as will let them act with ease."

"Children of both sexes have a great many amusements in common, and so they should; do they not have many in common when they are grown up as well? Each sex also has its own particular taste to distinguish it here. Boys love noisy, active games — beating a drum, whipping a top, dragging little carts about; girls, on the other hand, are fonder of things of show and ornament, such as mirrors, trinkets and dolls. The doll is the particular amusement of the female; and from this we see their taste plainly adapted to their destination. The physical part of the art of pleasing lies in dress, and that is all of the art children are capable of cultivating."

"Here, then, we see a primary propensity firmly established, which you need only follow up and regulate. The little creature will doubtless be very eager to know how to dress up her doll, to make its sleeve knots, its flounces, its head dress and so on. She is obliged to have so much recourse to the people about her for help with these things that it would be far more agreeable to her to owe them all to her own industry. Here we have a good reason for the first lessons usually taught to young girls, in which we do not seem to be setting them a task, but obliging them, by teaching them what is immediately useful to themselves. And in fact almost all of them learn to read and write with reluctance, but take very readily to the needle. They imagine themselves already grown up, and think with pleasure that such accomplishments will let them decorate themselves."

This is certainly nothing but an education of the body; but Rousseau is not the only man who has said indirectly that a young woman's person, without any mind at all — unless animal spirits count as one — is very pleasing. To keep it weak, and what some may call beautiful, the understanding is neglected, and girls are made to sit still, play with dolls and listen to foolish conversation; and then the effect of habit is urged on us as an undoubted indication of nature. I know it was Rousseau's opinion that the first years of youth should be spent forming the body, though in educating Emilius he departs from this plan. But the difference between strengthening the body, on which strength of mind largely depends, and merely giving it an easy motion, is a very wide one.

It is worth remarking that Rousseau's observations were made in a country where the art of pleasing had been refined only far enough to strain the coarseness out of vice. He did not go back to nature; or else his ruling appetite disturbed the operations of reason, or he would never have drawn such crude inferences.

In France, boys and girls, and girls particularly, are educated only to please, to manage their persons and regulate their outward behaviour; and their minds are corrupted at a very early age by the worldly and pious cautions they are given to guard them against immodesty. I am speaking of times past. The very confessions that mere children are obliged to make, and the questions the holy men ask — I state these facts on good authority — were enough to stamp a separate character on their sex; and the education society gave them was a school of coquetry and artifice. At ten or eleven, and often much sooner, girls began to flirt, and talked without reproof about establishing themselves in the world by marriage.

In short they were made women almost from birth, and listened to compliments instead of instruction. These weakened the mind — and then nature was supposed to have behaved like a stepmother when she formed this afterthought of creation.

Not allowing women any understanding, it was at least consistent to subject them to an authority independent of reason; and to prepare them for that subjection he gives the following advice:

"Girls ought to be active and diligent; nor is that all. They should also be subjected early to restraint. This misfortune, if it really is one, is inseparable from their sex, and they never throw it off except to suffer crueller evils. They must be subject all their lives to the most constant and severe restraint, which is decorum; so it is necessary to accustom them early to such confinement, that it may not cost them too dear later, and to the suppression of their whims, so that they may submit more readily to the will of others. If they are fond of being always at work, they should sometimes be made to lay it aside. Dissipation, levity and inconstancy are faults that spring up readily from their first propensities when those are corrupted or perverted by too much indulgence. To prevent this abuse we should teach them above all things to lay a due restraint on themselves. The life of a modest woman is reduced by our absurd institutions to a perpetual conflict with herself — though it is only just that this sex should share in the sufferings that arise from the evils it has caused us."

And why is the life of a modest woman a perpetual conflict? I should answer that this very system of education makes it one. Modesty, temperance and self-denial are the sober offspring of reason; but when sensibility is nursed at the expense of the understanding, such weak beings must be restrained by arbitrary means and subjected to continual conflicts. Give their mental activity a wider range, and nobler passions and motives will govern their appetites and sentiments.

"The ordinary attachment and regard of a mother — indeed, mere habit — will make her beloved by her children, if she does nothing to earn their hatred. Even the restraint she puts them under, if it is well directed, will increase their affection instead of lessening it; because, a state of dependence being natural to the sex, they perceive that they were formed for obedience."

This is begging the question; for servitude not only debases the individual, its effects seem to be passed on to posterity. Considering how long women have been dependent, is it surprising that some of them hug their chains and fawn like the spaniel? "These dogs," a naturalist observes, "at first kept their ears erect; but custom has overruled nature, and a token of fear has become a beauty."

"For the same reason," Rousseau adds, "women have, or ought to have, very little liberty; they are apt to indulge themselves excessively in whatever is allowed them. Addicted to extremes in everything, they are even more transported by their amusements than boys are."

The answer to this is very simple. Slaves and mobs have always indulged themselves in the same excesses once they broke loose from authority. A bent bow recoils violently when the hand that was forcibly holding it is suddenly relaxed; and sensibility, the plaything of outward circumstances, must either be subjected to authority or moderated by reason.

"From this habitual restraint," he goes on, "there results a tractableness that women have need of all their lives, since they remain constantly under subjection either to men or to the opinions of humankind, and are never permitted to set themselves above those opinions. The first and most important qualification in a woman is good nature, or sweetness of temper. Formed to obey a being as imperfect as man, often full of vices and always full of faults, she ought to learn early to endure even injustice, and to bear a husband's insults without complaining. It is not for his sake but for her own that she should be of a mild disposition. The perverseness and ill nature of women only aggravate their own misfortunes and their husbands' misconduct; they might easily see that these are not the weapons by which they gain the upper hand."

Formed to live with a being as imperfect as man, women ought to learn from exercising their faculties that forbearance is necessary. But all the sacred rights of humanity are violated by insisting on blind obedience — or else the most sacred rights belong only to man.

The being who patiently endures injustice and silently bears insults will soon become unjust herself, or unable to tell right from wrong. Besides, I deny the fact: this is not the true way to form or improve a temper. As a sex, men have better tempers than women, because they are occupied by pursuits that interest the head as well as the heart, and steadiness in the head gives the heart a healthy temperature. People of sensibility have seldom good tempers. The formation of the temper is the cool work of reason, mixing jarring elements with a happy art as life goes on. I never knew a weak or ignorant person with a good temper, though that constitutional good humour and that docility which fear stamps on the behaviour often go by the name. I say behaviour; for genuine meekness never reached the heart or mind except as the effect of reflection. And many sensible men, who find some of these gentle irritable creatures very troublesome company, will admit that simple restraint produces a great many bad humours in family life.

"Each sex," he argues further, "should preserve its own tone and manner: a meek husband may make a wife impertinent, but mildness of disposition on the woman's side will always bring a man back to reason, at least if he is not an absolute brute, and will sooner or later triumph over him." True — the mildness of reason will. But abject fear always inspires contempt, and tears are only eloquent when they run down fair cheeks.

What can a heart be made of that melts when it is insulted, and, instead of revolting at injustice, kisses the rod? Is it unfair to infer that her virtue is built on narrow views and on selfishness, when she can caress a man with true feminine softness at the very moment he is treating her tyrannically? Nature never dictated such insincerity; and though prudence of this sort is called a virtue, morality becomes a vague thing when any part of it is supposed to rest on falsehood. These are mere expedients, and expedients are only useful for the moment.

Let the husband beware of trusting too implicitly to this servile obedience. For if his wife can caress him with winning sweetness when he is angry — and when she ought to be angry herself, unless contempt has stifled a natural effervescence — she may do the same after parting from a lover. These are all preparations for adultery. Or, if the fear of the world or of hell restrains her desire to please other men when she can no longer please her husband, what substitute is to be found by a being formed by nature and art for no purpose but to please men? What can make up to her for the loss, and where is she to look for a fresh employment? Where is she to find strength of mind enough to decide to begin the search, when her habits are fixed and vanity has long ruled her chaotic mind?

But this partial moralist recommends cunning systematically and plausibly.

"Daughters should always be submissive; their mothers, however, should not be inexorable. To make a young person tractable she must not be made unhappy; to make her modest she must not be made stupid. On the contrary, I should not be displeased at her being permitted to use a little art — not to escape punishment for disobedience, but to exempt herself from the necessity of obeying. It is not necessary to make her dependence burdensome, only to let her feel it. Subtlety is a talent natural to the sex; and as I am persuaded that all our natural inclinations are right and good in themselves, I think this one should be cultivated like the rest. We need only prevent its abuse."

"Whatever is, is right," he then proceeds triumphantly to infer. Granted — yet perhaps no aphorism ever contained a more paradoxical assertion. It is a solemn truth with respect to God. He, I speak reverently, sees the whole at once, and saw its just proportions in the womb of time. But man, who can only inspect disjointed parts, finds a great many things wrong; and it is part of the system, and therefore right, that he should try to alter what looks wrong to him, even while he bows to the wisdom of his Creator and respects the darkness he is labouring to disperse.

The inference that follows is sound, supposing the principle to be sound: "The superior address peculiar to the female sex is a very equitable compensation for their inferiority in strength. Without it woman would not be man's companion but his slave; it is by her superior art and ingenuity that she preserves her equality and governs him while she pretends to obey. Woman has everything against her — our faults as well as her own timidity and weakness. She has nothing in her favour but her subtlety and her beauty. Is it not very reasonable, then, that she should cultivate both?" Greatness of mind can never live with cunning, or with address — for I shall not quibble about words when their plain meaning is insincerity and falsehood. I shall content myself with observing that if any class of humankind is so created that it must necessarily be educated by rules not strictly deducible from truth, then virtue is a matter of convention. How could Rousseau dare to assert, after giving this advice, that the object of both sexes in the grand end of existence should be the same, when he knew perfectly well that the mind is formed by its pursuits, and either expands as great views swallow up little ones, or becomes little itself?

Men have superior strength of body; but if it were not for mistaken notions of beauty, women would acquire enough of it to earn their own living, which is the true definition of independence, and to bear the bodily inconveniences and exertions that are needed to strengthen the mind.

Let us, then, by being allowed the same exercise as boys, not only in infancy but in youth, arrive at bodily perfection, so that we may know how far the natural superiority of man really extends. For what reason or virtue can be expected from a creature whose seedtime of life has been neglected? None — except that the winds of heaven happen to scatter a good many useful seeds on the fallow ground.

"Beauty cannot be acquired by dress, and coquetry is an art not so quickly or so early learned. While girls are still young, however, they are quite capable of studying an agreeable gesture, a pleasing modulation of voice, an easy carriage and bearing, and of learning to adapt their looks and attitudes gracefully to the time, the place and the occasion. Their application, therefore, should not be confined solely to the arts of industry and the needle, when they come to display other talents whose usefulness is already apparent."

"For my part, I would have a young Englishwoman cultivate her agreeable talents in order to please her future husband with as much care and assiduity as a young Circassian cultivates hers to fit her for the harem of an eastern pasha."

To make women completely insignificant, he adds: "The tongues of women are very voluble; they speak earlier, more readily and more agreeably than men, and they are accused of speaking a great deal more. So it ought to be, and I should be very ready to turn the reproach into a compliment. Their lips and their eyes have the same activity, and for the same reason. A man speaks of what he knows, a woman of what pleases her; the one requires knowledge, the other taste. The principal object of a man's talk should be what is useful, of a woman's what is agreeable. There ought to be nothing in common between their two kinds of conversation but truth."

"We ought not, therefore, to check the prattle of girls as we check that of boys, with the severe question 'What are you talking for?', but with another that is no less hard to answer: 'How will what you say be received?' In infancy, while they are still incapable of telling good from evil, they ought to observe it as a law never to say anything disagreeable to the person they are speaking to; and what will make this rule harder still to keep is that it must always be subordinate to the earlier one, of never speaking falsely or telling an untruth." To govern the tongue in that fashion must require great address indeed; and both men and women practise it too much. Out of the abundance of the heart, how few speak! So few that I, who love simplicity, would gladly give up politeness for a quarter of the virtue that has been sacrificed to an equivocal quality which at best should be nothing more than the polish on virtue.

But to finish the sketch. "It is easy to conceive that if male children are in no condition to form any true notions of religion, such ideas must be far beyond the conception of girls. That is the very reason why I would begin to speak to them earlier on the subject; for if we waited until they were capable of discussing such profound questions methodically, we should run the risk of never speaking to them about it as long as they lived. Reason in women is a practical reason, which makes them skilful at discovering the means to a known end, but would never enable them to discover the end itself. The social relations of the sexes are truly admirable: from their union there results a moral person of which woman may be called the eyes and man the hand, with this dependence on each other, that it is from the man that the woman learns what she is to see, and from the woman that the man learns what he ought to do. If woman could go back to first principles as well as man, and man could enter into details as well as woman, each being always independent of the other, they would live in perpetual discord and their union could not last. But in the harmony that naturally subsists between them, their different faculties tend to one common end. It is hard to say which of them contributes most to it: each follows the other's impulse; each obeys, and both are masters."

"Since a woman's conduct is subservient to public opinion, her faith in matters of religion should for that very reason be subject to authority. 'Every daughter ought to be of her mother's religion, and every wife of her husband's; for even if that religion were false, the docility which leads the mother and daughter to submit to the order of nature takes away, in the sight of God, the criminality of their error.' As they are in no condition to judge for themselves, they ought to abide by the decision of their fathers and their husbands as confidently as by that of the church."

Footnote: And what is the consequence if the mother's opinion and the husband's happen to disagree? An ignorant person cannot be reasoned out of an error, and when she is persuaded to give up one prejudice for another, her mind is unsettled. Indeed the husband may have no religion at all to teach her, though in such a situation she will greatly need some support for her virtue that is independent of worldly considerations.

"Since authority ought to regulate women's religion, it is not so necessary to explain the reasons for their belief as to lay down precisely what they are to believe. For a creed that offers the mind nothing but obscure ideas is the source of fanaticism, and one that offers absurdities leads to unbelief."

Absolute, unquestioned authority, it seems, must exist somewhere. But is this not a direct and exclusive appropriation of reason? The rights of humanity have been confined in this way to the male line from Adam downwards. Rousseau would carry his male aristocracy further still, for he hints that he would not blame those who argue for leaving woman in the profoundest possible ignorance, if it were not necessary — in order to preserve her chastity and justify a man's choice in the eyes of the world — to give her a little knowledge of men and of the customs human passions produce; otherwise she might breed at home without being made any less voluptuous and innocent by the exercise of her understanding. Except, of course, during the first year of marriage, when she might employ that understanding on dress, as Sophia does. "Her dress is extremely modest in appearance and yet very coquettish in fact. She does not display her charms, she conceals them; but in concealing them she knows how to work on your imagination. Everyone who sees her will say: there is a modest, discreet girl. But while you are near her, your eyes and affections wander all over her person and you cannot draw them away; and you would conclude that every part of her dress, simple as it looks, was put in order only to be taken to pieces by the imagination." Is this modesty? Is this a preparation for immortality? And again: what opinion are we to form of a system of education when its author says of his heroine "that with her, doing things well is only a secondary concern; her principal concern is to do them neatly"?

Secondary indeed are all her virtues and qualities. On religion, he has her parents address her — accustomed as she is to submission — with: "Your husband will instruct you in good time."

Having cramped a woman's mind in this way, if he has not made it a complete blank in order to keep it fair, he then advises her to reflect, so that a reflecting man may not yawn in her company once he is tired of caressing her. What has she to reflect about, who must obey? And would it not be a refinement of cruelty merely to open her mind so as to make the darkness and misery of her fate visible? Yet these are his sensible remarks; how consistent they are with what I have already had to quote, in order to give a fair view of the subject, the reader may decide.

"Those who spend their whole lives working for their daily bread have no ideas beyond their business or their interest, and all their understanding seems to lie in their fingers' ends. This ignorance is prejudicial neither to their integrity nor to their morals; it is often of service to them. Sometimes, by means of reflection, we are led to compound with our duty and end by substituting a jargon of words for things. Our own conscience is the most enlightened philosopher. A man need not be acquainted with Cicero's Offices to be a man of probity; and perhaps the most virtuous woman in the world is the least acquainted with any definition of virtue. But it is no less true that only an improved understanding can make society agreeable; and it is a melancholy thing for the father of a family who is fond of home to be obliged to be always wrapped up in himself, with nobody about him to whom he can communicate his thoughts.

"Besides, how should a woman without reflection be capable of educating her children? How should she discern what is proper for them? How should she incline them to virtues she does not know, or to a merit she has no idea of? She can only soothe them or scold them, make them insolent or timid; she will turn them into formal coxcombs or ignorant blockheads, but she will never make them sensible or amiable." How indeed should she, when her husband is not always at hand to lend her his reason — when the two of them together make only one moral being? A blind will, "eyes without hands", would go a very little way. And perhaps his abstract reason, which is supposed to concentrate the scattered beams of her practical reason, may be occupied in judging the flavour of a wine, or holding forth on the right sauces for turtle; or, more profoundly intent at a card table, he may be generalizing his ideas as he bets away his fortune, leaving all the details of education to his helpmate or to chance.

But granting that woman ought to be beautiful, innocent and silly in order to make her a more alluring and indulgent companion — what is her understanding being sacrificed for? And why is all this preparation necessary only, by Rousseau's own account, to make her her husband's mistress for a very short time? For no man ever insisted more on the transient nature of love. Hear the philosopher: "Sensual pleasures are transient. The habitual state of the affections always loses by their gratification. The imagination, which decks out the object of our desires, is lost in fruition. Except for the Supreme Being, who is self-existent, there is nothing beautiful but what is ideal."

But he returns to his unintelligible paradoxes when he addresses Sophia in these terms: "Emilius, in becoming your husband, has become your master and claims your obedience. Such is the order of nature. When a man is married, however, to such a wife as Sophia, it is proper that he should be directed by her: this too agrees with the order of nature. It is therefore in order to give you as much authority over his heart as his sex gives him over your person that I have made you the arbiter of his pleasures. It may cost you some disagreeable self-denial; but you will be certain of keeping your empire over him if you can keep it over yourself; and what I have already observed shows me that this difficult attempt is not beyond your courage.

"Would you have your husband constantly at your feet? Then keep him at some distance from your person. You will long maintain the authority of love if you know how to make your favours rare and valuable. This is how you may employ even the arts of coquetry in the service of virtue, and those of love in the service of reason."

I shall close my extracts with an accurate description of a comfortable couple. "And yet you must not imagine that even such management will always be enough. Whatever precautions are taken, enjoyment will by degrees take the edge off passion. But when love has lasted as long as it can, a pleasing habit takes its place, and the attachment of a mutual confidence succeeds the transports of passion. Children often form a more agreeable and permanent connection between married people than even love itself. When you cease to be the mistress of Emilius, you will go on being his wife and his friend; you will be the mother of his children."

Footnote: Rousseau's Emilius.

Children, he truly observes, form a much more permanent connection between married people than love does. Beauty, he declares, will not be valued, or even noticed, after a couple have lived six months together, and artificial graces and coquetry will pall on the senses just as surely. Why, then, does he say that a girl should be educated for her husband with the same care as for an eastern harem?

I now appeal from the daydreams of fancy and refined licentiousness to the good sense of humankind. If the object of education is to prepare women to become chaste wives and sensible mothers, is the method so plausibly recommended in that sketch really the one best calculated to produce those ends? Will anyone allow that the surest way to make a wife chaste is to teach her to practise the wanton arts of a mistress — called virtuous coquetry by the sensualist who can no longer relish the artless charms of sincerity, or taste the pleasure that arises from a tender intimacy, where confidence is unchecked by suspicion and made interesting by sense?

The man who can be content to live with a pretty, useful companion who has no mind has lost, among his voluptuous gratifications, the taste for more refined enjoyments. He has never felt the calm satisfaction that refreshes a parched heart like the silent dew of heaven — the satisfaction of being loved by someone who could understand him. In his wife's company he is still alone, except when the man has sunk into the brute. "The charm of life," says a grave philosophical reasoner, "is sympathy; nothing pleases us more than to observe in other men a fellow feeling with all the emotions of our own breast."

But according to the reasoning that keeps women away from the tree of knowledge, the important years of youth, the usefulness of age and the rational hopes of a future life are all to be sacrificed in order to make a woman an object of desire for a short time. Besides, how could Rousseau expect women to be virtuous and constant when reason is not allowed to be the foundation of their virtue, nor truth the object of their enquiries?

But all Rousseau's errors of reasoning arose out of sensibility — and women are very ready to forgive a sensibility to their own charms. Where he should have reasoned he became impassioned, and reflection inflamed his imagination instead of enlightening his understanding. Even his virtues led him further astray; for, born with a warm constitution and a lively fancy, nature carried him towards the other sex with such eager fondness that he soon became lascivious. If he had given way to those desires the fire would have gone out naturally; but virtue, and a romantic kind of delicacy, made him practise self-denial. And then, when fear or delicacy or virtue held him back, he debauched his imagination instead; and, brooding on sensations that fancy had given force to, he traced them in the most glowing colours and sank them deep into his soul.

So he sought solitude — not to sleep beside the man of nature, or to investigate the causes of things calmly in the shade where Sir Isaac Newton indulged his contemplations, but merely to indulge his feelings. And he has painted what he felt so strongly, and with such warmth, that his readers' hearts are engaged and their imaginations inflamed; and, in proportion to the strength of their fancy, they imagine that their understanding has been convinced, when all they have done is sympathize with a poetic writer who skilfully exhibits the objects of sense, most voluptuously shadowed or gracefully veiled. And so, making us feel while we dream that we are reasoning, he leaves erroneous conclusions in our minds.

Why was Rousseau's life divided between ecstasy and misery? What other answer can be given than that the effervescence of his imagination produced both — and that if his fancy had been allowed to cool he might possibly have acquired more strength of mind? Still, if the purpose of life is to educate the intellectual part of us, then everything about him was right. Yet if death did not lead on to a nobler scene of action, it is likely he would have enjoyed a steadier happiness on earth, and felt the calm sensations of the man of nature, instead of being prepared for another stage of existence by nourishing the passions that agitate civilized man.

But peace to his spirit! I make war on his opinions, not his ashes. I make war only on the sensibility that led him to degrade woman by making her the slave of love.

	… "Curs'd vassalage,
	First idoliz'd till love's hot fire be o'er,
	Then slaves to those who courted us before."
	—Dryden

The pernicious tendency of those books in which the writers insidiously degrade women while prostrating themselves before their personal charms cannot be exposed too often or too severely.

Let us rise above such narrow prejudices, my dear contemporaries! If wisdom is desirable for its own sake, if virtue must be founded on knowledge to deserve the name, then let us try to strengthen our minds by reflection until our heads make a counterweight for our hearts. Let us not confine all our thoughts to the petty occurrences of the day, nor all our knowledge to an acquaintance with our lovers' or our husbands' hearts; but let every duty we practise be subordinate to the grand duty of improving our minds and preparing our affections for a more exalted state.

Beware, then, my friends, of letting your hearts be moved by every trivial incident. The reed is shaken by a breeze and dies every year, but the oak stands firm and braves the storm for centuries.

If we really were created only to flutter out our hour and die, then let us indulge our sensibility by all means, and laugh at the severity of reason. Yet even then, alas, we should want strength of body and mind, and life would be lost in feverish pleasures or wearisome languor.

But the system of education I so earnestly wish to see exploded seems to take for granted what ought never to be taken for granted: that virtue shields us from the accidents of life, and that fortune, slipping off her blindfold, will smile on a well-educated girl and bring an Emilius or a Telemachus along in her hand. Whereas in fact the reward virtue promises her followers is plainly confined to their own hearts; and often they must contend with the most vexatious worldly cares and put up with the vices and moods of relations they can never feel any friendship for.

There have been many women in the world who, instead of being supported by the reason and virtue of their fathers and brothers, have strengthened their own minds by struggling against those men's vices and follies — and who have never met a hero in the shape of a husband to pay them the debt humankind owed them and perhaps restore their reason to its natural dependent state, handing back to man the usurped prerogative of rising above opinion.

Section Two: Dr. Fordyce's Sermons

Dr. Fordyce's sermons have long formed part of a young woman's library; girls at school are even allowed to read them. But I should dismiss them instantly from my own pupil's shelf if I wanted to strengthen her understanding by leading her to form sound principles on a broad basis — or even if I were only anxious to cultivate her taste, though the sermons must be allowed to contain a good many sensible observations.

Dr. Fordyce may have had a very praiseworthy end in view; but these discourses are written in such an affected style that on that account alone, and even if I had nothing to object to in his mellifluous precepts, I should not let girls read them, unless I intended to hunt every spark of nature out of their composition and melt every human quality down into female weakness and artificial grace. I say artificial, because true grace arises from some kind of independence of mind.

Children, careless of pleasing anyone and anxious only to amuse themselves, are often very graceful; and the nobility, who have mostly lived among inferiors and always had money at command, acquire a graceful ease of bearing which should rather be called a habitual grace of body than that higher gracefulness which is really an expression of the mind. This mental grace, unnoticed by vulgar eyes, often flashes across a rough face and lights up every feature, showing simplicity and independence of mind. Then we read characters of immortality in the eye and see the soul in every gesture, though at rest neither the face nor the limbs may have much beauty to recommend them, nor the manner anything remarkable enough to attract general attention. Most people, however, look for something more tangible in the way of beauty; yet simplicity is generally admired when people do not stop to consider what they are admiring — and can there be simplicity without sincerity? But enough of these remarks, which are somewhat rambling, though the subject naturally called them up.

In declamatory periods Dr. Fordyce spins out Rousseau's eloquence, and in the most sentimental rant sets out his opinions about the female character and the behaviour a woman should adopt in order to make herself lovely.

He shall speak for himself, for this is how he makes nature address man: "Look at these smiling innocents, whom I have graced with my fairest gifts and committed to your protection; look on them with love and respect; treat them with tenderness and honour. They are timid and need to be defended. They are frail; oh, do not take advantage of their weakness! Let their fears and their blushes endear them to you. Let their confidence in you never be abused. But is it possible that any of you could be such barbarians, so supremely wicked, as to abuse it? Can you find it in your hearts to despoil these gentle, trusting creatures of their treasure, or to do anything to strip them of their native robe of virtue? Cursed be the impious hand that would dare to violate the unblemished form of Chastity! You wretch! You ruffian! Forbear, and do not venture to provoke heaven's fiercest vengeance." I know of no comment that could be made seriously on this curious passage, and I could produce a great many like it — some of them so very sentimental that I have heard rational men use the word indecent when they mentioned them with disgust.

Footnote: Can you? Can you? would be the most emphatic comment, if it were drawled out in a whining voice.

Throughout there is a display of cold, artificial feeling, and that parade of sensibility which boys and girls alike should be taught to despise as the sure mark of a small vain mind. Florid appeals are made to heaven, and to the beauteous innocents who are heaven's fairest images here below, while sober sense is left far behind. This is not the language of the heart, and it will never reach the heart, though it may tickle the ear.

I shall be told, perhaps, that the public have been pleased with these volumes. True — and Hervey's Meditations are still read, though he sinned equally against sense and taste.

I particularly object to the lover-like phrases of pumped-up passion scattered everywhere through them. If women are ever to be allowed to walk without leading-strings, why must they be coaxed into virtue by artful flattery and compliments on their sex? Speak to them in the language of truth and soberness, and away with the lullaby strains of condescending endearment. Let them be taught to respect themselves as rational creatures, instead of being led into a passion for their own insipid persons. It stirs my bile to hear a preacher holding forth on dress and needlework, and still more to hear him address the "British fair, the fairest of the fair", as if they had nothing but feelings.

Even when he is recommending piety he uses this argument: "Never, perhaps, does a fine woman strike more deeply than when, composed into pious recollection and possessed by the noblest considerations, she assumes, without knowing it, a superior dignity and new graces, so that the beauties of holiness seem to radiate about her and the bystanders are almost induced to fancy her already worshipping among her kindred angels." Why are women to be brought up like this with a desire for conquest? The very word, used in that sense, gives me a sickly qualm. Do religion and virtue offer no stronger motives, no brighter reward? Must women always be debased by being made to think about the sex of their companions? Must they always be taught to be pleasing? And when they level their small artillery at the heart of man, is it necessary to tell them that a little sense is enough to make their attention incredibly soothing? "As a small degree of knowledge entertains in a woman, so from a woman, though for a different reason, a small expression of kindness delights, particularly if she is beautiful." I should have supposed it was for the same reason.

Why are girls told that they resemble angels, when the effect is to sink them below women? Or that a gentle, innocent woman is the object that comes nearest to our idea of an angel? And yet they are told at the same time that they are only like angels while they are young and beautiful — so that it is their persons, not their virtues, that earn them this homage.

Idle, empty words! What can such delusive flattery lead to but vanity and folly? A lover, it is true, has a poetic licence to exalt his mistress; his reason is the bubble of his passion, and he tells no lie when he borrows the language of adoration. His imagination may raise the idol of his heart above humanity and go unblamed; and it would be a happy thing for women if they were only flattered by the men who loved them — I mean, who love the individual and not the sex. But should a grave preacher lard his discourses with such fooleries?

In sermons or in novels, however, voluptuousness is always true to its text. Moralists allow men to cultivate different qualities, as nature directs, and to take on the different characters that the same passions, modified almost infinitely, give to each individual. A virtuous man may have a choleric or a sanguine constitution, may be gay or grave without reproach; he may be so firm as to be nearly overbearing, or so weakly submissive as to have no will or opinion of his own. But all women are to be levelled by meekness and docility into a single character of yielding softness and gentle compliance.

I will use the preacher's own words. "Let it be observed that in your sex manly exercises are never graceful; that in a woman a masculine tone and figure, and a masculine air and bearing, are always forbidding; and that men of sensibility desire soft features and a flowing voice in every woman, a form that is not robust, and a delicate and gentle manner."

Is the following portrait not the portrait of a house slave? "I am astonished at the folly of many women who are still reproaching their husbands for leaving them alone, for preferring this or that company to theirs, for treating them with this or the other mark of disregard or indifference — when, to tell the truth, they have themselves largely to blame. Not that I would justify the men in anything wrong on their side. But if you had behaved to them with more respectful observance and a more equal tenderness; studying their humours, overlooking their mistakes, submitting to their opinions in matters of indifference, passing over little instances of unevenness, caprice or temper, giving soft answers to hasty words, complaining as seldom as possible, and making it your daily care to relieve their anxieties and anticipate their wishes, to enliven the dull hour and call up ideas of happiness — if you had pursued this conduct, I do not doubt that you would have kept and even increased their esteem, so far as to secure every degree of influence that could conduce to their virtue or to your mutual satisfaction; and your house might this day have been the home of domestic bliss." Such a woman ought to be an angel — or else she is an ass; for I can discern no trace of human character in this domestic drudge, neither reason nor passion, whose whole being is absorbed into a tyrant's.

Still, Dr. Fordyce must have very little acquaintance with the human heart if he really supposed such conduct would bring back a wandering love, instead of exciting contempt. No: beauty and gentleness and the rest may win a heart, but esteem, the only lasting affection, can only be got by virtue supported by reason. It is respect for the understanding that keeps tenderness for the person alive.

Since these volumes are so often put into the hands of young people, I have taken more notice of them than, strictly speaking, they deserve; but as they have helped to spoil the taste and weaken the understanding of so many of my fellow creatures, I could not pass over them in silence.

Section Three: Dr. Gregory's Legacy to His Daughters

Such fatherly concern runs through Dr. Gregory's Legacy to His Daughters that I begin the work of criticism with affectionate respect. But since this little volume has many attractions to recommend it to the most respectable part of my sex, I cannot silently pass over arguments that so plausibly support opinions which I believe have had the most damaging effect on the morals and manners of the female world.

His easy, familiar style is particularly suited to the tenor of his advice, and the melancholy tenderness that his respect for the memory of a beloved wife spreads through the whole work makes it very affecting. Yet there is a degree of concise elegance visible in many passages that disturbs this sympathy, and we come upon the author when we only expected to meet the father.

Besides, having two objects in view, he seldom held steadily to either. Wishing to make his daughters amiable, and afraid that unhappiness would be the only consequence of instilling sentiments that might draw them out of the track of common life without enabling them to act with a matching independence and dignity, he checks the natural flow of his thoughts and advises neither one thing nor the other.

In the preface he tells them a mournful truth: "that they will hear, at least once in their lives, the genuine sentiments of a man who has no interest in deceiving them."

Hapless woman! What can be expected of you, when the beings you are said naturally to depend on for reason and support all have an interest in deceiving you? This is the root of the evil that has cast a corroding mildew over all your virtues, and, blighting your opening faculties in the bud, has made you the weak thing you are. It is this separate interest, this insidious state of warfare, that undermines morality and divides humankind.

If love has made some women wretched, how many more has the cold, meaningless intercourse of gallantry made vain and useless? Yet this heartless attention to women is reckoned so manly, so polite, that until society is very differently organized I am afraid this relic of gothic manners will not be replaced by a more reasonable and affectionate way of behaving. Besides, to strip it of its imaginary dignity, I must observe that in the most civilized European states this lip service prevails to a very great degree, accompanied by an extreme dissoluteness of morals. In Portugal, the country I have particularly in mind, it takes precedence of the most serious moral obligations: a man is seldom assassinated while he is in a woman's company. The savage hand of robbery is unnerved by this chivalrous spirit; and if the blow of vengeance cannot be stayed, the lady is begged to pardon the rudeness and depart in peace, though sprinkled, perhaps, with her husband's or her brother's blood.

I shall pass over his remarks on religion, because I mean to discuss that subject in a separate chapter.

His remarks about behaviour, though many of them are very sensible, I entirely disapprove of, because it seems to me to be beginning at the wrong end. A cultivated understanding and an affectionate heart will never need starched rules of decorum; something more substantial than seemliness will be the result. And without understanding, the behaviour he recommends would be rank affectation. Decorum, indeed, is the one thing needful! Decorum is to supplant nature and banish all simplicity and variety of character from the female world. Yet what good can all this superficial counsel do? It is, however, much easier to prescribe this or that mode of behaviour than to set the reason to work; and when the mind has been stocked with useful knowledge and strengthened by being employed, the regulation of the behaviour may safely be left to its guidance.

Why, for instance, should the following caution be given, when art of every kind must contaminate the mind? Why entangle the grand motives of action, which reason and religion combine to enforce, with pitiful worldly shifts and sleight-of-hand tricks to gain the applause of gaping, tasteless fools? "Be even cautious in displaying your good sense. It will be thought that you are assuming a superiority over the rest of the company. But if you happen to have any learning, keep it a profound secret, especially from the men, who generally look with a jealous and malignant eye on a woman of great parts and a cultivated understanding." If men of real merit are above this meanness, as he afterwards observes, where is the necessity for the behaviour of a whole sex to be modulated to please fools, or to please men who, having little claim to respect as individuals, choose to keep close together in their phalanx? Men who insist on the superiority they share with all men, having no other, are certainly very excusable.

Footnote: Let women once acquire good sense, and, if it deserves the name, it will teach them how to employ it — or else of what use is it?

There would be no end to rules of behaviour if it were proper always to take the tone of the company; for by changing key forever in that way, a flat would often pass for a natural note.

Surely it would have been wiser to advise women to improve themselves until they rose above the fumes of vanity, and then let public opinion come round. For where are rules of accommodation to stop? The narrow path of truth and virtue inclines neither to the right nor to the left; it is a straightforward business, and those who are earnestly pursuing their road may leap over a good many decorous prejudices without leaving modesty behind. Make the heart clean and give the head employment, and I will venture to predict that there will be nothing offensive in the behaviour.
