The air of fashion that so many young people are eager to acquire always strikes me like the studied attitudes in certain modern prints, copied with tasteless servility from the antique: the soul is left out, and none of the parts are tied together by anything that could properly be called character. This varnish of fashion, which seldom sticks very close to sense, may dazzle the weak; but leave nature to itself and it will seldom disgust the wise. Besides, when a woman has sense enough not to pretend to anything she does not understand to some degree, there is no need for any resolution to hide her talents under a bushel. Let things take their natural course and all will be well.

It is this system of dissimulation, running through the whole volume, that I despise. Women are always to seem to be this or that — and yet virtue might address them in Hamlet's words: Seems! I know not seems! I have that within which passes show!

The same note keeps sounding. In another place, after recommending delicacy without sufficiently distinguishing what he means by it, he adds: "The men will complain of your reserve. They will assure you that a franker behaviour would make you more amiable. But trust me, they are not sincere when they tell you so. I acknowledge that on some occasions it might make you more agreeable as companions, but it would make you less amiable as women — an important distinction that many of your sex are not aware of."

This desire to be always women is the very consciousness that degrades the sex. Except with a lover, I must repeat an earlier observation with emphasis: it would be well if women were only agreeable or rational companions. But in this respect his advice is even inconsistent with a passage I mean to quote with the most marked approval.

"The idea that a woman may allow all innocent freedoms provided her virtue is secure is both grossly indelicate and dangerous, and has proved fatal to many of your sex." With this opinion I entirely agree. Any man or woman of feeling must always wish to convince the person they love that it is the caresses of the individual, not of the sex, that are received and returned with pleasure, and that the heart is moved rather than the senses. Without this natural delicacy, love becomes a selfish personal gratification that soon degrades the character.

I carry the sentiment further still. Affection, where love is out of the question, authorises many personal endearments which, flowing naturally from an innocent heart, give life to behaviour; but the personal familiarity of appetite, gallantry or vanity is contemptible. When a man squeezes the hand of a pretty woman he has never seen before as he hands her into a carriage, she will regard the impertinent liberty as an insult, if she has any true delicacy, instead of being flattered by this meaningless homage to beauty. These are the privileges of friendship, or the momentary homage the heart pays to virtue when it flashes suddenly into view. Mere animal spirits have no claim on the kindnesses of affection.

Wishing to feed the affections with what is now the food of vanity, I would gladly persuade my sex to act on simpler principles. Let them deserve love, and they will get it, even if nobody ever tells them that "the power of a fine woman over the hearts of men, and of men of the finest parts, is even beyond what she imagines."

I have already noticed his narrow cautions about duplicity, female softness and delicacy of constitution; these are the changes he rings without ceasing, more decorously than Rousseau, it is true, but it all comes home to the same point. Anyone who takes the trouble to analyse these sentiments will find the first principles not quite so delicate as the structure raised on them.

The subject of amusements is treated too cursorily, and in the same spirit.

When I come to friendship, love and marriage it will be found that we differ materially in opinion. I shall not anticipate here what I have to say on those important subjects, but confine my remarks to the general tenor of his — to that cautious family prudence, and those confined views of a partial, unenlightened affection, which shut out pleasure and improvement in the vain hope of warding off sorrow and error, and which, in guarding the heart and mind so carefully, destroy all their energy as well. It is far better to be often deceived than never to trust; to be disappointed in love than never to love; to lose a husband's fondness than to forfeit his esteem.

How happy it would be for the world, and for individuals too, if all this useless anxiety to attain worldly happiness on a confined plan were turned into an anxious desire to improve the understanding. "Wisdom is the principal thing: therefore get wisdom; and with all your getting, get understanding." "How long, you simple ones, will you love simplicity, and hate knowledge?" says Wisdom to the daughters of men.

Section Four: Against the Prerogative of Man

I do not mean to go through all the writers who have written on the manners of women; that would only be going over the old ground, for they have generally written in the same strain. But in attacking the boasted prerogative of man — the prerogative that may emphatically be called the iron sceptre of tyranny, the original sin of tyrants — I declare against all power built on prejudice, however venerable with age.

If the submission demanded is founded on justice, there is no appealing to a higher power, for God is justice itself. Let us, then, as children of the same parent, and not bastards merely because we were born later, reason together, and learn to submit to the authority of reason when her voice is distinctly heard. But if it can be shown that this throne of prerogative rests on nothing but a chaotic mass of prejudices with no inherent principle of order to hold them together — or on an elephant, or a tortoise, or even the mighty shoulders of a son of the earth — then those who dare to brave the consequences may escape, without any breach of duty and without sinning against the order of things.

While reason raises man above the brutal herd, and death is big with promises, only those are subject to blind authority who have no reliance on their own strength. "They are free who will be free!"

Footnote: "He is the free man, whom truth makes free!" —Cowper

The being who can govern itself has nothing to fear in life; but if anything is dearer to it than its own self-respect, the price must be paid to the last farthing. Virtue, like everything else that is valuable, must be loved for herself alone, or she will not come and live with us. She will not give us that peace "which passes understanding" when she is used merely as the stilts of a reputation and respected with pharisaical exactness because honesty is the best policy.

That the plan of life which lets us carry some knowledge and virtue into another world is also the one best calculated to give us contentment in this one cannot be denied. Yet few people act on the principle, though everyone allows that it is beyond dispute. Present pleasure, or present power, carries these sober convictions before it, and man bargains with happiness for the day and not for a lifetime. How few — how very few — have foresight or resolution enough to endure a small evil now in order to avoid a greater one later.

Woman in particular, whose virtue is built on mutual prejudices, seldom attains this greatness of mind. So, becoming the slave of her own feelings, she is easily subdued by other people's. Degraded in this way, her reason — her misty reason — is employed in polishing her chains rather than in snapping them.

Footnote: I mean to use a word that takes in more than chastity, the virtue that is supposed to belong to one sex.

I have listened with indignation to women arguing in the same track as men, and adopting with all the pertinacity of ignorance the very sentiments that brutalize them.

I must illustrate my point with a few examples. Mrs. Piozzi, who often repeated by rote what she did not understand, comes forward in Johnsonian periods.

"Do not look for happiness in singularity, and dread a refinement of wisdom as a deviation into folly." So she dogmatically addresses a newly married man; and to explain this pompous opening she adds: "I said that your lady's person would not grow more pleasing to you, but pray let her never suspect that it grows less so. That a woman will pardon an affront to her understanding much sooner than one to her person is well known, and none of us will contradict it. All our attainments, all our arts, are employed to gain and keep the heart of man; and what mortification can exceed the disappointment if the end is not obtained? There is no reproof, however pointed, no punishment, however severe, that a woman of spirit will not prefer to neglect; and if she can endure it without complaining, that only proves that she means to make it up to herself by other men's attention for her husband's slights."

These are truly masculine sentiments. "All our arts are employed to gain and keep the heart of man" — and what is the inference? That if her person is neglected, and was there ever a person, even one formed with the symmetry of a Medici Venus, that was not slighted in the end, she will make it up to herself by trying to please other men. Noble morality! And so the understanding of a whole sex is insulted and their virtue is deprived of the common basis of all virtue. A woman must know that her person cannot be as pleasing to her husband as it was to her lover; and if she is offended with him for being a human creature, she may as well whine about the loss of his heart as about any other foolish thing. This very want of discernment, or unreasonable anger, proves that he could not have changed his fondness for her person into an affection for her virtues or a respect for her understanding.

While women avow such opinions and act on them, their understandings at least deserve the contempt and abuse that men who never insult their persons have pointedly aimed at the female mind. And it is the sentiments of these polite men, who do not wish to be encumbered with a mind, that vain women thoughtlessly adopt. Yet they ought to know that only an insulted reason can spread that sacred reserve about the person which makes human affections — for human affections always have some base alloy in them — as permanent as is consistent with the grand end of existence, the attainment of virtue.

The Baroness de Staël speaks the same language as the lady just quoted, with more enthusiasm. Her eulogy on Rousseau happened to fall into my hands, and her sentiments, which are the sentiments of too many of my sex, may serve as a text for a few comments. "Though Rousseau," she observes, "has tried to prevent women from interfering in public affairs and playing a brilliant part in the theatre of politics, yet how much he has done, in speaking of them, to satisfy them! If he wished to deprive them of some rights foreign to their sex, how completely has he restored to them all those they have a claim to! And in trying to diminish their influence over the deliberations of men, how sacredly has he established the empire they hold over men's happiness! In helping them down from a usurped throne, he has seated them firmly on the one nature destined them for; and though he is full of indignation against them when they try to resemble men, yet when they come before him with all the charms, weaknesses, virtues and errors of their sex, his respect for their persons amounts almost to adoration." True! For there never was a sensualist who paid more fervent adoration at the shrine of beauty. So devout, indeed, was his respect for the person that, chastity excepted for obvious reasons, he only wished to see it embellished by charms, weaknesses and errors. He was afraid that the austerity of reason would disturb the soft playfulness of love. The master wanted a meretricious slave to fondle, entirely dependent on his reason and his bounty; he did not want a companion he would be obliged to esteem, or a friend he could entrust with the education of his children if death took their father before he had finished the sacred task. He denies woman reason, shuts her out from knowledge and turns her away from truth — and yet he is pardoned, because "he admits the passion of love." It would take some ingenuity to show why women should be under such an obligation to him for admitting love, when it is clear that he admits it only for the relaxation of men and the perpetuation of the species. But he talked with passion, and that powerful spell worked on the sensibility of a young admirer. "What does it matter to women," this rhapsodist goes on, "that his reason disputes the empire with them, when his heart is devotedly theirs?" It is not empire but equality that they ought to be contending for. Yet if all they wanted was to prolong their sway, they should not trust entirely to their persons; for though beauty may win a heart, it cannot keep one, even in full bloom, unless the mind lends it at least a few graces.

Once women are enlightened enough to see their real interest on any grand scale, they will, I am persuaded, be very ready to give up all the prerogatives of love that are not mutual — speaking of them as lasting prerogatives — for the calm satisfaction of friendship and the tender confidence of a settled esteem. They will not put on insolent airs before marriage, or submit abjectly afterwards; but, trying to act like reasonable creatures in both situations, they will not be tumbled from a throne to a stool.

Madame de Genlis has written several entertaining books for children, and her letters on education offer many useful hints that sensible parents will certainly make use of; but her views are narrow, and her prejudices are as unreasonable as they are strong.

I shall pass over her vehement argument for the eternity of future punishments, because I blush to think that a human being should ever argue vehemently in such a cause, and shall make only a few remarks on her absurd way of making parental authority supplant reason. For everywhere she inculcates not only blind submission to parents but blind submission to the opinion of the world.

Footnote: A person is not to act in this or that way, though convinced it is right, because some ambiguous circumstance might lead the world to suspect that they acted from other motives. This is sacrificing the substance for a shadow. Let people watch their own hearts and act rightly as far as they can judge, and they may wait patiently until the opinion of the world comes round. It is best to be directed by a simple motive, for justice has too often been sacrificed to propriety — which is another word for convenience.

She tells the story of a young man engaged at his father's express wish to a girl with a fortune. Before the marriage can take place the girl loses her fortune and is thrown friendless on the world. The father practises the most infamous arts to separate his son from her; and when the son detects his villainy and, following the dictates of honour, marries the girl, nothing but misery follows, because, if you please, he married without his father's consent. What ground can religion or morality rest on, when justice is defied like this? In the same style she presents an accomplished young woman as ready to marry anybody her mamma chose to recommend, and as actually marrying the young man of her own choice without feeling any emotion of passion at all, because a well-educated girl had not had time to fall in love. Is it possible to have much respect for a system of education that insults reason and nature in this way?

Many similar opinions occur in her writings, mixed with sentiments that do honour to her head and heart. Yet so much superstition is mixed with her religion, and so much worldly wisdom with her morality, that I would not let a young person read her works unless I could talk the subjects over afterwards and point out the contradictions.

Mrs. Chapone's Letters are written with such good sense and such unaffected humility, and contain so many useful observations, that I mention them only to pay their worthy author this tribute of respect. I cannot always agree with her, it is true, but I always respect her.

The very word respect brings Mrs. Macaulay to my mind — undoubtedly the woman of the greatest abilities this country has ever produced. And yet this woman has been allowed to die without sufficient respect being paid to her memory.

Posterity, however, will be more just, and will remember that Catharine Macaulay was an example of intellectual attainments supposed to be incompatible with the weakness of her sex. Indeed, no sex appears in her style of writing; it is like the sense it conveys, strong and clear.

I will not call her understanding masculine, because I do not admit any such arrogant assumption of reason; but I maintain that it was a sound one, and that her judgment, the matured fruit of profound thinking, proved that a woman can acquire judgment in the fullest sense of the word. Possessing more penetration than shrewdness and more understanding than fancy, she writes with a sober energy and a closeness of argument; yet sympathy and benevolence give her sentiments an interest and give her arguments the vital heat that forces a reader to weigh them.

Footnote: Since I agree with Mrs. Macaulay about many branches of education, I refer the reader to her valuable work rather than quoting her sentiments in support of my own.

When I first thought of writing these criticisms I anticipated Mrs. Macaulay's approval with a little of that sanguine ardour it has been the business of my life to hold down; but I soon heard, with the sickly qualm of disappointed hope and the still seriousness of regret, that she was no more.

Section Five: Lord Chesterfield's Letters

In surveying the various works that have been written on education, Lord Chesterfield's Letters must not be passed over in silence. Not that I mean to analyse his unmanly, immoral system, or even to pick out the useful shrewd remarks that occur in his frivolous correspondence. I only mean to make a few reflections on their avowed purpose: the art of acquiring an early knowledge of the world. It is an art that preys secretly, like the worm in the bud, on the expanding powers, and turns to poison the generous juices that should be rising with vigour in a young frame and inspiring warm affections and great resolves.

For everything, says the wise man, there is a season; and who would look for the fruits of autumn during the kindly months of spring? But this is mere declamation, and I mean to reason with those worldly-wise instructors who, instead of cultivating the judgment, instil prejudices, and harden a heart that gradual experience would only have cooled. An early acquaintance with human infirmity, or what is called knowledge of the world, is in my opinion the surest way to contract the heart and damp that natural youthful ardour which produces not only great talents but great virtues. For the vain attempt to bring forth the fruit of experience before the sapling has put out its leaves only exhausts its strength and prevents it taking a natural form — just as the form and strength of cooling metals are injured when the attraction that holds them together is disturbed.

Tell me, you who have studied the human mind, is it not a strange way of fixing principles to show young people that principles are seldom stable? And how can they be fortified into habits when they are proved fallacious by example? Why should the ardour of youth be damped like this, and the luxuriance of fancy cut back to the quick? This dry caution may guard a character against worldly mischances, it is true; but it will infallibly rule out any excellence in virtue or in knowledge. The stumbling block that suspicion throws across every path will prevent any vigorous exertion of genius or benevolence, and life will be stripped of its most alluring charm long before its calm evening, when a man should be able to retire into contemplation for comfort and support.

A young man who has been brought up among friends at home, and encouraged to store his mind with as much speculative knowledge as reading can give, along with the natural reflections that youthful ebullitions of animal spirits and instinctive feelings inspire, will enter the world with warm and mistaken expectations. But this seems to be the course of nature; and in morals as in works of taste we should observe her sacred indications, and not presume to lead where we ought obediently to follow.

In the world few people act from principle: present feelings and early habits are the great springs of action. But how deadened the first would be, and how much like corroding iron fetters the second, if the world were shown to young people exactly as it is, before any knowledge of humankind or of their own hearts, slowly gained by experience, had made them forbearing. Their fellow creatures would not then be seen as frail beings like themselves, condemned to struggle with human infirmities and showing now the light and now the dark side of their character, drawing out alternate feelings of love and disgust; they would be guarded against like beasts of prey, until every enlarged social feeling — in a word, humanity — had been rooted out.

In life, by contrast, as we gradually discover the imperfections of our nature we discover virtues too; and various circumstances attach us to our fellow creatures when we mix with them and look at the same objects — circumstances never thought of in acquiring a hasty, unnatural knowledge of the world. We see a folly swell into a vice by almost imperceptible degrees, and pity even while we blame. But if the hideous monster burst on our sight all at once, then fear and disgust, making us more severe than any man ought to be, might lead us in blind zeal to usurp the character of omnipotence and pronounce damnation on our fellow mortals — forgetting that we cannot read the heart, and that the seeds of the same vices lurk in our own.

I have already remarked that we expect more from instruction than mere instruction can give. Instead of preparing young people to meet the evils of life with dignity, and to acquire wisdom and virtue by exercising their own faculties, we heap precept on precept and demand blind obedience where conviction should be brought home to the reason.

Suppose, for instance, that a young person in the first ardour of friendship makes a god of the person they love. What harm can come of this mistaken, enthusiastic attachment? Perhaps it is necessary for virtue to appear first in a human form in order to make an impression on young hearts; the ideal model that a more mature and exalted mind looks up to and shapes for itself would escape their sight altogether. If a person does not love the brother he has seen, how can he love God? asked the wisest of men.

It is natural for youth to adorn the first object of its affection with every good quality; and the emulation produced by ignorance — or, to speak more properly, by inexperience — brings forward the very mind that is capable of forming such an affection. Then, when in the course of time perfection turns out not to be within mortal reach, virtue in the abstract is thought beautiful and wisdom sublime. Admiration gives place to friendship properly so called, because it is cemented by esteem; and the person walks alone, depending on heaven alone for that emulous panting after perfection which always glows in a noble mind. But a man must gain this knowledge by exerting his own faculties; and surely it is the blessed fruit of disappointed hope. For He who delights to spread happiness and show mercy to the weak creatures who are learning to know him never planted a good propensity in us to be a tormenting will-o'-the-wisp.

We now let our trees spread with a wild luxuriance, and do not expect to combine the majestic marks of time with youthful graces by force; we wait patiently until they have struck their roots deep and braved many a storm. Is the mind, then, which advances towards perfection more slowly in proportion to its dignity, to be treated with less respect? Reasoning by analogy, everything around us is in a progressive state; and when an unwelcome knowledge of life produces something like a satiety of living, and we discover in the natural course of things that all that is done under the sun is vanity, we are drawing near the awful close of the drama. The days of activity and hope are over, and the opportunities the first stage of existence has offered for advancing in the scale of intelligence must soon be summed up. A knowledge of the futility of life at that period — or earlier, if it is gained by experience — is very useful, because it is natural. But when a frail being is shown the follies and vices of humankind so that he may be taught to guard prudently against the common accidents of life by sacrificing his heart, then surely it is not speaking harshly to call it the wisdom of this world, as against the nobler fruit of piety and experience.

I will venture a paradox and give my opinion without reserve. If men were born only to complete a circle of life and death, it would be wise to take every step foresight could suggest to make life happy. Moderation in every pursuit would then be the supreme wisdom, and the prudent voluptuary might enjoy a degree of contentment though he neither cultivated his understanding nor kept his heart pure. Prudence, supposing we were mortal, would be true wisdom, or, to be more explicit, would procure the greatest portion of happiness taking life as a whole; but knowledge beyond the conveniences of life would be a curse.

Why should we injure our health with close study? The exalted pleasure that intellectual pursuits give would hardly be equivalent to the hours of languor that follow, especially if we have to reckon in the doubts and disappointments that cloud our researches. Vanity and vexation close every enquiry, for the cause we particularly wished to discover flies before us as we advance, like the horizon. The ignorant, on the contrary, are like children, and suppose that if they could only walk straight forward they would arrive at last where the earth and the clouds meet. Yet, disappointed as we are in our researches, the mind gains strength from the exercise — enough, perhaps, to comprehend the answers it may receive at another stage of existence to the anxious questions it asked here, when the understanding was fluttering on feeble wings round the visible effects and trying to dive down to the hidden cause.

The passions too, the winds of life, would be useless if not injurious, if the substance that composes our thinking selves, after we had thought in vain, merely became the support of vegetable life and went to invigorate a cabbage or blush in a rose. The appetites would answer every earthly purpose and produce a more moderate and lasting happiness. But the powers of the soul that are of so little use here, and that probably disturb our animal enjoyments even while a conscious dignity makes us glory in having them, prove that life is only an education, a state of infancy, whose one hope worth cherishing should not be thrown away. I mean to infer, therefore, that we ought to have a precise idea of what we want education to achieve; for the immortality of the soul is contradicted by the actions of a great many people who firmly profess to believe in it.

If your first consideration is to secure ease and prosperity on earth and to leave the future to look after itself, then you act prudently in giving your child an early insight into the weaknesses of his nature. You may not, it is true, make an Inkle of him; but do not imagine that a boy who has absorbed a mean opinion of human nature very early will stick to anything more than the letter of the law, or think it necessary to rise much above the common standard. He may avoid gross vices, because honesty is the best policy; but he will never aim at great virtues. The example of writers and artists will illustrate the remark.

I must therefore venture to doubt whether what has been treated as an axiom in morals may not have been a dogmatic assertion made by men who have coolly seen humankind through the medium of books; and I say, in direct contradiction to them, that the regulation of the passions is not always wisdom. On the contrary, it would seem that one reason why men have better judgment and more fortitude than women is undoubtedly that they give freer scope to the grand passions and, by going astray more often, enlarge their minds. If they then fix on some stable principle by exercising their own reason, they probably have to thank the force of their passions, nourished by false views of life and allowed to leap over the boundary that keeps a man contented. But if in the dawn of life we could soberly survey the scene before us as if in perspective, and see everything in its true colours, how could the passions ever gain strength enough to unfold our faculties?

Let me now, as from a height, survey the world stripped of all its false, deluding charms. The clear air lets me see each object in its true perspective, while my heart is still. I am as calm as the prospect on a morning when the mists disperse slowly and silently unveil the beauties of nature, refreshed by rest.

In what light does the world appear now? I rub my eyes and think, perhaps, that I am only just waking from a vivid dream.

I see the sons and daughters of men pursuing shadows and anxiously wasting their powers to feed passions that have no adequate object — unless the very excess of these blind impulses, pampered by that lying yet constantly trusted guide the imagination, does in fact prepare them for some other state and so make short-sighted mortals wiser without their meaning to be, or, what comes to the same thing, while they were chasing some imaginary present good.

Having looked at things in this light, it would not be very fanciful to imagine that this world is a stage on which a pantomime is performed daily for the amusement of superior beings. How they would be entertained to see the ambitious man consuming himself in the pursuit of a phantom, chasing the bubble fame into "the cannon's mouth" that was to blow him into nothing — for once consciousness is gone it does not matter whether we go up in a whirlwind or come down in rain. And if they compassionately sharpened his sight and showed him the thorny path that leads to eminence, sinking like a quicksand as he climbs and disappointing his hopes when they are almost within his grasp, would he not leave the honour of amusing them to somebody else and set about securing the present moment — though, from the constitution of his nature, he would not find it easy to catch that flying stream? Such slaves we are to hope and fear!

But vain as the ambitious man's pursuit is, he is often striving for something more substantial than fame; fame alone would be the veriest meteor, the wildest fire that ever lured a man to ruin. What — renounce the most trifling gratification in order to be applauded when he is no longer there to hear it! Why this struggle, whether man is mortal or immortal, if that noble passion did not really raise him above his fellows?

And love! What diverting scenes it would produce — Pantaloon's tricks must yield to a more egregious folly. To see a mortal adorn an object with imaginary charms, and then fall down and worship the idol he set up himself — how ridiculous! But what serious consequences follow, to rob a man of the portion of happiness that the Deity, in calling him into existence, has indubitably promised him — for on what else can his attributes rest? Would not all the purposes of life have been much better served if he had only felt what has been called physical love? And would not the sight of the object, seen without the medium of imagination, soon reduce the passion to an appetite, if reflection, the noble distinction of man, did not give it force and make it an instrument for raising him above this earthly dross, by teaching him to love the centre of all perfection — whose wisdom appears clearer and clearer in the works of nature as reason is illuminated and exalted by contemplation, and by acquiring that love of order which the struggles of passion produce?

The habit of reflection, and the knowledge gained by nursing any passion, could be shown to be equally useful even if the object proved equally illusory; for all objects would look the same if they were not magnified by the governing passion the Author of all good implanted in us to call out and strengthen the faculties of each individual, and to let it gather all the experience an infant can gather, who does certain things and cannot tell why.

I come down from my height and, mixing with my fellow creatures, feel myself hurried along in the common stream. Ambition, love, hope and fear exert their usual power, though reason has convinced us that their present and most attractive promises are only lying dreams. But if the cold hand of circumspection had damped every generous feeling before it left any permanent mark on the character or fixed any habit, what could be expected but a selfish prudence and a reason rising only just above instinct? Who that has read Dean Swift's disgusting description of the Yahoos, and his insipid one of the Houyhnhnms, with a philosophical eye can fail to see the futility of degrading the passions, or of making a man rest content?

A young man should act; for if he had the experience of a grey head he would be fitter for death than for life, and his virtues, residing in his head rather than his heart, could produce nothing great, while his understanding, prepared for this world, would not prove by any noble flights that it had a title to a better one.

Besides, it is not possible to give a young person a just view of life. He must have struggled with his own passions before he can estimate the force of the temptation that betrayed his brother into vice. Those who are entering life and those who are leaving it see the world from such very different points of view that they can seldom think alike, unless the unfledged reason of the first has never attempted a solitary flight.

When we hear of some daring crime, it comes on us full in the deepest shade of turpitude and rouses indignation; but the eye that watched the darkness thicken by degrees must look at it with a more compassionate forbearance. The world cannot be seen by an unmoved spectator; we must mix in the crowd and feel as men feel before we can judge of their feelings. If we mean, in short, to live in the world in order to grow wiser and better, and not merely to enjoy the good things of life, we must acquire a knowledge of others at the same time that we become acquainted with ourselves. Knowledge acquired any other way only hardens the heart and perplexes the understanding.

I may be told that the knowledge gained in this way is sometimes bought too dear. I can only answer that I very much doubt whether any knowledge can be got without labour and sorrow, and that those who wish to spare their children both should not complain if their children turn out neither wise nor virtuous. They only aimed at making them prudent — and prudence, early in life, is nothing but the cautious craft of an ignorant self-love.

I have observed that young people whose education has had particular attention paid to it are generally very superficial and conceited, and far from pleasing in any respect, because they had neither the unsuspecting warmth of youth nor the cool depth of age. I cannot help imputing this unnatural appearance chiefly to the hasty, premature instruction that leads them to repeat presumptuously all the crude notions they have taken on trust — so that the careful education they received makes them the slaves of prejudice for the rest of their lives.

Mental exertion, like bodily exertion, is irksome at first, so much so that most people would gladly let others both work and think for them. An observation I have often made will illustrate what I mean. When, in a circle of strangers or acquaintances, a person of moderate abilities asserts an opinion heatedly, I will venture to say — for I have traced this to its source very often — that the opinion is a prejudice. These echoes have a high respect for the understanding of some relation or friend, and, without fully comprehending the opinions they are so eager to retail, they maintain them with an obstinacy that would astonish even the person who concocted them.

I know a fashion now prevails of respecting prejudices, and that when anyone dares to face them, though moved by humanity and armed with reason, he is superciliously asked whether his ancestors were fools. No, I should answer; opinions of every description were probably all considered at first, and so were founded on some reason. But quite often, of course, it was a local expedient rather than a fundamental principle that would be reasonable at all times. Moss-covered opinions take on the disproportionate form of prejudices when they are lazily adopted only because age has given them a venerable look, though the reason they were built on has ceased to be a reason, or cannot be traced. Why are we to love prejudices merely because they are prejudices? A prejudice is a fond, obstinate persuasion for which we can give no reason; for the moment a reason can be given for an opinion it stops being a prejudice, though it may be an error of judgment. Are we, then, advised to cherish opinions only in order to set reason at defiance? This way of arguing, if it can be called arguing, reminds me of what is vulgarly called a woman's reason. For women sometimes declare that they love or believe certain things because they love or believe them.

It is impossible to converse to any purpose with people who in this style use nothing but affirmatives and negatives. Before you can bring them to a point to start fairly from, you must go back to the simple principles that came before the prejudices power has broached; and it is ten to one that you are stopped by the philosophical assertion that certain principles are as practically false as they are abstractly true. Indeed one may infer that reason has whispered some doubts, for it generally happens that people assert their opinions most heatedly when they are beginning to waver: striving to drive out their own doubts by convincing an opponent, they grow angry when those gnawing doubts are thrown back to prey on themselves.

The fact is that men expect from education what education cannot give. A sagacious parent or tutor may strengthen the body and sharpen the instruments the child is to gather knowledge with; but the honey must be the reward of the individual's own industry. It is almost as absurd to try to make a boy wise on somebody else's experience as to expect a body to grow strong on exercise that is only talked about, or watched.

Many of the children whose conduct has been most narrowly watched become the weakest men, because their instructors only instil into their minds certain notions that have no foundation except the instructor's authority; and if that instructor is loved or respected, the mind is cramped in its exertions and unsteady in its advances. In such a case the business of education is only to guide the shooting tendrils to a proper pole; and yet, after laying precept on precept without ever letting a child acquire judgment for itself, parents expect them to act by this borrowed, fallacious light exactly as if they had lit it themselves, and to be, when they enter life, what their parents are at the close of it. They do not consider that a tree, and even the human body, does not strengthen its fibres until it has reached its full growth.

There seems to be something analogous in the mind. The senses and the imagination give the character its form during childhood and youth; and the understanding, as life advances, gives firmness to the first fair purposes of feeling — until virtue arises from the clear conviction of reason rather than from the impulse of the heart, and morality is made to rest on a rock that the storms of passion beat against in vain.

I hope I shall not be misunderstood when I say that religion will not have this consolidating power unless it is founded on reason. If it is merely the refuge of weakness or of wild fanaticism, and not a governing principle of conduct drawn from self-knowledge and from a rational opinion about the attributes of God, what can it be expected to produce? The religion that consists in warming the affections and exalting the imagination is only the poetical part of it, and may give an individual pleasure without making that individual any more moral. It may be a substitute for worldly pursuits and still narrow the heart instead of enlarging it. Virtue must be loved as sublime and excellent in itself, and not for the advantages it brings or the evils it wards off, if any great degree of excellence is to be expected. Men will not become moral by building castles in the air in a future world to make up for the disappointments they meet with in this one, if they turn their thoughts away from the duties in front of them towards religious daydreams.

Most prospects in life are spoiled by the shuffling worldly wisdom of men who, forgetting that they cannot serve God and money, try to blend contradictory things. If you wish to make your son rich, take one road; if you are only anxious to make him virtuous, you must take another. But do not imagine that you can bound from one to the other without losing your way.

Footnote: See an excellent essay on this subject by Mrs. Barbauld, in Miscellaneous Pieces in Prose.

Chapter Six: The Effect That Early Associations of Ideas Have on Character

Educated in the enervating style recommended by the writers I have been criticizing, and given no chance by their subordinate place in society to recover the ground they have lost, is it surprising that women everywhere look like a defect in nature? Is it surprising, when we consider how decisively an early association of ideas affects the character, that they neglect their understandings and turn all their attention to their persons?

The great advantages that naturally follow from stocking the mind with knowledge are obvious from these considerations. The association of our ideas is either habitual or instantaneous, and the second kind seems to depend on the original temperature of the mind rather than on the will. Once ideas and matters of fact have been taken in, they lie by for use until some chance circumstance makes information that was received at very different periods of our lives dart into the mind with illustrative force. Many recollections are like a flash of lightning, one idea assimilating and explaining another with astonishing speed. I am not talking here about that quick perception of truth which is so intuitive that it baffles investigation, and leaves us unable to decide whether it is memory or reasoning, lost sight of in its own swiftness, that opens the dark cloud. Over those instantaneous associations we have little power; for once the mind has been enlarged by roving flights or by profound reflection, the raw materials will to some degree arrange themselves. The understanding, it is true, may keep us from going out of drawing when we group our thoughts, or transcribe the warm sketches of fancy from the imagination; but it is the animal spirits, the individual character, that give the colouring. How little power we have over that subtle electric fluid, and how little power reason can gain over it! These fine, intractable spirits appear to be the essence of genius, and, beaming in its eagle eye, produce in the highest degree that happy energy of associating thoughts which surprises, delights and instructs. These are the glowing minds that concentrate pictures for their fellow creatures, forcing them to look with interest at objects reflected from an impassioned imagination which they had passed over in nature itself.

Footnote: When I have felt inclined to laugh at materialists, I have sometimes asked whether, since the most powerful effects in nature are apparently produced by fluids — the magnetic and so on — the passions might not be fine volatile fluids that embrace humanity and hold its more refractory elementary parts together; or whether they are simply a liquid fire pervading the more sluggish materials and giving them life and heat.

I must be allowed to explain myself. Most people cannot see or feel poetically; they lack fancy, and so they fly from solitude in search of tangible objects. But when an author lends them his eyes they can see as he saw, and be entertained by images they could never have picked out for themselves, though the images lay in front of them.

Education, then, only supplies the man of genius with knowledge to give variety and contrast to his associations. But there is also an habitual association of ideas, growing "with our growth", which has a great effect on the moral character of humankind and gives the mind a turn that usually stays with it for life. The understanding is so ductile, and yet so stubborn, that the associations formed by accidental circumstances during the period the body takes to reach maturity can seldom be untangled by reason. One idea calls up another, its old associate, and memory, faithful to first impressions — particularly when the intellectual powers are not employed in cooling our sensations — retraces them with mechanical exactness.

This habitual slavery to first impressions has a more damaging effect on the female character than on the male, because business and the other dry employments of the understanding tend to deaden the feelings and break associations that do violence to reason. But women, who are made women of while they are mere children and brought back to childhood when they ought to be leaving the go-cart forever, have not strength of mind enough to rub out the artificial additions that have smothered nature.

Everything they see or hear serves to fix impressions, call up emotions and form associations that give their minds a character supposed to belong to their sex. False notions of beauty and delicacy stunt the growth of their limbs and produce a sickly tenderness rather than a fineness of organs; and, weakened in this way by being employed in unfolding their first associations rather than examining them — associations forced on them by every object around them — how are they to attain the vigour they would need to throw off their artificial character? Where are they to find the strength to fall back on reason and rise above a system of oppression that blights the fair promises of spring? This cruel association of ideas, which everything conspires to twist into all their habits of thinking, or, to be more precise, of feeling, gains new force when they begin to act a little for themselves; for they then see that pleasure and power are only to be got through their skill in exciting emotion in men. Besides, all the books written expressly for their instruction, which make the first impression on their minds, teach the same opinions. Educated in a bondage worse than Egyptian, it is unreasonable as well as cruel to reproach them with faults they could hardly avoid, unless we suppose a degree of native vigour that falls to the lot of very few people of either sex.

The severest sarcasms, for instance, have been aimed at women, and they have been ridiculed for repeating "a set of phrases learnt by rote", when nothing could be more natural, considering the education they get and the fact that their "highest praise is to obey, unargued" the will of man. If they are not allowed enough reason to govern their own conduct, then everything they learn must be learned by rote. And when all their ingenuity is called out to arrange their dress, "a passion for a scarlet coat" is so natural that it has never surprised me. And granting that Pope's summary of their character is just, "that every woman is at heart a rake", why should they be bitterly censured for looking for a congenial mind and preferring a rake to a man of sense?

Rakes know how to work on their sensibility, while the modest merit of reasonable men naturally has less effect on their feelings; such men cannot reach the heart by way of the understanding, because they have few sentiments in common with it.

It seems a little absurd to expect women to be more reasonable than men in their likings, and still to deny them the uncontrolled use of reason. When do men fall in love with sense? When do they, with all their superior powers and advantages, turn from the person to the mind? And how can they then expect women, taught only to observe behaviour and to acquire manners rather than morals, to despise what they have spent their whole lives labouring to attain? Where are they suddenly to find judgment enough to weigh patiently the good sense of an awkward, virtuous man, when his manners, which they are made critical judges of, are off-putting, and his conversation cold and dull because it does not consist of pretty repartees or well-turned compliments? To admire or esteem anything for any length of time, we must at least have our curiosity roused by knowing to some degree what we admire; for we cannot estimate the value of qualities and virtues that are above our comprehension. Such a respect, when it is felt, may be very sublime, and the confused consciousness of humility may make the dependent creature an interesting object from some points of view; but human love must have coarser ingredients, and the person will very naturally come in for its share — an ample share, in most cases.

Love is to a great extent an arbitrary passion, and will reign, like certain other stalking mischiefs, on its own authority, without condescending to reason. It can also be easily distinguished from esteem, the foundation of friendship, because it is often excited by fleeting beauties and graces — though to give the sentiment any energy something more solid must deepen the impression and set the imagination to work, making what is fairest into what is first and best.

Common passions are excited by common qualities. Men look for beauty and the simper of good-humoured docility; women are captivated by easy manners. A gentlemanly man seldom fails to please them, and their thirsty ears drink in the insinuating nothings of politeness eagerly, while they turn away from the unintelligible sounds of that charmer, reason, however wisely he charms. As for superficial accomplishments, the rake certainly has the advantage, and of those women can form an opinion, since it is their own ground. Made gay and giddy by the whole tenor of their lives, the very look of wisdom, or the severe graces of virtue, must seem lugubrious to them and produce a kind of restraint that they, and love, that sportive child, naturally revolt from. Having no taste except of the lighter kind, since taste is the offspring of judgment, how can they discover that true beauty and grace must arise from the play of the mind? And how can they be expected to relish in a lover what they do not possess themselves, or possess very imperfectly? The sympathy that unites hearts and invites confidence is so very faint in them that it cannot catch fire and rise into passion. No, I say it again: the love such minds cherish must have coarser fuel.

The inference is obvious. Until women are led to exercise their understandings, they should not be satirized for their attachment to rakes, nor even for being rakes at heart, when that appears to be the inevitable consequence of their education. Those who live to please must find their enjoyment, their happiness, in pleasure. It is a trite remark, but a true one, that we never do anything well unless we love it for its own sake.

Suppose, however, for a moment that women were, in some future revolution of time, to become what I sincerely wish them to be. Then even love would acquire a more serious dignity and be purified in its own fires; and virtue, giving their affections a true delicacy, would make them turn from a rake in disgust. Reasoning then, as well as feeling — which is at present woman's only province — they could easily guard against exterior graces, and would quickly learn to despise a sensibility that had been excited and worn out among women whose trade was vice and allurement's wanton airs. They would remember that the flame they wished to kindle — one must use the appropriate expressions — had been burnt out by lust, and that a sated appetite, having lost all relish for pure and simple pleasures, could only be roused by the licentious arts of variety. What satisfaction could a woman of delicacy promise herself in a union with such a man, when the very artlessness of her affection might seem insipid to him? This is how Dryden describes the situation:

	"Where love is duty on the female side,
	On theirs mere sensual gust, and sought with surly pride."

But there is one grand truth women have still to learn, though it matters a great deal that they should act on it. In choosing a husband they should not be led astray by the qualities of a lover — for a lover is what a husband, even supposing him wise and virtuous, cannot long remain.

If women were more rationally educated, and could take a more comprehensive view of things, they would be content to love only once in their lives, and after marriage would calmly let passion subside into friendship — into that tender intimacy which is the best refuge from care, and which is built on affections so pure and so quiet that idle jealousies would not be allowed to disturb the discharge of the sober duties of life, or to monopolize thoughts that ought to be otherwise employed. Many men live in that state; but few, very few women. And the difference can easily be accounted for without falling back on a character proper to each sex. Men, for whom we are told women were made, have occupied women's thoughts too much; and this association has so entangled love with all their motives for acting that — to harp a little on an old string — having been employed solely either in preparing themselves to excite love or in actually putting their lessons into practice, they cannot live without it. But when a sense of duty, or the fear of shame, obliges them to restrain this pampered desire to please beyond a certain point — too far for delicacy, it is true, though far short of criminality — they obstinately determine to love their husbands, I am speaking of their passion, to the end of the chapter. And then, acting the part they foolishly demanded of their lovers, they become abject wooers and fond slaves.

Men of wit and fancy are often rakes, and fancy is the food of love. Such men will inspire passion. Half of my sex, in its present infantile state, would pine for a Lovelace, a man so witty, so graceful and so valiant; and can they deserve blame for acting on principles so constantly drummed into them? They want a lover and a protector, and here he is, kneeling in front of them — bravery prostrate before beauty! A husband's virtues are thrown into the background by love, and gay hopes and lively emotions banish reflection until the day of reckoning comes. And come it surely will, to turn the sprightly lover into a surly, suspicious tyrant who contemptuously insults the very weakness he fostered. Or, supposing the rake reformed, he cannot get rid of old habits quickly. When a man of ability is first carried away by his passions, sentiment and taste have to varnish the enormities of vice and give a zest to brutal indulgences; but once the gloss of novelty has worn off and pleasure palls on the senses, lasciviousness becomes barefaced and enjoyment is nothing but the desperate effort of a weakness fleeing from reflection as from a legion of devils. Oh, virtue, you are no empty name! All that life can give, you give.

If not much comfort is to be expected from the friendship of a reformed rake of superior abilities, what is the consequence when he lacks sense as well as principles? Truly, misery in its most hideous shape. When the habits of weak people have been consolidated by time, reform is barely possible, and actually makes wretched the very people who have not mind enough to be amused by innocent pleasure. As with the tradesman who retires from the hurry of business, nature presents them with nothing but a universal blank, and their restless thoughts prey on their damped spirits. Their reformation, like his retirement, actually makes them miserable, because it takes away all their employment by quenching the hopes and fears that used to set their sluggish minds in motion.

If such is the force of habit, if such is the bondage of folly, how carefully we ought to guard the mind against storing up vicious associations! And how equally careful we should be to cultivate the understanding, and save the poor creature from the weak, dependent state of even a harmless ignorance. For it is the right use of reason alone that makes us independent of everything except the unclouded Reason itself — "whose service is perfect freedom".

Chapter Seven: Modesty, Considered in Full, and Not as a Virtue Belonging to One Sex

Modesty! Sacred offspring of sensibility and reason! True delicacy of mind! May I presume, without blame, to investigate your nature, and trace to its hiding place that mild charm which mellows every harsh feature of a character and makes lovely what would otherwise inspire nothing but cold admiration? You that smooth the wrinkles of wisdom, and soften the tone of the more sublime virtues until they all melt into humanity; you that spread the ethereal cloud which, surrounding love, heightens every beauty it half shades, breathing those coy sweets that steal into the heart and charm the senses — modulate for me the language of persuasive reason, until I have roused my sex from the flowery bed where they lie sleeping their lives away.

In speaking of the association of our ideas I noticed two distinct modes; and in defining modesty it seems to me equally proper to distinguish that purity of mind which is the effect of chastity from the simplicity of character that leads us to form a just opinion of ourselves — equally far from vanity and from presumption, though by no means incompatible with a lofty consciousness of our own dignity. Modesty in the second sense of the word is that soberness of mind which teaches a man not to think more highly of himself than he ought; and it should be distinguished from humility, because humility is a kind of self-abasement.

A modest man often conceives a great plan and holds to it tenaciously, conscious of his own strength, until success gives it a sanction that settles its character. Milton was not arrogant when he let slip a suggestion of his judgment that turned out to be a prophecy; nor was General Washington when he accepted command of the American forces. Washington has always been described as a modest man; but if he had been merely humble he would probably have shrunk back irresolute, afraid to trust himself with the direction of an enterprise on which so much depended.

A modest man is steady, a humble man timid, and a vain one presumptuous: that is the judgment which observing many characters has led me to form. Jesus Christ was modest, Moses was humble, and Peter was vain.

Having distinguished modesty from humility in the one case, I do not mean to confound it with bashfulness in the other. Bashfulness is in fact so distinct from modesty that the most bashful girl, or the rawest country lout, often becomes the most impudent; for their bashfulness being merely the instinctive timidity of ignorance, custom soon turns it into assurance.

Footnote: "Such is the country maiden's fright, when first a redcoat is in sight; behind the door she hides her face, next time at distance eyes the lace: she now can all his terrors stand, nor from his squeeze withdraws her hand, she plays familiar in his arms, and every soldier has his charms; from tent to tent she spreads her flame; for custom conquers fear and shame."

The shameless behaviour of the prostitutes who infest the streets of London, rousing pity and disgust by turns, may serve to illustrate the remark. They trample on virgin bashfulness with a sort of bravado and, glorying in their shame, become more audaciously lewd than men ever appear to be, however depraved — men to whom the quality has not been gratuitously granted along with their sex. But these poor ignorant wretches never had any modesty to lose when they consigned themselves to infamy, for modesty is a virtue and not a quality. No: they were only bashful, shamefaced innocents, and when they lost their innocence their shamefacedness was rudely brushed off. A virtue would have left some traces in the mind, even if it had been sacrificed to passion, to make us respect the grand ruin.

Purity of mind, or that genuine delicacy which is the only virtuous support of chastity, is near kin to the refinement of humanity that never lives in any but a cultivated mind. It is something nobler than innocence: it is the delicacy of reflection, and not the coyness of ignorance. This reserve of reason, which, like habitual cleanliness, is seldom seen to any great degree unless the soul is active, is easily distinguished from rustic shyness or wanton skittishness; and so far from being incompatible with knowledge, it is knowledge's fairest fruit. What a gross idea of modesty the writer of the following remark had! "The lady who asked whether women may be instructed in the modern system of botany, consistently with female delicacy, was accused of ridiculous prudery. Nevertheless, if she had put the question to me, I should certainly have answered: they cannot." So the fair book of knowledge is to be shut with an everlasting seal. On reading passages like that I have reverently lifted my eyes and my heart to Him who lives forever and ever, and said: O my Father, have you, by the very constitution of her nature, forbidden your child to seek you in the fair forms of truth? And can her soul be sullied by the knowledge that calls her so awfully towards you?

Then I have philosophically pursued these reflections until I concluded that the women who have improved their reason most must have the most modesty, though a dignified sedateness of manner may have taken the place of the playful, bewitching bashfulness of youth.

Footnote: Modesty is the graceful calm virtue of maturity; bashfulness is the charm of vivacious youth.

And this is how I have argued. To make chastity the virtue from which an unaffected modesty naturally flows, the attention must be drawn away from employments that only exercise the sensibility, and the heart must be made to beat time to humanity rather than throb with love. The woman who has devoted a considerable part of her time to purely intellectual pursuits, and whose affections have been exercised by humane plans of usefulness, must as a natural consequence have more purity of mind than the ignorant creatures whose time and thoughts have been taken up with gay pleasures or with schemes for conquering hearts. Regulating the behaviour is not modesty, though the women who study the rules of decorum are generally called modest. Make the heart clean, let it expand and feel for everything human instead of being narrowed by selfish passions; let the mind often contemplate subjects that exercise the understanding without heating the imagination; and an artless modesty will put the finishing touches to the picture.

The woman who can discern the dawn of immortality in the streaks shooting across the misty night of ignorance, promising a clearer day, will respect as a sacred temple the body that houses such an improvable soul. True love likewise spreads this kind of mysterious sanctity round the beloved object, making the lover most modest in her presence. Affection is so reserved that in giving or receiving personal endearments it wishes not only to avoid the human eye, as a kind of profanation, but to draw a cloudy obscurity all round to shut out even the saucy sparkling sunbeams. Yet no affection deserves the name of chaste that does not take on a sublime gloom of tender melancholy, allowing the mind to stand still for a moment and enjoy the present satisfaction, when a consciousness of the Divine presence is felt — for that must always be the food of joy.

Since I have always been fond of tracing any prevailing custom back to its source in nature, I have often thought that what gave rise to the respect for relics, so much abused by selfish priests, was a feeling of affection for whatever has touched the person of an absent or a lost friend. Devotion, or love, may be allowed to hallow the clothes as well as the person; for a lover must be short of fancy who has no sort of sacred respect for his mistress's glove or slipper. He could not confuse them with common things of the same kind.

This fine sentiment, perhaps, would not bear analysis by the experimental philosopher — but this is the stuff human rapture is made of. A shadowy phantom glides in front of us, obscuring every other object; yet when the soft cloud is grasped, the form melts into common air, leaving a solitary void, or a sweet perfume stolen from the violet, which memory holds dear for a long time afterwards. But I have wandered onto fairy ground without meaning to, and feel the balmy breeze of spring stealing over me though November is frowning.

As a sex, women are more chaste than men; and since modesty is the effect of chastity, they may deserve to have the virtue ascribed to them in a rather special sense. Yet I must be allowed to add a hesitating if. For I doubt whether chastity will produce modesty, though it may produce propriety of conduct, when it is merely a respect for the opinion of the world, and when coquetry and the lovelorn tales of novelists occupy the thoughts. Indeed, experience and reason would lead me to expect more modesty among men than among women, simply because men exercise their understandings more.

But as far as propriety of behaviour goes, one class of women excepted, women plainly have the advantage. What can be more disgusting than that impudent dross of gallantry, thought so manly, which makes so many men stare insultingly at every woman they meet? Is this respect for the sex? Such loose behaviour shows so habitual a depravity, such a weakness of mind, that it is useless to expect much public or private virtue until both men and women grow more modest — until men, curbing a sensual fondness for women, or an affectation of manly assurance, or, more properly speaking, impudence, treat each other with respect, instead of letting appetite or passion set the tone of their behaviour. I mean personal respect: the modest respect of humanity and fellow feeling, not the libidinous mockery of gallantry, nor the insolent condescension of protection.

To carry the observation further still, modesty must heartily disown and refuse to live with that debauchery of mind which lets a man coolly produce indecent allusions or obscene witticisms, without blushing, in the presence of a fellow creature. Women are out of the question here, for in their presence it is brutality. Respect for a man as a man is the foundation of every noble sentiment. How much more modest is the libertine who obeys the call of appetite or fancy than the lewd joker who sets the table in a roar.

This is one of many instances in which the distinction of the sexes with regard to modesty has proved fatal to virtue and to happiness. It is carried further still, for woman — weak woman, made by her education the slave of sensibility — is required on the most trying occasions to resist that very sensibility. "Can anything," says Knox, "be more absurd than keeping women in a state of ignorance, and yet insisting so vehemently on their resisting temptation?" So when virtue or honour makes it proper to check a passion, the burden is thrown on the weaker shoulders, contrary to reason and to true modesty, which should at least make the self-denial mutual — to say nothing of the generosity of bravery, which is supposed to be a manly virtue.

Rousseau's and Dr. Gregory's advice about modesty, strangely so called, runs in the same strain; for they both want a wife to leave it in doubt whether it was sensibility or weakness that brought her into her husband's arms. The woman is immodest who can let the shadow of such a doubt stay in her husband's mind for a moment.

But to put the subject in a different light. The want of modesty that I chiefly deplore as subversive of morality arises from that state of warfare which voluptuous men so strenuously maintain to be the very essence of modesty, though it is in fact modesty's bane; for it is a refinement on sensual desire that men fall into who have not virtue enough to relish the innocent pleasures of love. A man of delicacy carries his notions of modesty further, for neither weakness nor sensibility will satisfy him: he is looking for affection.

Again: men boast of their triumphs over women. What are they boasting of? Truly, that a creature of sensibility was surprised by her own sensibility into folly, into vice; and the dreadful reckoning falls heavily on her own weak head when reason wakes. For where are you to find comfort, forlorn and disconsolate creature? The man who ought to have directed your reason and supported your weakness has betrayed you. In a dream of passion you consented to wander through flowery meadows, and, stepping heedlessly over the precipice to which your guide lured you instead of guarding you, you start out of your dream only to face a sneering, frowning world and find yourself alone in a wilderness; for the man who triumphed in your weakness is off pursuing new conquests. But for you there is no redemption this side of the grave. And what resource do you have, with an enfeebled mind, to lift a sinking heart?

Footnote: The poor moth fluttering round a candle burns its wings.

But if the sexes really must live in a state of warfare, if nature has marked it out for them, then let men act nobly, or let their pride whisper to them that it is a mean victory merely to overcome a woman's sensibility. The real conquest is over an affection that was not taken by surprise — when, like Heloisa, a woman deliberately gives up the whole world for love. I am not considering the wisdom or the virtue of such a sacrifice now; I only maintain that it was a sacrifice to affection and not merely to sensibility, though sensibility had its share. And I must be allowed to call her a modest woman before I leave this part of the subject, saying only that until men are more chaste, women will be immodest. Where indeed could modest women find husbands they would not be continually turning from in disgust? Modesty must be cultivated equally by both sexes, or it will always be a sickly hothouse plant, while the affectation of it, that fig leaf borrowed by wantonness, gives a zest to voluptuous enjoyments.

Men will probably still insist that a woman ought to have more modesty than a man; but it is not the dispassionate reasoners who will oppose my opinion most earnestly. No: it is the men of fancy, the favourites of the sex, who outwardly respect and inwardly despise the weak creatures they play with. They cannot bring themselves to give up the highest sensual gratification, or even to relish the epicurism of virtue, which is self-denial.

To take another view of the subject, confining my remarks to women.

The ridiculous falsehoods told to children out of mistaken notions of modesty tend very early to inflame their imaginations and set their little minds working on subjects nature never intended them to think about until the body had reached some degree of maturity — when the passions naturally begin to take the place of the senses as instruments for unfolding the understanding and forming the moral character.

I am afraid it is in nurseries and boarding schools that girls are first spoiled, and particularly in boarding schools. A number of girls sleep in the same room and wash together. And though I should be sorry to contaminate an innocent creature's mind by instilling a false delicacy, or those indecent prudish notions that early cautions about the other sex naturally breed, I should be very anxious to prevent their acquiring indelicate or immodest habits; and since a great many girls have learned very indelicate tricks from ignorant servants, mixing them together indiscriminately in this way is very improper.

To tell the truth, women are in general too familiar with one another, and this leads to the gross familiarity that so often makes married life unhappy. Why in the name of decency are sisters, or female friends, or ladies and their maids, to be so grossly familiar as to forget the respect one human creature owes another? The squeamish delicacy that shrinks from the most disgusting offices when affection or humanity calls us to sit up beside a sickbed is contemptible. But why women in health should be more familiar with each other than men are, when they boast of their superior delicacy, is a puzzle in manners I have never been able to solve.

To preserve health and beauty I would earnestly recommend frequent washing — to dignify my advice so that it may not offend a fastidious ear. And girls ought to be taught by example to wash and dress alone, whatever their rank; and if custom has made them need a little assistance, let them not need it until that part of the business is over which ought never to be done in front of a fellow creature, because it is an insult to the majesty of human nature. Not on the score of modesty, but of decency: for the care some modest women take not to let their legs be seen, while making a display of taking it, is as childish as it is immodest.

Footnote: I remember coming across a sentence in a book on education that made me smile: "It would be needless to caution you against putting your hand, by chance, under your neck handkerchief; for a modest woman never did such a thing."

I could go further, and criticize customs more indelicate still, which men never fall into. Secrets are told where silence ought to reign; and that regard for cleanliness which some religious sects have perhaps carried too far — the Essenes among the Jews particularly, by making an insult to God of what is only an insult to humanity — is violated in a brutal fashion. How can delicate women force on other people's notice that part of the animal economy which is so very disgusting? And is it not quite reasonable to conclude that women who have not been taught to respect the human nature of their own sex in these particulars will not long respect the mere difference of sex in their husbands? Once their maidenly bashfulness is lost, I have generally observed that women fall back into old habits and treat their husbands as they used to treat their sisters or their female acquaintance.

Besides, women, because their minds are not cultivated, have recourse very often to what I familiarly call bodily wit, and their intimacies are of the same kind. In short, in mind and in body alike, they are too intimate. That decent personal reserve which is the foundation of dignity of character must be kept up between women, or their minds will never gain either strength or modesty.

On this account too I object to numbers of women being shut up together in nurseries, schools or convents. I cannot recall without indignation the jokes and hoyden tricks that knots of young women indulged in when, in my youth, accident threw me, an awkward country girl, in their way. They were almost on a level with the double meanings that shake a convivial table once the glass has gone round freely. But it is useless to try to keep the heart pure unless the head is furnished with ideas and set to work comparing them, so as to acquire judgment by generalizing the simple ones, and modesty by making the understanding damp down the sensibility.

It may be thought that I lay too much stress on personal reserve; but reserve is always the handmaid of modesty. So that if I were to name the graces that ought to adorn beauty, I should exclaim at once: cleanliness, neatness and personal reserve. It is obvious, I suppose, that the reserve I mean has nothing to do with sex, and that I think it equally necessary in both. So necessary indeed is that reserve, and that cleanliness which indolent women too often neglect, that I will venture to say that where two or three women live in the same house, the one who is most respected by the men of the family living with them — leaving love entirely out of the question — will be the one who pays this kind of habitual respect to her own person.

When friends who live together meet in the morning, an affectionate seriousness naturally prevails, especially if each is looking forward to the duties of the day. It may be reckoned fanciful, but this feeling has often risen unbidden in my mind. After breathing the sweet bracing morning air I have been pleased to see the same freshness in the faces I particularly loved; I was glad to see them braced, as it were, for the day, and ready to run their course with the sun. The greetings of affection in the morning are in this way more respectful than the familiar tenderness that so often prolongs the evening's talk. Indeed I have often felt hurt, not to say disgusted, when a friend I parted from fully dressed the evening before appeared with her clothes huddled on, because she had chosen to indulge herself in bed until the last moment.

Domestic affection can only be kept alive by these neglected attentions. Yet if men and women took half as much trouble to be habitually neat as they take to ornament, or rather to disfigure, their persons, a great deal would be done towards attaining purity of mind. But women only dress to gratify men of gallantry; a lover is always best pleased with the simple garment that sits close to the shape. There is an impertinence about ornament that repels affection, because love always clings round the idea of home.

As a sex, women are habitually indolent, and everything conspires to make them so. I do not forget the starts of activity that sensibility produces; but as these flights of feeling only increase the evil, they must not be confused with the slow, orderly walk of reason. So great in reality is their indolence of body and mind that until the body is strengthened and the understanding enlarged by active exertion there is little reason to expect modesty to take the place of bashfulness. They may find it prudent to assume the appearance of it; but the fair veil will only be worn on gala days.

Perhaps there is no virtue that mixes so kindly with every other as modesty. It is the pale moonbeam that makes every virtue it softens more interesting, giving a mild grandeur to a narrow horizon. Nothing could be more beautiful than the poetic fiction that makes Diana, with her silver crescent, the goddess of chastity. I have sometimes thought that as she wandered with sedate step in some lonely place, a modest lady of antiquity must have felt a glow of conscious dignity when, after contemplating the soft shadowy landscape, she invited with placid fervour the mild reflection of her sister's beams to turn towards her own chaste breast.
