A Christian has still nobler motives for preserving her chastity and acquiring modesty, for her body has been called the temple of the living God — of that God who requires more than a modest bearing. His eye searches the heart; and let her remember that if she hopes to find favour in the sight of purity itself, her chastity must be founded on modesty and not on worldly prudence, or truly a good reputation will be her only reward. For that awful intercourse, that sacred communion which virtue establishes between a human being and its Maker must give rise to the wish to be as pure as he is pure.

After these remarks it is almost superfluous to add that I consider all those feminine airs of maturity that succeed bashfulness to be immodest — the airs to which truth is sacrificed in order to secure a husband's heart, or rather to force him to go on being a lover when nature, if she had not been interrupted, would have made love give place to friendship. The tenderness a man feels for the mother of his children is an excellent substitute for the ardour of unsatisfied passion; but to prolong that ardour it is indelicate, not to say immodest, for a woman to feign an unnatural coldness of constitution. Women, like men, ought to have the common appetites and passions of their nature; those are only brutal when they are unchecked by reason, and the obligation to check them is a human duty, not a duty of one sex. In these respects nature may safely be left to herself. Let women only acquire knowledge and humanity, and love will teach them modesty. There is no need of falsehoods as disgusting as they are futile, for studied rules of behaviour only impose on shallow observers; a man of sense soon sees through the affectation and despises it.

The behaviour of young people towards each other as men and women is the last thing that should be thought about in education. In fact behaviour is now so much thought about in most circumstances that simplicity of character is rarely to be seen. Yet if men were only anxious to cultivate each virtue and let it take firm root in the mind, the grace that results from it, which is its natural outward mark, would soon strip affectation of its flaunting plumes; for conduct that is not founded on truth is as fallacious as it is unstable.

Footnote: The behaviour of many newly married women has often disgusted me. They seem anxious never to let their husbands forget the privilege of marriage, and to find no pleasure in his company unless he is playing the lover. The reign of love must indeed be short when the flame is constantly blown up like that without being given any solid fuel.

If you would really possess modesty, O my sisters, you must remember that the possession of virtue of any kind is incompatible with ignorance and vanity. You must acquire that soberness of mind which only the exercise of duties and the pursuit of knowledge can inspire, or you will still be left in a doubtful, dependent situation, and be loved only while you are beautiful. The downcast eye, the rosy blush, the retiring grace are all proper in their season; but modesty, being the child of reason, cannot exist for long alongside a sensibility untempered by reflection. Besides, when love, even innocent love, is the whole employment of your lives, your hearts will be too soft to give modesty that tranquil retreat where she loves to live, in close union with humanity.

Chapter Eight: How Morality Is Undermined by Ideas About a Woman's Reputation

It occurred to me long ago that the advice about behaviour, and all the various ways of preserving a good reputation, which have been so strenuously drummed into the female world, were plausible poisons that encrusted morality and ate away its substance — and that this measuring of shadows produced a false calculation, since a shadow's length depends so much on the height of the sun and on other accidental circumstances.

Where does a courtier's easy, deceptive manner come from? From his situation, undoubtedly; for, needing dependants, he is obliged to learn the art of refusing without giving offence, and of feeding hope evasively on the chameleon's diet of air. So politeness plays with truth, and, eating away the sincerity and humanity natural to a man, produces the fine gentleman.

Women in the same way acquire, from a supposed necessity, an equally artificial way of behaving. Yet truth is not to be played with unpunished, for the practised dissembler ends by becoming the dupe of his own arts. He loses that shrewdness which has justly been called common sense — a quick perception of the common truths that an unsophisticated mind always accepts as truths, even if it would not have had energy enough to discover them for itself when they were obscured by local prejudice. Most people take their opinions on trust to save themselves the trouble of exercising their own minds, and these indolent beings naturally cling to the letter rather than the spirit of a law, divine or human. "Women," says an author whose name I cannot recall, "do not mind what only heaven sees." And why indeed should they? It is the eye of man they have been taught to dread; and if they can lull their Argus to sleep they seldom think about heaven or about themselves, because their reputation is safe. And it is reputation, not chastity with all its fair train, that they are employed in keeping spotless — not as a virtue, but to preserve their station in the world.

To prove the truth of this remark I need only point to the intrigues of married women, particularly in high life, and in countries where women are married off suitably to their respective ranks by their parents. If an innocent girl falls prey to love, she is degraded forever, though her mind has not been polluted by the arts that married women practise under the convenient cloak of marriage, and though she has violated no duty except the duty of respecting herself. The married woman, on the contrary, breaks a most sacred engagement, and becomes a cruel mother when she becomes a false and faithless wife. If her husband still has any affection for her, the arts she must practise to deceive him will make her the most contemptible of human beings; and in any case the contrivances necessary to keep up appearances will keep her mind in that childish or vicious turmoil which destroys all its energy. Besides, in time, like people who habitually take cordials to raise their spirits, she will need an intrigue to give life to her thoughts, having lost all relish for pleasures that are not highly seasoned with hope or fear.

Sometimes married women behave still more audaciously. I will mention an instance.

A woman of quality, notorious for her gallantries, though nobody chose to place her in the class where she belonged since she still lived with her husband, made a point of treating with the most insulting contempt a poor timid creature who was abashed by the memory of her own former weakness, and whom a neighbouring gentleman had seduced and afterwards married. This woman had actually confused virtue with reputation; and I do believe she prided herself on the propriety of her behaviour before marriage, though once she was settled to her family's satisfaction, she and her lord were equally faithless — so that the half-alive heir to an immense estate came from heaven knows where.

To look at the subject in another light.

I have known a number of women who, if they did not love their husbands, loved nobody else either, giving themselves up entirely to vanity and dissipation and neglecting every domestic duty — even squandering money that should have been saved for their helpless younger children — and who yet plumed themselves on an unsullied reputation, as though the whole compass of their duty as wives and mothers were simply to preserve it. Other indolent women, neglecting every personal duty, have thought they deserved their husbands' affection because in this one respect they behaved with propriety.

Weak minds are always fond of resting in the ceremonies of duty, but morality offers much simpler motives; and it would have been better if superficial moralists had said less about behaviour and outward observances. For unless virtue of any kind is built on knowledge, it will produce nothing but a kind of insipid decency. Respect for the opinion of the world has, however, been called a woman's chief duty in so many words, for Rousseau declares "that reputation is no less indispensable than chastity". "A man," he adds, "secure in his own good conduct, depends only on himself and may brave public opinion; but a woman in behaving well performs only half her duty, since what is thought of her is as important to her as what she really is. It follows that the system of a woman's education should in this respect be directly contrary to ours. Opinion is the grave of virtue among men, but its throne among women." It is strictly logical to infer that a virtue resting on opinion is merely worldly, and that it is the virtue of a being to whom reason has been denied. But even as far as the opinion of the world goes, I am convinced that this class of reasoners is mistaken.

This regard for reputation, quite apart from being one of the natural rewards of virtue, took its rise from a cause I have already deplored as the grand source of female depravity: the impossibility of regaining respectability by returning to virtue, while men keep theirs throughout the indulgence of vice. It was natural, then, for women to try to preserve what, once lost, was lost forever, until this one care swallowed up every other, and a reputation for chastity became the one thing needful. But the scrupulousness of ignorance is vain; for neither religion nor virtue, when they live in the heart, requires such childish attention to mere ceremonies, since behaviour must on the whole be proper when the motive is pure.

I can produce very respectable authority in support of my opinion; and the authority of a cool reasoner ought to carry weight enough to enforce consideration, if not to establish a sentiment. Speaking of the general laws of morality, Dr. Smith observes: "By some very extraordinary and unlucky circumstance a good man may come to be suspected of a crime he was altogether incapable of, and may on that account be most unjustly exposed for the rest of his life to the horror and aversion of humankind. By an accident of this kind he may be said to lose everything, in spite of his integrity and justice, in the same way that a cautious man may be ruined by an earthquake or a flood in spite of the utmost circumspection. Accidents of the first kind, however, are perhaps rarer still, and still more contrary to the common course of things, than those of the second; and it remains true that the practice of truth, justice and humanity is a certain and almost infallible method of acquiring what those virtues chiefly aim at, the confidence and love of the people we live among. A person may easily be misrepresented with regard to a particular action; but it is scarcely possible that he should be misrepresented with regard to the general tenor of his conduct. An innocent man may be believed to have done wrong; this, however, will rarely happen. On the contrary, the established opinion of the innocence of his conduct will often lead us to acquit him where he really has been at fault, in spite of very strong presumptions."

I entirely agree with this writer, for I truly believe that few people of either sex have ever been despised for certain vices without deserving to be. I am not speaking of the slander of the moment, which hangs over a character like one of the dense November fogs over this city until it gradually clears in the common light of day. I only maintain that the daily conduct of most people stamps their character with the impression of truth. Quietly the clear light, shining day after day, refutes the ignorant guess or the malicious tale that has thrown dirt on a pure character. A false light distorted its shadow, reputation, for a short time; but the shadow seldom fails to become true again once the cloud that caused the trick of vision has dispersed.

Many people, undoubtedly, obtain a better reputation in various respects than, strictly speaking, they deserve, for unremitting industry will generally reach its goal in every kind of race. Those who strive only for this paltry prize, like the Pharisees who prayed at the street corners to be seen by men, do truly get the reward they were after; for the heart of one human being cannot be read by another. Still, the fair fame that is naturally reflected by good actions, when a man is only concerned to direct his own steps rightly and pays no attention to onlookers, is generally not only truer but surer.

There are trials, it is true, in which a good man must appeal to God against the injustice of men, and, amid the whining candour or the hissing of envy, must build a pavilion in his own mind to retire into until the rumour has passed over. The darts of undeserved censure may indeed pierce an innocent, tender heart through with many sorrows. But these are all exceptions to the general rule, and it is by the common laws that human behaviour ought to be regulated. The eccentric orbit of a comet never affects astronomical calculations about the unvarying order established in the motion of the principal bodies of the solar system.

I will venture to say, then, that once a man has reached maturity, the general outline of his character in the world is a just one, allowing for the exceptions I have mentioned. I do not say that a prudent, worldly-wise man with nothing but negative virtues and qualities may not sometimes get a smoother reputation than a wiser or a better man. So far from it, that I am inclined to conclude from experience that where two people's virtue is roughly equal, the more negative character will be better liked by the world at large, while the other may have more friends in private life. But the hills and valleys, the clouds and sunshine so conspicuous in the virtues of great men set each other off; and though they give envious weakness a fairer mark to shoot at, the real character will still work its way into the light, however bespattered by weak affection or ingenious malice.

Footnote: I have various biographical writings in mind, but particularly Boswell's Life of Johnson.

As for the anxiety to preserve a hard-earned reputation, which leads shrewd people to analyse it, I shall not make the obvious comment; but I am afraid that morality is very insidiously undermined in the female world by attention being turned to the show instead of the substance. A simple thing is thus made strangely complicated; indeed, virtue and its shadow are sometimes set at odds. We should probably never have heard of Lucretia if she had died to preserve her chastity instead of her reputation. If we really deserve our own good opinion we shall generally be respected in the world; but if we long for higher improvement and higher attainments, it is not enough to see ourselves as we suppose others see us, though this has been ingeniously argued to be the foundation of our moral sentiments — because every bystander has his own prejudices, besides the prejudices of his age and country. We should rather try to see ourselves as we suppose that Being sees us who watches each thought ripen into action, and whose judgment never swerves from the eternal rule of right. Righteous are all his judgments, and as just as they are merciful.

Footnote: Smith.

The humble mind that seeks to find favour in his sight, and calmly examines its conduct when only his presence is felt, will seldom form a very mistaken opinion of its own virtues. In the still hour of self-collection, the angry brow of offended justice will be fearfully deprecated, or else the tie that draws a human being to the Deity will be recognized in that pure sentiment of reverential adoration which swells the heart without stirring up any tumultuous emotion. In these solemn moments a man discovers in himself the germ of the vices that, like the Java tree, shed a pestilential vapour all round them — death is in the shade! — and he sees them without abhorrence, because he feels himself drawn by some cord of love to all his fellow creatures, whose follies he is anxious to find every excuse for in their nature, and in his own. If I, he may argue, who exercise my own mind and have been refined by tribulation, find the serpent's egg in some fold of my own heart and crush it only with difficulty, shall I not pity those who were stamped with less vigour, or who heedlessly nursed the insidious reptile until it poisoned the vital stream it fed on? Can I, conscious of my own secret sins, cast off my fellow creatures and watch calmly while they drop into the chasm of perdition that yawns to receive them? No, no. The agonized heart will cry out with suffocating impatience: I too am human, and have vices, hidden perhaps from every human eye, that bend me to the dust before God, and that tell me loudly when everything else is silent that we are formed of the same earth and breathe the same air. Humanity rises naturally out of humility in this way, and twists the cords of love that wind about the heart in so many convolutions.

This sympathy reaches further still, until a man is well pleased to see force in arguments that carry no conviction to his own breast, and gladly puts in the fairest light for himself the shows of reason that have led others astray, glad to find some reason in all the errors of humankind, though he was convinced already that He who rules the day makes his sun shine on everyone. Yet even while shaking hands, as it were, with corruption, with one foot on the earth, the other mounts with bold strides to heaven and claims kinship with higher natures. Virtues unnoticed by men drop their balmy fragrance at this cool hour, and the thirsty land, refreshed by the pure streams of comfort that suddenly gush out, is crowned with smiling green. This is the living green on which that eye may look with pleasure which is too pure to look on iniquity.

But my spirits are flagging, and I must silently indulge the reverie these reflections lead to, unable to describe the feelings that have calmed my soul when, watching the rising sun, a soft shower drizzling through the leaves of the neighbouring trees seemed to fall on my languid but tranquil spirits, cooling a heart that had been heated by the passions reason was labouring to tame.

The leading principles running through all my discussions would make it unnecessary to enlarge on this subject, if a constant attention to keeping the varnish of the character fresh and in good condition were not so often taught as the sum total of a woman's duty, and if rules for regulating the behaviour and preserving the reputation did not so frequently supersede moral obligations. But where reputation is concerned, all the attention is confined to a single virtue: chastity. If a woman's honour, as it is absurdly called, is safe, she may neglect every social duty, and even ruin her family by gambling and extravagance, and still present a shameless front — for truly she is an honourable woman.

Mrs. Macaulay has justly observed that "there is only one fault a woman of honour may not commit with impunity." She then adds, justly and humanely: "This has given rise to the trite and foolish observation that a woman's first fault against chastity has a radical power to deprave her character. But no such frail beings come out of the hands of nature. The human mind is built of nobler materials than to be corrupted so easily; and with all their disadvantages of situation and education, women seldom become entirely abandoned until they are thrown into a state of desperation by the venomous rancour of their own sex."

But in proportion as this regard for a reputation of chastity is prized by women, it is despised by men; and the two extremes are equally destructive to morality.

Men are certainly more under the influence of their appetites than women, and their appetites are more depraved by unbridled indulgence and by the fastidious contrivances of satiety. Luxury has introduced a refinement in eating that destroys the constitution, and a degree of gluttony so beastly that all sense of seemliness must be worn away before one human being could eat immoderately in the presence of another and afterwards complain of the oppression his own intemperance naturally produced. Some women, and Frenchwomen particularly, have also lost their sense of decency here, for they will talk very calmly about an indigestion. It is to be wished that idleness were not allowed to breed on the rank soil of wealth those swarms of summer insects that feed on putrefaction; then we should not be disgusted by the sight of such brutal excesses.

There is one rule about behaviour that I think ought to govern every other, and it is simply to cherish such a habitual respect for humankind as will keep us from disgusting a fellow creature for the sake of some present indulgence. The shameful indolence of many married women, and of others a little advanced in life, often leads them to sin against delicacy. For though they are convinced that the person is the bond of union between the sexes, how often, out of sheer indolence or to enjoy some trifling indulgence, do they disgust?

The depravity of the appetite that brings the sexes together has had a still more fatal effect. Nature must always be the standard of taste, the gauge of appetite — and yet how grossly the voluptuary insults nature. Leaving the refinements of love out of the question, nature, by making the gratification of this appetite, like every other, a natural and imperious law for preserving the species, exalts the appetite and mixes a little mind and affection with a sensual relish. The feelings of a parent, mingling with an instinct that is merely animal, give it dignity; and, since the man and the woman often meet on the child's account, a mutual interest and affection is excited by the exercise of a common sympathy. Women, then, having some duty to fulfil nobler than adorning their persons, would not be content to be the slaves of a casual appetite, which is the present situation of a very considerable number of them who are, quite literally, standing dishes that any glutton may help himself from.

I may be told that, great as this enormity is, it affects only a devoted portion of my sex — devoted for the salvation of the rest. But quite apart from the falsity, easily proved, of any assertion that recommends sanctioning a small evil to produce a greater good, the mischief does not stop there. The moral character and peace of mind of the chaster part of the sex is undermined by the conduct of the very women they allow no refuge from guilt; whom they inexorably consign to practising the arts that lure their husbands from them, debauch their sons, and force them — let no modest woman start at this — to take on something of the same character themselves. For I will venture to assert that all the causes of female weakness, and of female depravity, that I have already enlarged on branch out of one grand cause: the want of chastity in men.

This intemperance, so prevalent, depraves the appetite to such a degree that a wanton stimulus is needed to rouse it; but nature's parental design is forgotten, and the mere person, and that only for a moment, engrosses the thoughts. Indeed the lustful prowler often grows so voluptuous that he refines on female softness.

To satisfy this taste in men, women are made systematically voluptuous; and though they may not all carry their libertinism to the same length, this heartless intercourse that men allow themselves with the sex depraves both sexes, because men's taste is vitiated, and women of every class naturally square their behaviour to gratify the taste through which they obtain pleasure and power. Women, consequently becoming weaker in mind and body than they ought to be if one of the grand ends of their being were taken into account, the bearing and nursing of children, have not strength enough to discharge a mother's first duty; and, sacrificing to lasciviousness the parental affection that ennobles instinct, they either destroy the embryo in the womb or cast it off once it is born. Nature demands respect in everything, and those who violate her laws seldom violate them with impunity. The weak, enervated women who particularly catch a libertine's attention are unfit to be mothers though they may conceive; so that the rich sensualist who has rioted among women, spreading depravity and misery, receives from his wife, when he wishes to perpetuate his name, nothing but a half-formed being that inherits both its father's and its mother's weakness.

In contrasting the humanity of the present age with the barbarism of antiquity, great stress has been laid on the savage custom of exposing children whom their parents could not maintain; while the man of sensibility who perhaps makes the complaint produces by his promiscuous amours a most destructive barrenness and a contagious viciousness of manners. Surely nature never intended that women, by satisfying an appetite, should defeat the very purpose that appetite was implanted for.

I have observed before that men ought to maintain the women they have seduced; this would be one means of reforming female manners and stopping an abuse that has an equally fatal effect on population and on morals. Another, no less obvious, would be to turn women's attention to the real virtue of chastity. For a woman has little claim to respect on the score of modesty, though her reputation may be white as driven snow, if she smiles on the libertine while she spurns the victims of his lawless appetites and their own folly.

Besides, pure as she thinks herself, she has a taint of the same folly when she studiously adorns her person only to be looked at by men, to excite respectful sighs and all the idle homage of what is called innocent gallantry. If women really respected virtue for its own sake, they would not look for compensation in vanity for the self-denial they must practise to preserve their reputation, nor would they keep company with men who defy reputation altogether.

The two sexes mutually corrupt and mutually improve each other. I believe this to be an indisputable truth, and I extend it to every virtue. Chastity, modesty, public spirit and all the noble train of virtues on which social virtue and happiness are built should be understood and cultivated by everyone, or they will be cultivated to little effect. And instead of giving the vicious or the idle a pretext for violating some sacred duty by calling it a duty of one sex only, it would be wiser to show that nature has made no such difference — that the unchaste man doubly defeats the purpose of nature, by making women barren and destroying his own constitution, though he escapes the shame that pursues the same crime in the other sex. Those are the physical consequences; the moral ones are more alarming still. For virtue is only a name when the duties of citizens, husbands, wives, fathers, mothers and heads of families become nothing but the selfish ties of convenience.

Why, then, do philosophers look for public spirit? Public spirit must be nourished by private virtue, or it will be like that artificial sentiment which makes women careful of their reputation and men careful of their honour — a sentiment that often exists without any virtue to support it, and without that sublime morality which makes the habitual breach of one duty a breach of the whole moral law.

Chapter Nine: The Harm Done by the Unnatural Distinctions Society Establishes

From the respect paid to property flow, as from a poisoned spring, most of the evils and vices that make this world such a dreary scene to a contemplative mind. For it is in the most polished society that noisome reptiles and venomous serpents lurk under the rank grass; and there voluptuousness is pampered by the still, sultry air, which relaxes every good disposition before it can ripen into virtue.

One class presses on another, since all are aiming to win respect on account of their property; and property, once gained, will procure the respect that is only due to talents and virtue. Men neglect the duties that belong to them as men and are treated like demigods all the same; religion too is separated from morality by a ceremonial veil — and then men wonder that the world is almost literally a den of sharpers or of oppressors.

There is a homely proverb that speaks a shrewd truth: whoever the devil finds idle, he will employ. And what but habitual idleness can hereditary wealth and titles produce? For a man is so constituted that he can only come to a proper use of his faculties by exercising them, and he will not exercise them unless necessity of some kind first sets the wheels in motion. Virtue likewise can only be acquired by discharging the duties we owe to one another; but the importance of these sacred duties will hardly be felt by a being who has been flattered out of his humanity by sycophants. There must be more equality established in society, or morality will never gain ground; and this virtuous equality will not stand firm even when it is founded on a rock, if one half of humankind is chained to the bottom of it by fate, for they will be continually undermining it out of ignorance or pride.

It is vain to expect virtue from women until they are to some degree independent of men. It is vain, indeed, to expect that strength of natural affection which would make them good wives and good mothers. While they are absolutely dependent on their husbands they will be cunning, mean and selfish; and men who can be gratified by the fawning fondness of a spaniel-like affection have not much delicacy, for love is not to be bought in any sense of the word — its silken wings shrivel up the instant anything but a return in kind is looked for. Yet while wealth enervates men, and women live, as it were, by their personal charms, how can we expect either to discharge those ennobling duties that require exertion and self-denial alike? Hereditary property sophisticates the mind, and its unfortunate victims, if I may put it that way, swaddled from their birth, seldom exert the locomotive faculty of body or of mind. Seeing everything through one medium, and that a false one, they cannot make out where true merit and happiness lie. False indeed the light must be, when the drapery of a situation hides the man and makes him stalk about in masquerade, dragging from one scene of dissipation to another the nerveless limbs that hang in stupid listlessness, and rolling the vacant eye that tells us plainly there is nobody at home.

I mean to infer, therefore, that any society is improperly organized which does not compel men and women to discharge their respective duties by making that the only road to the good opinion of their fellow creatures, which every human being wants to gain somehow. The respect paid to wealth and to mere personal charms is consequently a true north-east wind, blighting the tender blossoms of affection and virtue. Nature has wisely attached affections to duties, to sweeten labour and to give the exertions of reason the vigour that only the heart can give. But an affection put on merely because it is the badge assigned to a certain character, while its duties go unfulfilled, is one of those empty compliments that vice and folly are obliged to pay to virtue and to the real nature of things.

To illustrate my opinion I need only observe that when a woman is admired for her beauty and lets herself be so far intoxicated by the admiration that she neglects the indispensable duty of a mother, she sins against herself by neglecting to cultivate an affection that would equally tend to make her useful and happy. True happiness — by which I mean all the contentment and virtuous satisfaction that can be snatched in this imperfect state — must arise from well-regulated affections, and every affection includes a duty. Men are not aware of the misery they cause, and the vicious weakness they encourage, by urging women to do nothing but make themselves pleasing. They do not consider that they thus set natural and artificial duties clashing, sacrificing the comfort and respectability of a woman's whole life to voluptuous notions of beauty, when in nature all of it harmonizes.

Cold must be the heart of a husband, unless early debauchery has made him unnatural, who does not feel more delight in seeing his child nursed by its mother than the most artful wanton tricks could ever raise in him. Yet wealth leads women to spurn this natural way of cementing the marriage tie and twining esteem in with fonder recollections. To preserve their beauty and wear the flowery crown of the day, which gives them a sort of right to reign for a short time, they neglect to stamp impressions on their husbands' hearts that would be remembered with more tenderness, when the snow on the head began to chill the breast, than even their virgin charms. The maternal solicitude of a reasonable, affectionate woman is a most affecting thing, and the chastened dignity with which a mother returns the caresses that she and her child receive from a father who has been fulfilling the serious duties of his station is not only a respectable but a beautiful sight. So peculiar are my feelings — and I have tried not to catch any artificial ones — that after being wearied by the sight of insipid grandeur, and of the slavish ceremonies that supply the place of domestic affection with cumbrous pomp, I have turned to some other scene to relieve my eye by resting it on the refreshing green nature scatters everywhere. I have looked with pleasure then at a woman nursing her children and discharging the duties of her station with, perhaps, only one servant to take the drudgery of the household off her hands. I have seen her prepare herself and her children, with no luxury but cleanliness, to receive her husband, who came home weary in the evening to find smiling babies and a clean hearth. My heart has loitered among the group, and has even throbbed with sympathetic emotion when the scraping of the well-known foot outside raised a pleasing tumult.

While my benevolence was gratified by contemplating this artless picture, I have thought that a couple of this description, equally necessary to and independent of each other because each fulfilled the duties of their station, possessed everything life can give. Raised far enough above abject poverty not to have to weigh the consequence of every farthing they spend, and with enough not to be driven to the frigid system of economy that narrows heart and mind together. My conceptions are so vulgar, I confess, that I do not know what more is wanted to make this the happiest as well as the most respectable situation in the world, except a taste for literature to throw a little variety and interest into their conversation, and some spare money to give to the needy and to buy books with. For it is not pleasant, when the heart is opened by compassion and the head busy arranging plans of usefulness, to have a prim little imp forever twitching your elbow back to keep your hand from drawing out an almost empty purse, and whispering some prudential maxim about justice coming first.

Destructive as riches and inherited honours are to the human character, women are if possible more debased and cramped by them than men, because men may still unfold their faculties to some degree by becoming soldiers and statesmen.

As soldiers, I grant, they can now mostly gather nothing but vainglorious laurels, while they adjust the European balance to a hair, taking particular care that no bleak northern corner or strait tips the beam. But the days of true heroism are over, when a citizen fought for his country like a Fabricius or a Washington and then went back to his farm to let his virtuous fervour run in a more placid but no less wholesome stream. No: our British heroes come more often from the gaming table than from the plough, and their passions have been inflamed by hanging in dumb suspense on the turn of a die rather than sublimated by panting after the adventurous march of virtue in the pages of history.

The statesman, it is true, might more properly leave the faro bank or the card table to take the helm, for he has still only to shuffle and to trick. The whole system of British politics, if it may courteously be called a system, consists in multiplying dependants and contriving taxes that grind the poor to pamper the rich; so a war, or any wild goose chase, is what common speech calls a lucky turn-up of patronage for the minister, whose chief merit is the art of keeping himself in place.

It is not necessary, then, that he should have any bowels for the poor, so long as he can secure the odd trick for his family. Or if some show of respect for what is called with ignorant ostentation an Englishman's birthright happens to be expedient, in order to fool the gruff mastiff he has to lead by the nose, he can make an empty show very safely by giving his own single vote and letting his light squadron file off to the other side. And when a question of humanity is raised, he may dip a sop in the milk of human kindness to silence Cerberus, and talk of the interest his heart takes in an attempt to stop the earth crying out for vengeance as it drinks in its children's blood — while his cold hand at that very moment rivets their chains by sanctioning the abominable trade. A minister is a minister only so long as he can carry a point he is determined to carry. Yet it is not necessary for a minister to feel like a man, when a bold push might shake his seat.

But to have done with these episodes, let me come back to the more plausible slavery that chains the very soul of woman, keeping her forever in the bondage of ignorance.

The preposterous distinctions of rank, which make civilization a curse by dividing the world between voluptuous tyrants and cunning, envious dependants, corrupt almost every class of people equally; because respectability is attached not to discharging the duties of life but to the station, and when the duties are not fulfilled, the affections cannot gain strength enough to fortify the virtue they are the natural reward of. Still, there are a few loopholes a man may creep out of and dare to think and act for himself; but for a woman it is a herculean task, because she has difficulties peculiar to her sex to overcome, which take almost superhuman powers.

A truly benevolent legislator always tries to make it every individual's interest to be virtuous; and so, private virtue becoming the cement of public happiness, an orderly whole is consolidated by the tendency of all the parts towards a common centre. But the private or public virtue of women is very doubtful, for Rousseau and a long list of male writers insist that she should be subject all her life to a severe restraint, the restraint of propriety. Why subject her to propriety — blind propriety — if she is capable of acting from a nobler spring, if she is an heir of immortality? Is sugar always to be produced by human blood? Is one half of the human species, like the enslaved Africans, to be subject to prejudices that brutalize them, when principles would be a surer guard, and all so that the cup of man may be sweetened? Is this not an indirect way of denying women reason? For a gift is a mockery if it is unfit for use.

Women, like men, are made weak and luxurious by the relaxing pleasures wealth procures; but besides that, they are made slaves to their persons, and must make themselves alluring so that a man may lend them his reason to guide their tottering steps aright. Or, if they are ambitious, they must govern their tyrants by underhand tricks; for where there are no rights there can be no duties either. The laws respecting women, which I mean to discuss in a later part, make an absurd unit of a man and his wife; and then, by the easy transition of considering only him as responsible, she is reduced to a mere cipher.

The being who discharges the duties of its station is independent; and, speaking of women at large, their first duty is to themselves as rational creatures, and the next in importance, as citizens, is the one that includes so many others, the duty of a mother. Any rank in life that excuses them from fulfilling this duty necessarily degrades them by turning them into dolls. Or, if they do turn to something more important than fitting drapery on a smooth block, their minds are occupied by some soft platonic attachment; or the actual management of an intrigue may keep their thoughts moving. For when they neglect domestic duties they have no power to take the field and march and countermarch like soldiers, or to wrangle in the senate, to keep their faculties from rusting.

I know that Rousseau has exultingly exclaimed, as proof of the inferiority of the sex, how can they leave the nursery for the camp? And the camp has been called by some moralists the school of the most heroic virtues, though I think it would puzzle a keen casuist to prove the reasonableness of most of the wars that have dubbed men heroes. I do not mean to consider the question critically, because, having often looked on these freaks of ambition as the first natural mode of civilization, when the ground must be torn up and the woods cleared by fire and sword, I do not choose to call them pests. But surely the present system of war has little connection with virtue of any description, being rather the school of finesse and effeminacy than of fortitude.

Yet if defensive war, the only justifiable war in the present advanced state of society, where virtue can show its face and ripen amid the rigours that purify the air on a mountain top, were alone adopted as just and glorious, then the true heroism of antiquity might animate women's breasts again. But gently, gentle reader, male or female, do not alarm yourself; for though I have contrasted the character of a modern soldier with that of a civilized woman, I am not going to advise women to turn their distaffs into muskets, however sincerely I wish to see the bayonet turned into a pruning hook. I was only refreshing an imagination wearied with contemplating the vices and follies that all flow from a foul stream of wealth which has muddied the pure rills of natural affection — refreshing it by supposing that society may some day be so constituted that a man must fulfil the duties of a citizen or be despised, and that while he was employed in some department of civil life his wife, an active citizen too, would be equally intent on managing her family, educating her children and helping her neighbours.

But to make her really virtuous and useful, if she discharges her civil duties she must not be left without the protection of the civil laws as an individual; she must not depend on her husband's bounty for her subsistence during his life or her support after his death. For how can a being be generous who has nothing of her own, or virtuous who is not free? The wife who in the present state of things is faithful to her husband and neither nurses nor educates her children scarcely deserves the name of a wife, and has no right to the name of citizen. But take away natural rights, and duties are at an end.

Women infallibly become nothing but the wanton solace of men when they are so weak in mind and body that they can exert themselves only to chase some frothy pleasure or invent some frivolous fashion. What can be a more melancholy sight to a thinking mind than to look into the many carriages driving helter-skelter about this city in the morning, full of pale-faced creatures flying from themselves? I have often wished, with Dr. Johnson, to set some of them up in a little shop with half a dozen children looking up to their languid faces for support. I am much mistaken if some latent vigour would not soon give health and spirit to their eyes; and some lines drawn by the exercise of reason on those blank cheeks, which before were only dimpled, might restore lost dignity to the character, or rather let it attain the true dignity of its nature. Virtue is not to be acquired even by speculation, much less by the negative supineness that wealth naturally breeds.

Besides, when poverty is more disgraceful than even vice, is morality not cut to the quick? Still, to avoid being misunderstood: though I consider that women in the common walks of life are called by religion and reason to fulfil the duties of wives and mothers, I cannot help lamenting that women of a superior cast have no road open to them by which they could pursue wider plans of usefulness and independence. I may raise a laugh by dropping a hint I mean to pursue at some future time, for I really think that women ought to have representatives, instead of being arbitrarily governed with no direct share allowed them in the deliberations of government.

But since the whole system of representation in this country is now only a convenient handle for despotism, they need not complain, for they are as well represented as a numerous class of hard-working tradesmen who pay for the support of royalty when they can hardly stop their own children's mouths with bread. How are those people represented whose very sweat supports the splendid stable of an heir apparent, or varnishes the carriage of some female favourite who looks down on shame? Taxes on the very necessities of life enable an endless tribe of idle princes and princesses to pass in stupid pomp before a gaping crowd who almost worship the very parade that costs them so dear. This is mere gothic grandeur, something like the barbarous, useless parade of keeping mounted sentries at Whitehall, which I could never look at without a mixture of contempt and indignation.

How strangely the mind must be sophisticated when this sort of state impresses it! But until these monuments of folly are levelled by virtue, similar follies will leaven the whole mass; for the same character will prevail to some degree throughout society taken as a whole. The refinements of luxury, and the vicious repinings of envious poverty, will equally banish virtue from a society, considered as that society's characteristic, or else allow it to appear only as one of the stripes in the harlequin coat that civilized man wears.

In the higher ranks of life every duty is done by deputy, as if duties could ever be waived; and the vain pleasures that the resulting idleness forces the rich to pursue look so enticing to the rank below that the many scramblers after wealth sacrifice everything to tread on their heels. The most sacred trusts are then treated as sinecures, because they were got by influence and only sought so that a man might keep good company. Women in particular all want to be ladies — which is simply to have nothing to do but drift about, hardly caring where, because they cannot tell what for.

But what have women to do in society, I may be asked, except to loiter with an easy grace? Surely you would not condemn them all to suckle fools and chronicle small beer! No. Women might certainly study the art of healing and be physicians as well as nurses. And decency seems to allot midwifery to them, though I am afraid the word midwife will soon give place in our dictionaries to accoucheur, and one proof of the sex's former delicacy be rubbed out of the language.

They might also study politics and settle their benevolence on the broadest basis; for reading history will be hardly more useful than reading romances if it is read as mere biography, and if the character of the times, the political improvements, the arts and so on are not observed — in short, if it is not considered as the history of humanity, and not of the particular men who filled a niche in the temple of fame and then dropped into the black rolling stream of time that sweeps everything silently before it into the shapeless void called eternity. Though how can it be called a shape, that shape had none?

They might likewise go into various kinds of business if they were educated in a more orderly way, and that might save many of them from common and from legal prostitution. Women would not then marry for a living, as men accept places under government and then neglect the duties implied in them; nor would an attempt to earn their own subsistence — a most praiseworthy attempt — sink them almost to the level of the poor abandoned creatures who live by prostitution. For are milliners and dressmakers not reckoned the next class down? The few employments open to women, so far from being liberal, are menial; and when a superior education lets them take charge of children's education as governesses, they are not treated like the tutors of sons — though even clerical tutors are not always treated in a way calculated to make them respectable in their pupils' eyes, to say nothing of their private comfort. But since women educated as gentlewomen are never intended for the humiliating position necessity sometimes forces them into, such positions are regarded as a degradation; and anyone who has to be told that nothing sharpens the sensibility so painfully as such a fall in life knows very little of the human heart.

Some of these women may have been kept from marrying by a proper spirit or delicacy, and others may have had no chance of escaping in that pitiful way from servitude. Is any government not very defective, then, and very careless of the happiness of one half of its members, if it makes no provision for honest, independent women by encouraging them to fill respectable posts? But if their private virtue is to become a public benefit they must have a civil existence in the state, married or single; or we shall go on seeing some worthy woman, whose sensibility undeserved contempt has made painfully acute, droop like "the lily broken down by a ploughshare".

It is a melancholy truth — yet such are the blessed effects of civilization! — that the most respectable women are the most oppressed; and unless their understandings are far above the common run of understandings in either sex, they must become contemptible from being treated as contemptible beings. How many women waste their lives away as the prey of discontent, who might have practised as physicians, run a farm, managed a shop, and stood upright supported by their own industry, instead of hanging their heads surcharged with the dew of sensibility that consumes the very beauty it first gave lustre to! Indeed I doubt whether pity and love are such close kin as poets pretend, for I have seldom seen much compassion excited by the helplessness of women unless they happened to be beautiful — and then, perhaps, pity was the soft handmaid of love, or the harbinger of lust.

How much more respectable is the woman who earns her own bread by fulfilling some duty than the most accomplished beauty! Beauty, did I say? I am so alive to the beauty of moral loveliness, the harmonious propriety that tunes the passions of a well-regulated mind, that I blush at making the comparison. Yet I sigh to think how few women aim at that respectability by withdrawing from the giddy whirl of pleasure, or from the indolent calm that stupefies the good sort of women it draws in.

Proud of their weakness, however, they must always be protected, guarded from care and from all the rough labours that give the mind dignity. If this is the decree of fate, if they will make themselves insignificant and contemptible so as to waste life away sweetly, then let them not expect to be valued when their beauty fades; for it is the fate of the fairest flowers to be admired and then pulled to pieces by the careless hand that plucked them. In how many ways do I wish, out of the purest benevolence, to impress this truth on my sex! Yet I fear they will not listen to a truth that dear-bought experience has brought home to many an agitated heart, nor willingly give up the privileges of rank and sex for the privileges of humanity, which those who do not discharge its duties have no claim to.

Those writers are particularly useful, in my opinion, who make one human being feel for another, independently of the station he fills or the drapery of artificial sentiment. I would gladly convince reasonable men of the importance of some of my remarks, and persuade them to weigh the whole tenor of my observations dispassionately. I appeal to their understandings; and as a fellow creature I claim, in the name of my sex, some interest in their hearts. I beg them to help emancipate their companion — to make her a helpmate for them.

If men would only generously snap our chains and be content with a rational fellowship instead of slavish obedience, they would find us more observant daughters, more affectionate sisters, more faithful wives, more reasonable mothers — in a word, better citizens. We should then love them with a true affection, because we should have learned to respect ourselves; and a worthy man's peace of mind would not be disturbed by his wife's idle vanity, nor his babies sent to nestle at a stranger's breast because they never found a home at their mother's.

Chapter Ten: Parental Affection

Parental affection is perhaps the blindest form of a perverse self-love; for we do not have, as the French do, two separate terms to distinguish the pursuit of a natural and reasonable desire from the ignorant calculations of weakness. Parents often love their children in the most brutal way and sacrifice every other duty to push them forward in the world — to promote, such is the perversity of unprincipled prejudice, the future welfare of the very beings whose present lives they are embittering by the most despotic stretch of power. Power, in fact, is always true to its own vital principle, for in every shape it wants to reign without check or enquiry. Its throne is built across a dark abyss that no eye must dare to explore, in case the baseless structure should totter under investigation. Obedience, unconditional obedience, is the catchword of tyrants of every description; and, to make assurance doubly sure, one kind of despotism supports another. Tyrants would have cause to tremble if reason ever became the rule of duty in any of the relations of life, for the light might spread until perfect day appeared. And when it did appear, how men would smile at the bogeys they started at during the night of ignorance, or in the twilight of timid enquiry.

Footnote: L'amour propre, l'amour de soi-même.

Parental affection is in many minds nothing but a pretext for tyrannizing where it can be done with impunity; for only good and wise men are content with the kind of respect that will bear discussion. Convinced that they have a right to what they insist on, they do not fear reason, or dread having subjects that come back to natural justice sifted, because they firmly believe that the more enlightened the human mind becomes, the deeper root just and simple principles will take. They do not rest in expedients, or grant that what is metaphysically true can be practically false; disdaining the shifts of the moment, they wait calmly until time sanctions innovation and silences the hiss of selfishness or envy.

If the power of reflecting on the past, and of darting the keen eye of contemplation into the future, is humanity's grand privilege, it must be admitted that some people enjoy that prerogative in a very limited degree. Everything looks wrong to them; and, unable to distinguish the possible from the monstrous, they are afraid where no fear ought to find a place, running from the light of reason as though it were a firebrand. Yet the limits of the possible have never been fixed in a way that could stop a sturdy innovator's hand.

Woman, however, being a slave to prejudice in every situation, seldom exercises an enlightened maternal affection: she either neglects her children or spoils them with improper indulgence. Besides, the affection some women have for their children is, as I have called it before, often quite brutish, for it wipes out every spark of humanity. Justice, truth, everything is sacrificed by these Rebekahs, and for the sake of their own children they violate the most sacred duties, forgetting the common relationship that binds the whole human family together. Yet reason seems to say that anyone who lets one duty or one affection swallow up all the rest has not heart or mind enough to fulfil even that one conscientiously. It then loses the venerable look of a duty and takes on the fantastic shape of a whim.

Since the care of children in infancy is one of the grand duties nature attaches to women, that duty would supply many forcible arguments for strengthening the female understanding, if it were properly considered.

The formation of the mind must begin very early, and the temper in particular needs the most judicious attention — attention that women cannot give if they love their children only because they are their children, and look no further for the foundation of their duty than the feelings of the moment. It is this want of reason in their affections that makes women run so often into extremes, and be either the fondest or the most careless and unnatural of mothers.

To be a good mother, a woman must have sense, and that independence of mind which few women possess who have been taught to depend entirely on their husbands. Meek wives generally make foolish mothers: they want their children to love them best and to take their side in secret against the father, who is held up as a scarecrow. If the children are to be punished, though they have offended the mother, the father must inflict the punishment; he must be judge in every dispute. But I shall discuss this more fully when I come to private education. I only mean to insist here that unless a woman's understanding is enlarged, and her character made firmer by being allowed to govern her own conduct, she will never have sense enough, or command of temper enough, to manage her children properly. Her parental affection hardly deserves the name when it does not lead her to nurse her children herself; for discharging that duty is equally calculated to inspire maternal and filial affection, and it is the indispensable duty of men and women alike to fulfil the duties that give birth to the affections, which are the surest preservatives against vice. Natural affection, so called, I believe to be a very weak tie: affections must grow out of the habitual exercise of a mutual sympathy — and what sympathy does a mother exercise who sends her baby out to a nurse, and only takes it back from the nurse to send it away to school?

In the exercise of their natural feelings, providence has given women a natural substitute for love, for the time when the lover becomes only a friend and mutual confidence takes the place of overstrained admiration. A child then gently twists the slackening cord tight again, and a shared care produces a new shared sympathy. But a child, though it is a pledge of affection, will not enliven that affection if both father and mother are content to hand the charge over to hired help; for those who do their duty by proxy should not complain if they miss the reward of duty. Parental affection produces filial duty.

Chapter Eleven: Duty to Parents

There seems to be an indolent tendency in us to let long custom always take the place of reason, and to set every duty on an arbitrary foundation. The rights of kings are traced in a direct line from the King of kings, and the rights of parents from our first parent.

Why do we go back like this for principles that ought always to rest on the same base, and that have exactly the weight today they had a thousand years ago, and not a jot more? If parents discharge their duty they have a strong hold and a sacred claim on their children's gratitude; but few parents are willing to receive their children's respectful affection on those terms. They demand blind obedience because they do not deserve a reasonable service; and to make these demands of weakness and ignorance more binding, a mysterious sanctity is spread round the most arbitrary principle in the world — for what else can you call the blind duty of obeying vicious or feeble people merely because they obeyed a powerful instinct?

The reciprocal duty that naturally exists between parent and child can be defined simply, in a few words. The parent who pays proper attention to helpless infancy has a right to require the same attention when the feebleness of age comes on. But to subject a rational being to the mere will of another, once he is old enough to answer to society for his own conduct, is a most cruel and improper stretch of power, and perhaps as damaging to morality as those religious systems which allow right and wrong no existence except in the Divine will.

I have never known a parent who paid more than common attention to his children to be disregarded. On the contrary, the early habit of relying almost implicitly on the opinion of a respected parent is not easily shaken, even when mature reason convinces the child that his father is not the wisest man in the world. This weakness — for a weakness it is, though the epithet amiable is often tacked onto it — a reasonable man must steel himself against. For the absurd duty, too often taught, of obeying a parent merely because he is a parent shackles the mind and prepares it for slavish submission to any power except reason.

Footnote: Dr. Johnson makes the same observation.

I distinguish between the natural duty owed to parents and the accidental one.

The parent who assiduously works to form the heart and enlarge the understanding of his child has given a duty common to the whole animal world the dignity that only reason can give. This is the parental affection of a human being, and it leaves instinctive natural affection far behind. Such a parent acquires all the rights of the most sacred friendship, and his advice, even when his child is well advanced in life, deserves serious consideration.

As for marriage, though after the age of twenty-one a parent seems to have no right to withhold consent on any account, still twenty years of solicitude call for some return; and the son ought at least to promise not to marry for two or three years if the object of his choice does not entirely meet with the approval of his first friend.

But respect for parents is generally a much more debasing principle than that; it is only a selfish respect for property. The father who is blindly obeyed is obeyed out of sheer weakness, or from motives that degrade the human character.

A great proportion of the misery that wanders about the world in hideous shapes is allowed to arise from the negligence of parents; and still these are the very people who cling hardest to what they call a natural right, though it undermines the birthright of every human being, the right of acting by the direction of one's own reason.

I have already had frequent occasion to observe that vicious or indolent people are always eager to profit by enforcing arbitrary privileges, and generally in exactly the proportion in which they neglect the duties that alone make those privileges reasonable. At bottom this is a dictate of common sense, or the instinct of self-defence peculiar to ignorant weakness — like the instinct that makes a fish muddy the water it swims in to escape its enemy, instead of facing him boldly in the clear stream.

From the clear stream of argument the supporters of long custom, of every description, do indeed fly; and, taking refuge in the darkness that sublime poetry has supposed to surround the throne of Omnipotence, they dare to demand the implicit respect that is due only to his unsearchable ways. But let me not be thought presumptuous: the darkness that hides God from us concerns only speculative truths. It never obscures moral ones. Those shine clearly, for God is light, and by the constitution of our nature he never requires the discharge of a duty whose reasonableness does not shine on us the moment we open our eyes.

The indolent parent of high rank may, it is true, extort a show of respect from his child; and on the continent women are particularly subject to the plans of their families, who never think of consulting their inclinations or providing for the comfort of the poor victims of their pride. The consequence is notorious. These dutiful daughters become adulteresses and neglect the education of their own children, from whom they in their turn exact the same kind of obedience.

Women in every country are, it is true, too much under the dominion of their parents; and few parents think of addressing their children in the following way, though this is the reasonable way in which Heaven itself seems to command the whole human race. It is in your interest to obey me until you can judge for yourself; and the Almighty Father of all has planted an affection in me to serve as a guard for you while your reason is unfolding. But when your mind reaches maturity you must obey me, or rather respect my opinions, only so far as they agree with the light that is breaking in on your own mind.

A slavish bondage to parents cramps every faculty of the mind; and Mr. Locke very judiciously observes that "if the mind is curbed and humbled too much in children, if their spirits are abased and broken by too strict a hand over them, they lose all their vigour and industry." This strict hand may to some degree account for the weakness of women; for girls, from various causes, are kept down by their parents more than boys, in every sense of the phrase. The duty expected of them, like all the duties arbitrarily imposed on women, comes more from a sense of propriety, more out of respect for decorum, than from reason; and so, taught to submit slavishly to their parents, they are prepared for the slavery of marriage. I may be told that a great many women are not slaves in the married state. True; but then they become tyrants, for what they get is not rational freedom but a lawless kind of power, resembling the authority wielded by the favourites of absolute monarchs, and obtained by debasing means. Nor do I dream of hinting that boys or girls are always slaves. I only insist that when they are obliged to submit to authority blindly, their faculties are weakened and their tempers made either imperious or abject. I lament too that parents, lazily availing themselves of a supposed privilege, damp the first faint glimmer of reason, and at the same time make the very duty they are so anxious to enforce an empty name, because they will not let it rest on the only basis a duty can rest on securely. For unless it is founded on knowledge it cannot gain strength enough to resist the squalls of passion, or the silent sapping of self-love. But it is not the parents who have given the surest proof of their affection for their children — or, to speak more properly, who by fulfilling their duty have let a natural parental affection take root in their hearts, the child of exercised sympathy and reason and not the overweening offspring of selfish pride — who insist most vehemently on their children submitting to their will merely because it is their will. On the contrary, the parent who sets a good example patiently lets that example work, and it seldom fails to produce its natural effect: filial respect.

Children cannot be taught too early to submit to reason, which is the true definition of that necessity Rousseau insisted on without ever defining it; for to submit to reason is to submit to the nature of things, and to the God who formed them so in order to promote our real interest.

Why should children's minds be warped just as they begin to expand, merely to suit the indolence of parents who insist on a privilege without being willing to pay the price nature has fixed for it? I have observed before that a right always includes a duty, and I think it may fairly be inferred as well that those who do not fulfil the duty forfeit the right.

It is easier, I grant, to command than to reason; but it does not follow that children cannot understand why they are made to do certain things habitually. For from a steady adherence to a few simple principles of conduct flows the wholesome power that a judicious parent gradually gains over a child's mind. And that power becomes strong indeed when it is tempered by a steady display of affection brought home to the child's heart. For I believe it must be allowed as a general rule that the affection we inspire always resembles the affection we cultivate; so that the natural affections, which have been supposed almost separate from reason, may turn out to be more closely connected with judgment than is commonly allowed. Indeed, as another proof of the necessity of cultivating the female understanding, it is only fair to observe that the affections seem to have a kind of animal capriciousness when they live in the heart alone.

It is the irregular exercise of parental authority that first injures the mind, and girls are more subject to these irregularities than boys. The will of people who never allow their will to be disputed, unless they happen to be in a good humour and relax proportionately, is almost always unreasonable. To get round this arbitrary authority, girls learn very early the lessons they afterwards practise on their husbands; for I have often seen a sharp-faced little miss rule a whole family, except when now and then mamma's anger bursts out of some accidental cloud — either her hair was badly dressed, or she had lost more money at cards the night before than she cared to admit to her husband, or some such moral cause of anger.

Footnote: I once heard a little girl say to a servant: "My mamma has been scolding me finely this morning, because her hair was not dressed to please her." The remark was pert, but it was just. And what respect could a girl feel for such a parent, without doing violence to reason?

After watching outbursts of this kind I have been led into a melancholy train of reflection about women, concluding that when their first affection must lead them astray, or set their duties clashing until those duties rest on nothing but whim and custom, little can be expected of them as they advance in life. And how is any instructor to remedy the evil? For to teach them virtue on any solid principle is to teach them to despise their parents. Children cannot, and ought not to, be taught to make allowances for their parents' faults, because every such allowance weakens the force of reason in their minds and makes them more indulgent still to their own. It is one of the most sublime virtues of maturity that leads us to be severe with ourselves and forbearing to others; but children should be taught only the simple virtues, for if they begin too early to make allowances for human passions and manners, they wear the fine edge off the standard by which they ought to regulate their own, and grow unjust in exactly the proportion in which they grow indulgent.

The affections of children, and of weak people, are always selfish: they love others because others love them, and not on account of their virtues. Yet until esteem and love are blended together in the first affection, and reason made the foundation of the first duty, morality will stumble on the threshold. But until society is very differently constituted, parents, I am afraid, will still insist on being obeyed because they will be obeyed, and will keep trying to settle on a Divine right a power that will not bear the investigation of reason.

Chapter Twelve: On National Education

The good effects of attending to private education will always be very limited, and the parent who really puts his own hand to the plough will always be disappointed to some degree, until education becomes a grand national concern. A man cannot retire into a desert with his child; and if he did, he could not bring himself back to childhood and become the proper friend and playfellow of an infant or a boy. And when children are confined to the society of men and women, they very soon acquire that premature manhood which stops the growth of every vigorous power of mind or body. To open their faculties they must be stirred up to think for themselves, and that can only be done by mixing a number of children together and making them pursue the same objects jointly.

A child very soon contracts a numbing indolence of mind, which he seldom has vigour enough to shake off, when he merely asks a question instead of looking for the information, and then relies implicitly on the answer he gets. With children of his own age this could never happen; and the subjects of enquiry, though they might be influenced, would not be entirely under the direction of men, who frequently damp abilities, if they do not destroy them, by bringing them forward too hastily — and too hastily they will infallibly be brought forward if the child is confined to the company of a man, however sagacious the man may be.

Besides, the seeds of every affection ought to be sown in youth, and the respectful regard felt for a parent is very different from the social affections that are to make the happiness of later life. Those rest on equality, and on an exchange of sentiments unclogged by the watchful seriousness which prevents argument even if it does not compel submission. However great a child's affection for his parent, he will always be longing to play and chatter with other children; and the very respect he feels — for filial esteem always has a dash of fear mixed in — will, if it does not teach him cunning, at least keep him from pouring out the little secrets that first open the heart to friendship and confidence and lead gradually to a wider benevolence. On top of that, he will never acquire the frank openness of manner that young people can only get by being often in company where they dare to say what they think, neither afraid of being reproved for presumption nor laughed at for folly.

Forcibly struck by the reflections that the sight of schools as they are presently conducted naturally suggests, I once gave my opinion rather warmly in favour of a private education; but further experience has led me to see the subject in a different light. I still think, however, that schools as they are now run are hotbeds of vice and folly, and that the knowledge of human nature supposed to be gained there is merely a cunning selfishness.

At school boys become gluttons and slovens and, instead of cultivating domestic affections, rush very early into the libertinism that destroys the constitution before it is formed, hardening the heart as it weakens the understanding.

I should in fact be against boarding schools if it were for no other reason than the unsettled state of mind that waiting for the holidays produces. The children's thoughts are fixed on them with eager anticipation for at least half the time, to put it moderately; and when the holidays come they are spent in complete dissipation and beastly indulgence.

But when children are brought up at home, though they may follow a plan of study more orderly than can be managed when nearly a quarter of the year is spent in idleness and as much again in regret and anticipation, they acquire too high an opinion of their own importance from being allowed to tyrannize over the servants, and from the anxiety most mothers show on the score of manners, who, eager to teach a gentleman's accomplishments, stifle a man's virtues in the bud. Brought into company when they ought to be seriously employed, and treated like men while they are still boys, they become vain and effeminate.

The only way to avoid two extremes equally damaging to morality would be to contrive some way of combining a public and a private education. Two natural steps might be taken to make men into citizens, and they seem to lead straight to the point: the domestic affections, which first open the heart to the various modifications of humanity, would be cultivated, while the children were nevertheless allowed to spend a great part of their time on equal terms with other children.

I still remember with pleasure the country day school, where a boy trudged in of a morning, wet or dry, carrying his books and his dinner if the distance was considerable. No servant led master by the hand then; once he had put on coat and breeches he was left to shift for himself and to come home alone in the evening to recount the day's exploits at his father's knee. His father's house was his home, and was fondly remembered ever after. Indeed, I appeal to certain superior men who were educated this way: has the memory of some shady lane where they conned their lesson, or of some stile where they sat making a kite or mending a bat, not endeared their country to them?

But what boy ever remembered with pleasure the years he spent in close confinement at an academy near London — unless he happened to recall the poor scarecrow of an usher he tormented, or the tart-seller he snatched a cake from to devour with the cattish appetite of selfishness? At boarding schools of every description, the recreation of the junior boys is mischief and of the senior boys vice. Besides, in the great schools what can be more damaging to the moral character than the system of tyranny and abject slavery established among the boys — to say nothing of the slavery to forms, which makes religion worse than a farce? For what good can be expected of a boy who takes the sacrament of the Lord's Supper to avoid forfeiting half a guinea, which he probably then spends in some sensual way? Half the boys' energy goes into evading the obligation to attend public worship — and well it may, for such constant repetition of the same thing must be a very irksome restraint on their natural liveliness. Since these ceremonies have the most fatal effect on their morals, and since a ritual performed by the lips while the heart and mind are far away is no longer stored up by our church as a bank to draw on for the fees of poor souls in purgatory, why should they not be abolished?

But the fear of innovation in this country extends to everything. It is only a covert fear, the apprehensive timidity of indolent slugs who guard by sliming it over the snug place they regard as a hereditary estate, and who eat, drink and enjoy themselves instead of fulfilling the duties, a few empty forms excepted, that the place was endowed for. These are the people who insist most strenuously that the founder's will must be observed, crying out against every reform as though it were a violation of justice. I am referring particularly to the relics of popery kept up in our colleges, where the Protestant members seem to be such sticklers for the established church — but their zeal never makes them lose sight of the spoils of ignorance that rapacious priests of superstitious memory scraped together. No: wise in their generation, they venerate the prescriptive right of possession as a stronghold, and still let the sluggish bell tinkle for prayers as it did in the days when the elevation of the host was supposed to atone for the sins of the people, in case one reformation should lead to another and the spirit kill the letter. These Roman customs have the most damaging effect on the morals of our clergy. The idle vermin who two or three times a day perform in the most slovenly fashion a service they think useless but call their duty soon lose any sense of duty at all.
