At college, forced either to attend public worship or to dodge it, they acquire a habitual contempt for the very service whose performance is to enable them to live in idleness. It is mumbled over as a piece of business, as a stupid boy repeats his lesson; and often the college slang escapes the preacher the moment he has left the pulpit, and even while he is eating the dinner he earned so dishonestly.

Nothing indeed could be more irreverent than the cathedral service as it is now performed in this country, nor does it contain a feebler set of men than the slaves of this childish routine. A disgusting skeleton of the former state is still exhibited, but all the solemnity that engaged the imagination, even if it did not purify the heart, has been stripped away. The performance of high mass on the continent must impress every mind where a spark of fancy glows with that awful melancholy, that sublime tenderness, so near akin to devotion. I do not say that these devotional feelings are of more moral use than any other emotion of taste; but I maintain that theatrical pomp which gratifies the senses is to be preferred to a cold parade that insults the understanding without ever reaching the heart.

Among remarks on national education such observations cannot be out of place, especially as the supporters of these establishments, degenerated into puerilities, claim to be the champions of religion. Religion, pure source of comfort in this vale of tears! How your clear stream has been muddied by the dabblers who have presumptuously tried to confine within one narrow channel the living waters that flow always towards God, the sublime ocean of existence! What would life be without the peace that only the love of God, when it is built on humanity, can give? Every earthly affection turns back at intervals to prey on the heart that feeds it; and the purest outpourings of benevolence, so often rudely damped by other people, must rise as a freewill offering to Him who gave them birth, and whose bright image they faintly reflect.

In public schools, however, religion, confounded with irksome ceremonies and unreasonable restraints, takes on its most ungracious aspect — not the sober austere one that commands respect while it inspires fear, but a ludicrous cast that serves to point a pun. For in fact most of the good stories and smart sayings that enliven spirits concentrated at cards are manufactured out of the very incidents that the men who countenance the abuse in order to live off the spoils are labouring to give a droll turn to.

There is not, perhaps, in the kingdom a more dogmatic or more luxurious set of men than the pedantic tyrants who live in colleges and preside over public schools. The holidays are equally damaging to the morals of masters and pupils; and the intercourse the masters keep up with the nobility introduces into their families the same vanity and extravagance that banish domestic duties and comforts from the lordly mansion whose state is so awkwardly aped on a smaller scale. The boys, who live at great expense with the masters and assistants, are never domesticated, though that is why they were placed there; for after a silent dinner they swallow a hasty glass of wine and retire to plan some mischievous trick, or to ridicule the person or manners of the very people they have just been cringing to, and whom they ought to consider as standing in for their parents.

Can it be surprising, then, that boys shut out from social conversation like this become selfish and vicious? Or that a bishop's mitre often ends up on the brow of one of these diligent pastors?

The desire to live in the same style as the rank just above them infects every individual and every class of people, and meanness is the companion of this ignoble ambition. But the most debasing professions are those whose ladder is patronage — and it is generally out of one of these professions that the tutors of youth are chosen. Can men whose conduct must be regulated by a cautious prudence forever on the watch for preferment be expected to inspire independent sentiments?

So far from thinking about the morals of boys, several schoolmasters have argued in my hearing that they only undertook to teach Latin and Greek, and that they had done their duty by sending some good scholars up to college.

A few good scholars may have been formed by emulation and discipline, I grant; but to bring these clever boys forward, the health and morals of a great many others have been sacrificed.

The sons of our gentry and wealthy commoners are mostly educated at these establishments — and will anyone claim that the majority, making every allowance, come under the description of tolerable scholars?

It is not for the benefit of society that a few brilliant men should be brought forward at the expense of the multitude. It is true that great men seem to spring up as great revolutions occur, at proper intervals, to restore order and blow aside the clouds that thicken over the face of truth; but let more reason and virtue prevail in society and these strong winds would not be needed. Public education of every description should be directed towards forming citizens; but if you wish to make good citizens, you must first exercise the affections of a son and a brother. That is the only way to expand the heart; for public affections, like public virtues, must always grow out of the private character, or they are only meteors shooting across a dark sky and vanishing as they are gazed at and admired.

Few people, I believe, have had much affection for humankind who did not first love their parents, their brothers and sisters, and even the household animals they first played with. The exercise of youthful sympathies forms the moral temperature; and it is the memory of these first affections and pursuits that gives life to the ones that come later under the direction of reason. It is in youth that the fondest friendships are formed, the genial juices rising at the same time and mixing kindly; or rather the heart, tempered to receive friendship, gets into the habit of looking for pleasure in something nobler than the churlish gratification of appetite.

To inspire a love of home and of domestic pleasures, then, children ought to be educated at home; for riotous holidays only make them fond of home for their own sakes. Yet the holidays, which do not foster domestic affections, continually disturb the course of study and make any plan of improvement that involves temperance abortive. Still, if they were abolished, children would be entirely separated from their parents, and I doubt whether they would become better citizens by sacrificing those preparatory affections, and destroying the force of the relationships that make married life as necessary as it is respectable. But if a private education produces self-importance, or isolates a man within his family, the evil is only shifted and not remedied.

This train of reasoning brings me back to a subject I mean to dwell on: the necessity of establishing proper day schools.

But these should be national establishments; for while schoolmasters depend on the caprice of parents, little exertion can be expected of them beyond what is needed to please ignorant people. Indeed, the necessity of a master's giving the parents some sample of a boy's abilities, which is shown to every visitor during the holidays, does more mischief than would at first be supposed. For these samples are seldom done entirely by the child itself, to put it moderately; so the master countenances falsehood, or else winds the poor machine up to some extraordinary exertion that damages the wheels and stops the progress of gradual improvement. The memory is loaded with unintelligible words to make a display with, while the understanding acquires no distinct ideas at all. But only that education deserves emphatically to be called a cultivation of the mind which teaches young people how to begin to think. The imagination should not be allowed to debauch the understanding before it has gained strength, or vanity will become the forerunner of vice; for every way of exhibiting a child's attainments is injurious to its moral character.

How much time is lost in teaching children to recite what they do not understand, while the mammas, seated on benches in their best array, listen with astonishment to the parrot-like prattle uttered in solemn cadences with all the pomp of ignorance and folly! Such exhibitions only serve to drive the spreading fibres of vanity through the whole mind, for they teach children neither to speak fluently nor to behave gracefully. So far from it, that these frivolous pursuits might comprehensively be called the study of affectation. We now rarely see a simple, bashful boy, though few people of taste were ever disgusted by the awkward sheepishness so natural to that age, which schools and an early introduction into society have turned into impudence and apish grimace.

Yet how can any of this be remedied while schoolmasters depend entirely on parents for a living, and while so many rival schools hang out their lures to catch the attention of vain fathers and mothers, whose parental affection only makes them wish their children to outshine the neighbours'?

Without great good luck, a sensible, conscientious man would starve before he could get a school going, if he disdained to fool weak parents by practising the secret tricks of the trade.

Even in the best-regulated schools, where swarms of children are not crammed together, many bad habits must be picked up; but at common schools the body, heart and understanding are equally stunted. For parents are often only in search of the cheapest school, and the master could not live if he did not take many more pupils than he can manage himself, nor will the scanty pittance allowed for each child let him hire enough ushers to help with the mechanical part of the business. Besides, whatever appearance the house and garden may make, the children get no comfort from either, since they are continually reminded by irksome restrictions that they are not at home, and the state rooms and the garden must be kept in order for the recreation of the parents — who visit the school on a Sunday and are impressed by the very parade that makes their children's situation uncomfortable.

With what disgust have I heard sensible women — for girls are more restrained and cowed than boys — speak of the wearisome confinement they endured at school. Not allowed, perhaps, to step off one broad walk in a superb garden, and obliged to pace stupidly up and down it with a steady deportment, heads up, toes turned out and shoulders braced back, instead of bounding about as nature directs in order to complete her own design, in all the various attitudes that are so good for the health. The pure animal spirits that make mind and body shoot up and unfold the tender blossoms of hope are turned sour, and vent themselves in vain wishes or pert complaints that contract the faculties and spoil the temper. Or else they mount to the brain and, sharpening the understanding before it has gained proportionate strength, produce that pitiful cunning which so disgracefully characterizes the female mind — and which will always characterize it, I fear, while women remain the slaves of power.

The little respect the male world pays to chastity is, I am persuaded, the grand source of many of the physical and moral evils that torment humankind, as well as of the vices and follies that degrade and destroy women. And yet at school boys infallibly lose the decent bashfulness that might have ripened into modesty at home.

I have already criticized the bad habits women pick up when they are shut up together; and I think the observation may fairly be extended to the other sex, until we draw the natural inference I have had in view all along — that to improve both sexes they ought to be educated together, not only in private families but in public schools. If marriage is the cement of society, everyone should be educated on the same model, or the intercourse of the sexes will never deserve the name of fellowship; nor will women ever fulfil the duties peculiar to their sex until they become enlightened citizens, until they become free by being enabled to earn their own subsistence independently of men — in the same sense, to prevent misunderstanding, in which one man is independent of another. Indeed, marriage will never be held sacred until women, by being brought up alongside men, are prepared to be their companions rather than their mistresses; for the mean doublings of cunning will always make them contemptible while oppression makes them timid. I am so convinced of this that I will venture to predict that virtue will never prevail in society until the virtues of both sexes are founded on reason, and until the affections common to both are allowed to gain their proper strength by the discharge of mutual duties.

If boys and girls were allowed to pursue the same studies together, those graceful decencies might be instilled early that produce modesty, without the distinctions of sex that taint the mind. Lessons in politeness, and the whole formula of decorum that treads on the heels of falsehood, would be made useless by a habitual propriety of behaviour — not put on for visitors like the courtly robe of politeness, but the sober effect of a cleanliness of mind. Would this simple elegance of sincerity not be a chaste homage paid to domestic affection, far beyond the meretricious compliments that shine with a false lustre in the heartless intercourse of fashionable life? But until more understanding prevails in society there will always be a want of heart and of taste, and the harlot's rouge will take the place of that heavenly glow which only virtuous affections can give a face. Gallantry, and what is called love, may exist without simplicity of character; but the main pillars of friendship are respect and confidence, and esteem is never founded on it-cannot-tell-what.

A taste for the fine arts needs a great deal of cultivation — but not more than a taste for the virtuous affections; and both presuppose that enlargement of mind which opens so many sources of mental pleasure. Why do people hurry off to noisy scenes and crowded parties? I should answer: because they lack activity of mind, because they have not cherished the virtues of the heart. They see and feel only in the gross, and are forever pining after variety, finding everything simple insipid.

This argument can be carried further than philosophers realise. For if nature destined women in particular for the discharge of domestic duties, she made them susceptible to the attached affections in a high degree. Now women are notoriously fond of pleasure, and by my definition they naturally must be, because they cannot enter into the fine detail of domestic taste, lacking the judgment that is the foundation of all taste. For the understanding, in spite of sensual cavillers, reserves to itself the privilege of conveying pure joy to the heart.

With what a languid yawn have I seen an admirable poem thrown down that a man of true taste returns to again and again with rapture; and, while a melody has almost suspended my breathing, a lady has asked me where I bought my gown. I have seen an eye glance coldly over a most exquisite picture and then rest, sparkling with pleasure, on a rudely sketched caricature; and while some terrific feature of nature has spread a sublime stillness through my soul, I have been asked to notice the pretty tricks of a lapdog that my perverse fate obliged me to travel with. Is it surprising that such a tasteless being should caress the dog rather than her children, or prefer the rant of flattery to the simple accents of sincerity?

To illustrate the remark I must be allowed to observe that men of the first genius and the most cultivated minds have appeared to have the highest relish for the simple beauties of nature; and they must have felt forcibly what they have described so well, the charm that natural affections and unsophisticated feelings spread round the human character. It is this power of looking into the heart, and of vibrating in response to every emotion, that lets the poet personify each passion and the painter sketch with a pencil of fire.

True taste is always the work of the understanding employed in observing natural effects; and until women have more understanding it is vain to expect them to have any domestic taste. Their lively senses will always be at work hardening their hearts, and the emotions struck out of them will go on being vivid and transitory, unless a proper education stores their minds with knowledge.

It is the want of domestic taste, and not the acquisition of knowledge, that takes women out of their families and tears the smiling baby from the breast that ought to feed it. Women have been left in ignorance and slavish dependence for many, many years, and still we hear of nothing but their fondness for pleasure and for power, their preference for rakes and soldiers, their childish attachment to toys, and the vanity that makes them value accomplishments above virtues.

History brings forward a fearful catalogue of the crimes their cunning has produced, when the weak slaves have had address enough to overreach their masters. In France, and in how many other countries, have men not been the luxurious despots and women the crafty ministers? Does that prove that ignorance and dependence make women domestic? Is their folly not a byword among the libertines who relax in their company? And do men of sense not constantly lament that an immoderate fondness for dress and dissipation takes the mother of a family away from home forever? Their hearts have not been debauched by knowledge, nor their minds led astray by scientific pursuits; and yet they do not fulfil the duties that as women nature calls on them to fulfil. On the contrary, the state of warfare that exists between the sexes makes them use the wiles that frustrate the more open designs of force.

When, therefore, I call women slaves, I mean it in a political and civil sense; for indirectly they get too much power, and are debased by their efforts to obtain an illicit sway.

Let an enlightened nation try what effect reason would have in bringing them back to nature and to their duty; and, allowing them to share the advantages of education and government with men, let it see whether they will become better as they grow wiser and freer. They cannot be injured by the experiment, for it is not in the power of man to make them more insignificant than they are at present.

To make this practicable, day schools for particular ages should be set up by government, in which boys and girls could be educated together. The school for the younger children, from five to nine years old, ought to be absolutely free and open to every class. A sufficient number of masters should be chosen by a select committee in each parish, to whom any complaint of negligence or the like could be made, if it were signed by six of the children's parents.

Footnote: In treating this part of the subject I have borrowed some hints from a very sensible pamphlet on public education written by the late bishop of Autun.

Ushers would then be unnecessary; for I believe experience will always show that this kind of subordinate authority is particularly harmful to the morals of the young. What can tend to deprave the character more than outward submission and inward contempt? And how can boys be expected to treat an usher with respect when the master seems to regard him as a servant, and almost to countenance the ridicule that becomes the boys' chief amusement during playtime?

But nothing of this kind could happen in an elementary day school where boys and girls, rich and poor, met together. And to prevent any of the distinctions of vanity they should be dressed alike, and all obliged to submit to the same discipline or leave the school. The schoolroom ought to be surrounded by a large piece of ground where the children could take useful exercise, for at this age they should not be confined to any sedentary employment for more than an hour at a time. But these relaxations might all be made part of an elementary education, since many things amuse and improve the senses when they are introduced as a kind of show, though children would turn a deaf ear to their principles dryly laid down — botany, mechanics and astronomy, for instance. Reading, writing, arithmetic, natural history and some simple experiments in natural philosophy might fill up the day; but these pursuits should never encroach on gymnastic games in the open air. The elements of religion, history, the history of humankind, and politics might also be taught by conversation, in the Socratic form.

After the age of nine, girls and boys intended for domestic employment or for a trade ought to be moved to other schools and given instruction suited in some measure to what each is destined for, the two sexes still being together in the morning; but in the afternoon the girls should attend a school where plain sewing, dressmaking, millinery and so on would be their occupation.

Young people of superior ability or fortune might now be taught in another school the dead and living languages, the elements of science, and might continue the study of history and politics on a wider scale, which would not exclude literature. Girls and boys still together, I hear some readers ask? Yes. And I should fear no other consequence than that some early attachment might form — which, while it had the best possible effect on the young people's moral character, might not perfectly suit their parents' plans; for it will be a long time, I am afraid, before the world is so enlightened that parents anxious only to make their children virtuous will let them choose their own companions for life.

Besides, this would be a sure way of promoting early marriages, and from early marriages the most wholesome physical and moral effects naturally flow. What a different character a married citizen takes on from the selfish coxcomb who lives only for himself, and who is often afraid to marry in case he should not be able to live in a certain style. Great emergencies excepted, which would rarely occur in a society founded on equality, a man can only be prepared to discharge the duties of public life by habitually practising the lesser duties that form the man.

Under this plan of education boys' constitutions would not be ruined by the early debaucheries that now make men so selfish, nor would girls be made weak and vain by idleness and frivolous pursuits. But I am presupposing such a degree of equality between the sexes as would shut out gallantry and coquetry, while still allowing friendship and love to temper the heart for the discharge of higher duties.

These would be schools of morality. And with human happiness allowed to flow from the pure springs of duty and affection, what advances might the human mind not make? Society can only be happy and free in proportion as it is virtuous; but the distinctions now established in society corrode all private virtue and blast all public virtue.

I have already inveighed against the custom of tying girls to their needlework and shutting them out of all political and civil employment; for by narrowing their minds in this way we make them unfit to fulfil the very duties nature has assigned them.

Occupied only with the little incidents of the day, they necessarily grow up cunning. My very soul has often sickened at the sly tricks women practise to get some foolish thing their silly hearts are set on. Not allowed to dispose of money or call anything their own, they learn to turn the market penny; or, if a husband gives offence by staying out, or stirs up some feeling of jealousy, a new gown or some pretty trinket smooths Juno's angry brow.

But these littlenesses would not degrade their character if women were led to respect themselves, if political and moral subjects were opened to them. And I will venture to say that this is the only way to make them properly attentive to their domestic duties. An active mind embraces the whole circle of its duties and finds time enough for all of them. It is not, I insist, a bold attempt to emulate masculine virtues that leads women away from duty; it is not the enchantment of literary pursuits or the steady investigation of scientific subjects. No — it is indolence and vanity, the love of pleasure and the love of power, that reign supreme in an empty mind. I say empty emphatically, because the education women now receive hardly deserves the name. The little knowledge they are led to acquire during the important years of youth relates only to accomplishments — and accomplishments with no bottom to them; for unless the understanding is cultivated, every grace is superficial and monotonous. Like the charms of a made-up face, they only strike the senses in a crowd; at home, lacking mind, they lack variety. The consequence is obvious: in the gay scenes of dissipation we meet the artificial mind and the artificial face, for those who fly from solitude dread the domestic circle next after solitude. Having no power to amuse or interest, they feel their own insignificance, or find nothing to amuse or interest themselves with.

Besides, what can be more indelicate than a girl's coming out in the fashionable world — which, in other words, means bringing a marriageable miss to market, her person carried richly caparisoned from one public place to another? And yet, mixing in the giddy circle under restraint, these butterflies long to flutter free; for the first affection of their souls is for their own persons, which their attention has been called to with the most assiduous care while they were being prepared for the season that decides their fate for life. Instead of pursuing this idle routine and sighing after tasteless show and heartless state, with what dignity might young people of both sexes form attachments in the schools I have sketched, where, as they grew older, dancing, music and drawing might be allowed as relaxations — for at these schools young people of fortune ought to stay, more or less, until they came of age. Those intended for particular professions might attend the schools set aside for their special instruction three or four mornings a week.

I only drop these observations as hints at present — an outline of my plan rather than a digested version of it. But I must add that I strongly approve of one regulation mentioned in the pamphlet I referred to earlier: making children and young people independent of their masters with respect to punishment. They should be tried by their peers, which would be an admirable way of fixing sound principles of justice in the mind, and might have the happiest effect on the temper, which is soured or irritated very early by tyranny, until it becomes peevishly cunning or ferociously overbearing.

Footnote: The Bishop of Autun.

My imagination darts forward with benevolent fervour to greet these amiable and respectable groups, in spite of the sneers of cold hearts, who are at liberty to utter with frigid self-importance the damning epithet: romantic. I shall try to blunt its force by repeating the words of an eloquent moralist: "I do not know whether the illusions of a truly humane heart, whose zeal makes everything easy, are not preferable to that rough and repelling reason which always finds its first obstacle to whatever would promote the public good in its own indifference to it."

I know that libertines will also exclaim that a woman would be unsexed by acquiring strength of body and mind, and that beauty, soft bewitching beauty, would no longer adorn the daughters of men. I am of a very different opinion, for I think we should then see dignified beauty and true grace, produced by the concurrence of many powerful physical and moral causes. Not a relaxed beauty, it is true, nor the graces of helplessness, but the kind we see in the relics of antiquity, which makes us respect the human body as a majestic building fit to receive a noble inhabitant.

I do not forget the popular opinion that the Greek statues were not modelled from nature — that is, not from the proportions of a particular man, but that beautiful limbs and features were selected from various bodies to make a harmonious whole. This may be true in some degree. The fine ideal picture in an exalted imagination might be superior to the materials the sculptor found in nature, and so it might properly be called a model of humanity rather than of a man. But it was not the mechanical selection of limbs and features; it was the ebullition of a heated fancy bursting out, and the fine senses and enlarged understanding of the artist selected the solid matter he drew into that glowing focus.

I said it was not mechanical, because a whole was produced: a model of that grand simplicity, those concurring energies, which arrest our attention and command our reverence. For a servile copy even of beautiful nature produces nothing but an insipid lifeless beauty. Yet, quite apart from these observations, I believe the human form must have been far more beautiful once than it is now, because extreme indolence, barbarous bandaging and the many other causes that act forcibly on it in our luxurious state of society did not then retard its expansion or deform it. Exercise and cleanliness appear to be not only the surest means of preserving health but of promoting beauty, considering the physical causes alone. Yet that is not sufficient; moral causes must work with them, or beauty will be merely the rustic kind that blooms on the innocent, wholesome faces of some country people whose minds have never been exercised. To make the person perfect, physical and moral beauty ought to be attained at the same time, each lending and receiving force from the combination. Judgment must sit on the brow, affection and fancy beam in the eye, and humanity curve the cheek, or the sparkle of the finest eye and the elegant finish of the fairest features are vain; while in every movement that displays the active limbs and well-knit joints, grace and modesty should appear. But this fair assemblage is not brought together by chance. It is the reward of exertions that support one another; for judgment can only be acquired by reflection, affection by the discharge of duties, and humanity by the exercise of compassion towards every living creature.

Humanity to animals should be particularly instilled as part of a national education, for it is not at present one of our national virtues. Among the lower classes, tenderness towards their humble dumb servants is more often found in a savage state than a civilized one. For civilization prevents the kind of contact that creates affection in the rude hut or the mud cabin, and leads uncultivated minds, depraved only by the refinements prevailing in the society where they are trodden underfoot by the rich, to domineer over animals in revenge for the insults they must bear from their own superiors.

This habitual cruelty is first caught at school, where it is one of the boys' rare sports to torment the miserable creatures that come their way. The transition, as they grow up, from barbarity towards animals to domestic tyranny over wives, children and servants is very easy. Justice, and even benevolence, will not be a powerful spring of action unless it extends to the whole of creation. Indeed I believe it may be laid down as an axiom that those who can watch pain unmoved will soon learn to inflict it.

The vulgar are swayed by present feelings and by habits they have picked up by accident; but not much dependence can be placed on partial feelings, however just they are, for when they are not invigorated by reflection, custom weakens them until they are hardly felt at all. The sympathies of our nature are strengthened by pondering and deadened by thoughtless use. Macbeth's heart smote him more for one murder, the first, than for the hundred that followed to back it up. But when I used the word vulgar, I did not mean to confine the remark to the poor; a partial humanity founded on present sensations or on whim is quite as conspicuous among the rich, if not more so.

The lady who sheds tears over a bird starved in a snare, and curses the devils in human shape who goad a poor ox to madness or whip a patient donkey tottering under a load beyond its strength, will nevertheless keep her coachman and horses waiting whole hours for her while the sharp frost bites, or the rain beats against the well-closed windows that do not let in a breath of air to tell her how roughly the wind is blowing outside. And the woman who takes her dogs to bed and nurses them with a parade of sensibility when they are sick will let her babies grow up crooked in a nursery. This illustration is drawn from fact. The woman I am referring to was handsome, reckoned very handsome by people who do not miss the mind when the face is plump and fair; but her understanding had not been drawn away from female duties by literature, nor her innocence debauched by knowledge. No: she was quite feminine, in the masculine sense of the word. And so far from loving the spoiled brutes that filled the place her children ought to have occupied, she only lisped out a pretty mixture of French and English nonsense to please the men who flocked round her. The wife, the mother and the human creature were all swallowed up in the artificial character that an improper education and the selfish vanity of beauty had produced.

I do not like to make a distinction without a difference, and I confess I have been as much disgusted by the fine lady who took her lapdog to her bosom instead of her child as by the ferocity of a man who, beating his horse, declared that the animal knew when it was doing wrong as well as any Christian.

This brood of folly shows how mistaken those people are who, if they allow women to leave their harems, do not cultivate their understandings in order to plant virtues in their hearts. For if they had sense they might acquire the domestic taste that would lead them to love their whole family in reasonable order, from the husband down to the house dog; nor would they ever insult humanity in the person of the meanest servant by paying more attention to an animal's comfort than to a fellow creature's.

My observations on national education are obviously only hints; but what I chiefly want to press is the necessity of educating the sexes together in order to perfect both, and of making children sleep at home so that they may learn to love home — yet, so that private affections support the public ones instead of smothering them, of sending them to school to mix with a number of equals, since it is only by the jostlings of equality that we can form a just opinion of ourselves.

To make humankind more virtuous, and consequently happier, both sexes must act on the same principle. But how can that be expected when only one of them is allowed to see the reasonableness of it? To make the social compact truly equitable, and to spread the enlightening principles that alone can better the fate of humanity, women must be allowed to found their virtue on knowledge, which is scarcely possible unless they are educated in the same pursuits as men. For they are now made so inferior by ignorance and low desires that they do not deserve to be ranked with men; or else, by the serpentine wrigglings of cunning, they climb the tree of knowledge and gather just enough to lead men astray.

It is plain from the history of every nation that women cannot be confined to merely domestic pursuits, for they will not fulfil their family duties unless their minds take a wider range; and while they are kept in ignorance, they become slaves to pleasure in exactly the proportion in which they are slaves to men. Nor can they be shut out of great enterprises, though the narrowness of their minds often makes them spoil what they cannot understand.

The libertinism, and even the virtues, of superior men will always give women of a certain description great power over them; and these weak women, under the influence of childish passions and selfish vanity, will throw a false light over the very objects that the men who ought to be enlightening their judgment look at through their eyes. Men of fancy, and those sanguine characters who mostly hold the helm of human affairs, generally relax in the society of women; and surely I need not cite for even the most superficial reader of history the many examples of vice and oppression that the private intrigues of female favourites have produced, not to dwell on the mischief that naturally arises from the blundering interference of well-meaning folly. For in the transaction of business it is much better to deal with a knave than a fool, because a knave sticks to some plan, and any plan of reason can be seen through much sooner than a sudden flight of folly. The power that vile and foolish women have had over wise men who possessed sensibility is notorious. I shall mention only one instance.

Who ever drew a more exalted female character than Rousseau, though on the whole he was constantly labouring to degrade the sex? And why was he so anxious to do it? Truly, to justify to himself the affection that weakness and virtue had made him cherish for that fool Theresa. He could not raise her to the common level of her sex, and so he laboured to bring women down to hers. He found her a convenient humble companion, and pride made him determined to find some superior virtue in the being he had chosen to live with. But did her conduct during his life, and after his death, not show plainly how grossly mistaken he was when he called her a celestial innocent? Indeed, in the bitterness of his heart he laments himself that once his bodily infirmities kept him from treating her as a woman, she ceased to have any affection for him. And that was very natural, for, having so few sentiments in common, what was there to hold her once the tie of sex was broken? To hold the affection of a woman whose sensibility is confined to one sex, indeed to one man, requires sense enough to turn sensibility into the broad channel of humanity. Many women have not mind enough to feel affection for a woman, or friendship for a man. But the weakness of sex that makes a woman depend on a man for her subsistence produces a kind of cattish affection, which leads a wife to purr about her husband as she would about any man who fed and caressed her.

Men are, however, often gratified by this kind of fondness, which is confined in a beastly way to themselves; but should they ever become more virtuous, they will want to converse at their fireside with a friend, once they have stopped playing with a mistress.

Besides, understanding is necessary to give variety and interest even to sensual enjoyments; for low indeed in the intellectual scale is the mind that can go on loving when neither virtue nor sense gives an animal appetite any human appearance. But sense will always preponderate; and if women in general are not brought more onto a level with men, some superior women, like the Greek courtesans, will gather men of ability round them and draw many citizens away from families they would have stayed at home in, if their wives had had more sense, or the graces that come from exercising the understanding and the fancy, which are the lawful parents of taste. A woman of talent, unless she is absolutely ugly, will always obtain great power, raised by the weakness of her sex; and as men acquire virtue and delicacy by exercising their reason, they will look for both in women — but women can only acquire them in the same way men do.

Have the women of France or Italy confined themselves to domestic life? Though they have had no political existence so far, have they not had great illicit sway, corrupting themselves and the men whose passions they played on? In short, in whatever light I look at the subject, reason and experience convince me that the only way of leading women to fulfil their peculiar duties is to free them from all restraint by allowing them to share in the inherent rights of humankind.

Make them free, and they will quickly become wise and virtuous, as men become more so; for the improvement must be mutual, or else the injustice that one half of the human race is obliged to submit to will recoil on their oppressors, and the virtue of man will be worm-eaten by the insect he keeps under his feet.

Let men take their choice. Man and woman were made for each other, though not to become one being; and if men will not improve women, they will deprave them.

I am speaking of the improvement and emancipation of the whole sex; for I know that the behaviour of a few women who by accident, or by following a strong natural bent, have acquired a share of knowledge beyond the rest of their sex has often been overbearing. But there have been instances of women who attained knowledge without discarding modesty, and who did not always appear pedantically to despise the ignorance they were labouring to disperse in their own minds. The outcry that any advice about female learning commonly produces, especially from pretty women, often arises from envy. When they happen to see that even the lustre of their eyes and the flippant sportiveness of a refined coquetry will not always secure them attention through a whole evening, if a woman of more cultivated understanding tries to give the conversation a rational turn, the common consolation is that such women seldom get husbands. What arts have I not seen silly women use to interrupt by flirtation — a very significant word for such a manoeuvre — a rational conversation that had made the men forget they were pretty women.

But allowing what is very natural in us, that the possession of rare abilities really is calculated to excite an overweening pride, disgusting in men and women alike — in what a state of inferiority must the female faculties have been rusting when so small a portion of knowledge as those women attained who have been sneeringly called learned could be considered singular? Singular enough to puff up its possessor and excite envy in her contemporaries, and in some of the other sex. Indeed, has a little rationality not exposed many women to the severest censure? I am referring to well-known facts, for I have often heard women ridiculed, and every little weakness of theirs exposed, merely because they took the advice of some medical man and departed from the beaten track in the way they treated their infants. I have heard this barbarous hatred of innovation carried further still, and a sensible woman stigmatized as an unnatural mother who had been wisely solicitous to preserve her children's health, when in the midst of all her care she lost one of them to some accident of infancy that no prudence could have warded off. Her acquaintances observed that this was the consequence of newfangled notions — the newfangled notions of ease and cleanliness. And those who pretend to experience, though they have long clung to prejudices that in the opinion of the most sagacious physicians have thinned the human race, almost rejoiced at a disaster that gave a kind of sanction to established practice.

Indeed, if it were only on this account, the national education of women is of the utmost consequence; for what a number of human sacrifices are made to that Moloch, prejudice! And in how many ways are children destroyed by the lasciviousness of men? The want of natural affection in many women, drawn away from their duty by the admiration of men, and the ignorance of others, make human infancy a far more perilous state than that of animals; and yet men are unwilling to put women in a position to acquire understanding enough even to know how to nurse their own babies.

This truth strikes me so forcibly that I would rest the whole tendency of my reasoning on it; for whatever tends to incapacitate a woman as a mother takes her out of her sphere.

But it is vain to expect the present race of weak mothers either to take the reasonable care of a child's body that is needed to lay the foundation of a good constitution — supposing it does not suffer for the sins of its father — or to manage its temper so judiciously that as it grows up the child will not have to throw off everything its mother, its first instructor, taught it directly or indirectly. And unless the mind has uncommon vigour, womanish follies will stick to the character all through life. The weakness of the mother will be visited on the children. And while women are educated to rely on their husbands' judgment, this must always be the consequence; for there is no improving an understanding by halves, nor can any being act wisely by imitation, because in every circumstance of life there is a kind of individuality that requires an exertion of judgment to modify the general rule. The being who can think justly along one track will soon extend its intellectual empire; and the woman who has judgment enough to manage her children will not submit, right or wrong, to her husband, or patiently to social laws that make a nonentity of a wife.

In public schools, women should be taught the elements of anatomy and medicine as a guard against the errors of ignorance, not only so that they can take proper care of their own health but so that they can be rational nurses to their infants, their parents and their husbands; for the bills of mortality are swollen by the blunders of self-willed old women who administer remedies of their own without knowing anything of the human frame. It is proper too, even from a purely domestic point of view, to make women acquainted with the anatomy of the mind, by letting the sexes associate together in every pursuit, and by leading them to observe the progress of the human understanding in the improvement of the sciences and arts — never forgetting the science of morality, nor the study of the political history of humankind.

A man has been called a microcosm, and every family might likewise be called a state. States, it is true, have mostly been governed by arts that disgrace human character; and the want of a just constitution and of equal laws has so confused the notions of the worldly wise that they more than question the reasonableness of contending for the rights of humanity. So morality, polluted in the national reservoir, sends off streams of vice to corrupt the constituent parts of the body politic. But if nobler, or rather juster, principles regulated the laws — which ought to be the government of society, rather than the men who execute them — duty might become the rule of private conduct.

Besides, by exercising their bodies and their minds, women would acquire that mental activity so necessary in a mother, together with the fortitude that distinguishes steadiness of conduct from the obstinate perverseness of weakness. For it is dangerous to advise the indolent to be steady, because they instantly become rigorous, and to save themselves trouble punish with severity the faults that a patient fortitude of reason would have prevented. But fortitude presupposes strength of mind — and is strength of mind to be acquired by indolent acquiescence? By asking for advice instead of exerting the judgment? By obeying out of fear instead of practising the forbearance we all need to have shown us? The conclusion I wish to draw is obvious. Make women rational creatures and free citizens, and they will quickly become good wives and mothers — that is, if men do not neglect the duties of husbands and fathers.

In discussing the advantages that a combined public and private education, of the kind I have sketched, might reasonably be expected to produce, I have dwelt most on those that particularly concern the female world, because I think the female world is oppressed. Yet the gangrene produced by the vices that oppression breeds is not confined to the diseased part; it pervades society at large. So that when I wish to see my sex become more like moral agents, my heart leaps at the prospect of the general spread of that sublime contentment which only morality can spread.

Chapter Thirteen: Examples of the Folly That Women's Ignorance Produces, with Closing Reflections on a Revolution in the Manners of Women

There are many follies more or less peculiar to women: sins against reason, of commission as well as omission, though all of them flow from ignorance or prejudice. I shall point out only those that seem damaging to their moral character. And in criticizing them I particularly want to prove that the weakness of mind and body which men have tried by various motives to perpetuate prevents women from discharging the very duty peculiar to their sex. For when weakness of body will not let them nurse their children, and weakness of mind makes them spoil their tempers — is woman then in a natural state?

Section One: Fortune-Tellers and Credulity

One glaring instance of the weakness that comes from ignorance claims attention first, and calls for severe reproof.

In this city a number of lurking leeches make a living, infamously, by working on the credulity of women, pretending to cast nativities, to use the technical phrase. And many women who, proud of their rank and fortune, look down on ordinary people with sovereign contempt show by this credulity that the distinction is arbitrary, and that they have not cultivated their minds enough to rise above vulgar prejudices. Women, never having been led to consider the knowledge of their duty as the one thing needful to know, or to live in the present moment by discharging it, are very anxious to peep into the future, to learn what they have to expect that might make life interesting, and to fill up the vacuum of ignorance.

I must be allowed to remonstrate seriously with the ladies who follow these idle inventions; for ladies, mistresses of families, are not ashamed to drive in their own carriages to the fortune-teller's door. And if any of them should read this book, I beg them to answer the following questions in their own hearts, remembering that they are in the presence of God.

Do you believe that there is only one God, and that he is powerful, wise and good?

Do you believe that all things were created by him, and that every being depends on him?

Do you rely on his wisdom, so conspicuous in his works and in your own frame; and are you convinced that he has ordered everything outside the reach of your senses in the same perfect harmony, to fulfil his designs?

Do you acknowledge that the power of looking into the future, and of seeing things that are not as though they were, is an attribute of the Creator? And if he thought fit to impart to his creatures, by an impression on their minds, some event still hidden in the shades of time and unborn, to whom would the secret be revealed by direct inspiration? The opinion of the ages will answer that question: to reverend old men, and to people distinguished for eminent piety.

The oracles of antiquity were delivered in that way, by priests dedicated to the service of the god who was supposed to inspire them. The glare of worldly pomp surrounding these impostors, and the respect paid them by artful politicians who knew how to use so convenient an engine for bending the necks of the strong under the dominion of the cunning, spread a sacred, mysterious veil of sanctity over their lies and abominations.

Impressed by such solemn devotional parade, a Greek or Roman lady might be excused for enquiring of the oracle when she was anxious to pry into the future or to ask about some doubtful event; and her enquiries, however contrary to reason, could not be called impious. But can professing Christians ward off that charge? Can a Christian suppose that the favourites of the Most High would be obliged to lurk in disguise and practise the most dishonest tricks to cheat silly women out of the money that the poor cry for in vain?

Do not say such questions are an insult to common sense; for it is your own conduct, you foolish women, that brings odium on your sex. And these reflections ought to make you shudder at your own thoughtlessness and irrational devotion, for I do not suppose that all of you laid your religion aside, such as it is, when you entered those mysterious dwellings. Yet, since I have been supposing throughout that I am talking to ignorant women — and ignorant you are, in the most emphatic sense of the word — it would be absurd to reason with you about the egregious folly of wanting to know what Supreme Wisdom has concealed.

Probably you would not understand me if I tried to show you that it would be absolutely inconsistent with the grand purpose of life, which is to make human creatures wise and virtuous; and that if it were sanctioned by God it would disturb the order established in creation — while if it is not sanctioned by God, do you expect to hear the truth? Can events be foretold, events that have not yet taken on a body and become subject to mortal inspection? Can they be foreseen by a vicious worldling who pampers his appetites by preying on the foolish?

Perhaps, however, you devoutly believe in the devil, and imagine, to shift the question, that he may assist his votaries. But if you really believe in the power of such a being, an enemy to goodness and to God, can you go to church after being under an obligation to him?

From these delusions to the still more fashionable deceptions practised by the whole tribe of magnetizers, the transition is very natural. And with regard to them, it is equally proper to ask women a few questions.

Do you know anything about the construction of the human frame? If not, it is proper you should be told what every child ought to know: that when its admirable economy has been disturbed by intemperance or idleness — I am not speaking of violent disorders but of chronic diseases — it must be brought back to a healthy state by slow degrees. And that if the functions of life have not been materially injured, regimen, which is another word for temperance, along with air, exercise and a few medicines prescribed by people who have studied the human body, are the only human means yet discovered of recovering that priceless blessing, health, which will bear investigation.

Do you then believe that these magnetizers who pretend, by hocus-pocus tricks, to work a miracle are delegated by God, or assisted by the solver of all difficulties of this kind, the devil?

When they put to flight, as it is said, disorders that have baffled the powers of medicine, do they work in conformity with the light of reason? Or do they perform these wonderful cures by supernatural aid?

By communication, an adept may answer, with the world of spirits. A noble privilege, it must be allowed. Some of the ancients mention familiar spirits who guarded them from danger by kindly intimating — we cannot guess in what manner — when danger was near, or by pointing out what they ought to undertake. Yet the men who claimed this privilege outside the order of nature insisted that it was the reward or the consequence of superior temperance and piety. But the present workers of wonders are not raised above their fellows by any superior temperance or sanctity. They do not cure for the love of God but for money. These are the priests of quackery, though it is true they do not have the convenient expedient of selling masses for souls in purgatory, nor churches where they can display crutches and models of limbs made whole by a touch or a word.

I am not familiar with the technical terms, nor initiated into the mysteries, so I may speak improperly; but it is clear that men who will not conform to the law of reason and earn a living honestly, by degrees, are very fortunate to have become acquainted with such obliging spirits. We cannot give the spirits credit for much sagacity or goodness, at any rate, or they would have chosen nobler instruments when they wished to show themselves the benevolent friends of humankind.

It is, however, little short of blasphemy to claim such a power.

From the whole tenor of the dispensations of Providence it appears evident to sober reason that certain vices produce certain effects. And can anyone so grossly insult the wisdom of God as to suppose that a miracle will be permitted to disturb his general laws, and restore the intemperate and vicious to health merely so that they can go on in the same course with impunity? Be whole, and sin no more, said Jesus. And are greater miracles to be performed by people who do not follow in his footsteps — who healed the body in order to reach the mind?

Mentioning the name of Christ after such vile impostors may displease some of my readers. I respect their warmth; but let them not forget that the followers of these delusions bear his name and profess to be the disciples of him who said that by their works we should know who were the children of God and who the servants of sin. I allow that it is easier to touch the body of a saint, or to be magnetized, than to restrain our appetites or govern our passions; but health of body or of mind can only be recovered by those means, or else we make the Supreme Judge partial and vengeful.

Is he a man, that he should change, or punish out of resentment? He, the common father, wounds only to heal, says reason; and since our irregularities produce certain consequences, we are forcibly shown the nature of vice, so that, learning by experience to tell good from evil, we may hate the one and love the other in proportion to the wisdom we attain. The poison contains its own antidote; and either we reform our evil habits and stop sinning against our own bodies, to use the forcible language of scripture, or a premature death, the punishment of sin, snaps the thread of life.

Here an awful stop is put to our enquiries. But why should I conceal what I think? Considering the attributes of God, I believe that whatever punishment follows will tend, like the anguish of disease, to show the malignity of vice for the purpose of reformation. Positive punishment appears so contrary to the nature of God, discoverable in all his works and in our own reason, that I could sooner believe the Deity paid no attention to human conduct at all than that he punished without the benevolent design of reforming.

Merely to suppose that an all-wise and powerful Being, as good as he is great, should create a being while foreseeing that after fifty or sixty years of feverish existence it would be plunged into never-ending woe is blasphemy. What will the worm feed on that is never to die? On folly, on ignorance, you say. I should blush with indignation to draw the natural conclusion, if I could set it down, and should wish to withdraw myself from the wing of my God. On such a supposition, I speak with reverence, he would be a consuming fire. We should wish, though vainly, to fly from his presence, when fear had absorbed love and darkness enveloped all his counsels.

I know that many devout people boast of submitting blindly to the will of God, as to an arbitrary sceptre or rod, on the same principle as the Indians worship the devil. In other words, like people in the common concerns of life, they do homage to power and cringe under the foot that can crush them. Rational religion, on the contrary, is submission to the will of a being so perfectly wise that everything he wills must be directed by the proper motive, and must be reasonable.

And if that is how we respect God, can we give credit to the mysterious insinuations that insult his laws? Can we believe, though it stared us in the face, that he would work a miracle to authorize confusion by sanctioning an error? We must either accept these impious conclusions, or treat with contempt every promise to restore a diseased body to health by supernatural means, or to foretell incidents that only God can foresee.

Section Two: Sentiment and the Reading of Novels

Another instance of that feminine weakness of character so often produced by a confined education is a romantic twist of the mind, which has very properly been called sentimental.

Women, subjected by ignorance to their own sensations and taught to look for happiness only in love, refine on sensual feelings and adopt metaphysical notions about that passion which lead them shamefully to neglect the duties of life — and often, in the middle of these sublime refinements, they plunge into actual vice.

These are the women who are amused by the reveries of stupid novelists, who, knowing little of human nature, work up stale tales and describe meretricious scenes, all retailed in a sentimental jargon that equally corrupts the taste and draws the heart aside from its daily duties. I do not mention the understanding, because, never having been exercised, its slumbering energies lie inactive, like the lurking particles of fire that are supposed to pervade all matter.

Women, in fact, denied all political privileges and allowed no civil existence once married, except in criminal cases, naturally have their attention drawn away from the interest of the whole community to that of its minute parts — though any member of society must perform their private duty very imperfectly when it is not connected with the general good. The mighty business of a woman's life is to please; and, kept out of more important concerns by political and civil oppression, sentiments become events, and reflection deepens what it should and would have effaced, if the understanding had been allowed a wider range.

But, confined to trifling employments, women naturally absorb the opinions inspired by the only kind of reading calculated to interest an innocent, frivolous mind. Unable to grasp anything great, is it surprising that they find the reading of history a very dry business, and discussions addressed to the understanding intolerably tedious and almost unintelligible? So they are necessarily dependent on the novelist for amusement. Yet when I exclaim against novels, I mean by contrast with works that exercise the understanding and regulate the imagination. Any kind of reading I think better than leaving a blank a blank, because the mind must be enlarged a little, and gain a little strength, by any slight exertion of its thinking powers; and besides, even productions addressed only to the imagination raise a reader a little above the gross gratification of appetites that the mind has given no shade of delicacy to.

This observation is the result of experience. I have known several capable women, and one in particular who was a very good woman — as good as such a narrow mind would let her be — who took care that her daughters, three in number, should never see a novel. Being a woman of fortune and fashion, she provided them with various masters, and a sort of menial governess to watch their steps. From their masters they learned what tables and chairs and so on are called in French and Italian; but as the few books that came their way were either far above their capacities or devotional, they acquired neither ideas nor sentiments, and passed their time, when they were not compelled to repeat words, in dressing, quarrelling with one another, or talking to the maids in secret, until they were brought out as marriageable.

Their mother, a widow, was busy meanwhile keeping up her connections, as she called a numerous acquaintance, in case her girls should lack a proper introduction into the great world. And these young ladies, with minds vulgar in every sense of the word and with spoiled tempers, entered life puffed up with notions of their own consequence, looking down with contempt on anyone who could not match them in dress and display.

As for love, nature, or their nurses, had taken care to teach them the physical meaning of the word; and as they had few subjects of conversation and fewer refinements of sentiment, they expressed their gross wishes in no very delicate phrases when they spoke freely and talked about matrimony.

Could these girls have been harmed by reading novels? I had almost forgotten one shade in the character of one of them: she affected a simplicity bordering on folly, and would utter, with a simper, the most immodest remarks and questions, the full meaning of which she had picked up while she was shut away from the world, afraid to speak in the presence of her mother, who governed with a high hand. They were all educated, as their mother prided herself, in the most exemplary manner, and read their chapters and psalms before breakfast, never touching a silly novel.

That is only one instance; but I remember many other women who, never led by degrees to proper studies and never allowed to choose for themselves, have indeed been overgrown children — or else have picked up, from mixing in the world, a little of what is called common sense, meaning a distinct way of seeing common occurrences as they stand detached. But what deserves the name of intellect, the power of forming general or abstract ideas, or even intermediate ones, was out of the question. Their minds were quiescent, and when they were not roused by tangible objects and employments of that sort, they were low-spirited, and would cry, or go to sleep.

When, therefore, I advise my sex not to read such flimsy works, it is to induce them to read something better; for I agree with a sagacious man who, having a daughter and a niece in his care, followed a very different plan with each.

The niece, who had considerable ability, had been indulged in desultory reading before she was left to his guardianship. Her he tried to lead, and did lead, to history and moral essays. But his daughter, whom a fond weak mother had indulged and who consequently hated anything resembling application, he allowed to read novels, justifying himself by saying that if she ever acquired a relish for reading them he would have some foundation to work on, and that mistaken opinions were better than none at all.

In fact the female mind has been so totally neglected that knowledge could only be got from this muddy source, until some women of superior talent learned from reading novels to despise them.

The best method for correcting a fondness for novels, I believe, is to ridicule them — not indiscriminately, for then it would have little effect, but by having a judicious person with some turn for humour read several aloud to a young girl, pointing out by tone and by apt comparison with pathetic incidents and heroic characters in history how foolishly and ridiculously they caricature human nature. Just opinions might then be put in the place of romantic sentiments.

In one respect, however, the majority of both sexes are alike, and show an equal want of taste and modesty. Ignorant women, forced to be chaste to preserve their reputations, let their imaginations revel in the unnatural and meretricious scenes sketched by the novel-writers of the day, dismissing as insipid the sober dignity and matronly grace of history; while men carry the same vitiated taste into life, and fly for amusement to the wanton, away from the unsophisticated charms of virtue and the grave respectability of sense.

Footnote: I am not now referring to that superiority of mind which leads to the creation of ideal beauty, when life, surveyed with a penetrating eye, appears a tragicomedy in which little can be found to satisfy the heart without the help of fancy.

Besides, reading novels makes women, and ladies of fashion particularly, very fond of using strong expressions and superlatives in conversation; and though the dissipated, artificial life they lead prevents them from cherishing any strong legitimate passion, the language of passion in affected tones slips forever off their glib tongues, and every trifle produces those phosphoric flashes that only mimic the flame of passion in the dark.

Section Three: Dress and Vanity

Ignorance, and the mistaken cunning that nature sharpens in weak heads as a principle of self-preservation, make women very fond of dress, and produce all the vanity such a fondness may naturally be expected to breed, to the exclusion of emulation and magnanimity.

I agree with Rousseau that the physical part of the art of pleasing consists in ornament; and for that very reason I would guard girls against the contagious fondness for dress so common among weak women, so that they may not stop at the physical part. Yet weak indeed are the women who imagine they can please for long without the aid of the mind — in other words, without the moral art of pleasing. And the moral art, if it is not a profanation to use the word art of a grace that is the effect of virtue rather than the motive for action, is never found together with ignorance. The sportiveness of innocence, so pleasing to refined libertines of both sexes, is quite different in essence from this superior gracefulness.

A strong inclination for outward ornament always appears in barbarous states — only there it is the men and not the women who adorn themselves; for wherever women are allowed to be so far on a level with men, society has advanced at least one step in civilization.

The attention to dress, then, which has been thought a propensity of one sex, I think natural to humankind. But I ought to put it more precisely. When the mind is not sufficiently opened to take pleasure in reflection, the body will be adorned with assiduous care, and ambition will show itself in tattooing or painting it.

The first inclination is carried so far that even the hellish yoke of slavery cannot stifle the savage craving for admiration that enslaved people inherit from both their parents alike, for the hard-earned savings of a slave are commonly spent on a little tawdry finery. And I have seldom known a good servant, male or female, who was not particularly fond of dress. Their clothes were their riches. And I argue by analogy that the fondness for dress, so extravagant in women, arises from the same cause: a want of cultivation of mind. When men meet they talk about business, politics or literature; but, says Swift, "how naturally do women apply their hands to each other's lappets and ruffles." And it is very natural, for they have no business to interest them, no taste for literature, and they find politics dry, because they have not acquired a love of humankind by turning their thoughts to the grand pursuits that exalt the human race and promote general happiness.

Besides, the paths to power and fame that men pursue by accident or choice are various; and though they jostle each other — for men of the same profession are seldom friends — there are a great many more of their fellow creatures they never clash with at all. But women are very differently situated with regard to each other, for they are all rivals.

Before marriage their business is to please men, and afterwards, with a few exceptions, they follow the same scent with all the persevering pertinacity of instinct. Even virtuous women never forget their sex in company, for they are forever trying to make themselves agreeable. A female beauty and a male wit appear to be equally anxious to draw the company's attention to themselves — and the animosity of rival wits is proverbial.

Is it surprising, then, that when a woman's whole ambition centres on beauty, and self-interest gives vanity additional force, perpetual rivalries should follow? They are all running the same race, and would be more than mortally virtuous if they did not look at each other with a suspicious and even envious eye.

An immoderate fondness for dress, for pleasure and for power are the passions of savages: the passions that occupy uncivilized beings who have not yet extended the dominion of the mind, or even learned to think with the energy needed to link up the abstract train of thought that produces principles. And that women, from their education and the present state of civilized life, are in the same condition cannot, I think, be disputed. To laugh at them, then, or satirize the follies of a being who is never to be allowed to act freely by the light of her own reason, is as absurd as it is cruel; for it is entirely natural and certain that those who are taught to obey authority blindly will try to elude it cunningly.

Yet let it be proved that they ought to obey men implicitly, and I shall agree at once that it is a woman's duty to cultivate a fondness for dress in order to please, and a propensity to cunning for her own preservation.

But virtues supported by ignorance must always be unsteady — the house built on sand could not stand a storm. It is hardly necessary to draw the inference. If women are to be made virtuous by authority, which is a contradiction in terms, then let them be shut up in harems and watched with a jealous eye. Have no fear that the iron will enter into their souls; for the souls that can bear such treatment are made of yielding stuff, just animated enough to give the body life.

	"Matter too soft a lasting mark to bear,
	And best distinguish'd by black, brown, or fair."

The cruellest wounds will soon heal, of course, and they may still people the world and dress to please men — all the purposes that certain celebrated writers have allowed they were created to fill.

Section Four: The Sensibility Women Are Supposed to Have

Women are supposed to have more sensibility, and even more humanity, than men, and their strong attachments and instantaneous emotions of compassion are offered as proof. But the clinging affection of ignorance has seldom anything noble in it, and can mostly be resolved into selfishness, like the affection of children and of animals. I have known many weak women whose sensibility was entirely engrossed by their husbands; and as for their humanity, it was very faint indeed, or rather it was only a passing emotion of compassion. "Humanity does not consist in a squeamish ear," says an eminent orator. "It belongs to the mind as well as the nerves."

But this kind of exclusive affection, though it degrades the individual, should not be brought forward as proof of the inferiority of the sex, because it is the natural consequence of confined views. Even women of superior sense, having their attention turned to little employments and private plans, rarely rise to heroism unless they are spurred on by love; and love as a heroic passion, like genius, appears only once in an age. So I agree with the moralist who asserts "that women have seldom so much generosity as men", and that their narrow affections, to which justice and humanity are often sacrificed, make the sex apparently inferior — especially as those affections are commonly inspired by men. But I maintain that the heart would expand as the understanding gained strength, if women were not kept down from their cradles.

I know that a little sensibility and a great deal of weakness will produce a strong attachment between the sexes, and that reason must cement friendship; so I allow that more friendship is to be found in the male world than the female, and that men have a higher sense of justice. The exclusive affections of women do indeed resemble Cato's most unjust love for his country. He wished to crush Carthage not to save Rome but to promote its vainglory; and it is generally to principles like these that humanity is sacrificed, for genuine duties support each other.

Besides, how can women be just or generous when they are the slaves of injustice?

Section Five: The Rearing of Children

Since the rearing of children — that is, laying a foundation of sound health of body and mind in the rising generation — has justly been insisted on as woman's peculiar destination, the ignorance that unfits her for it must be contrary to the order of things. And I maintain that women's minds can take in much more than they are given, and ought to, or they will never become sensible mothers. Many men attend to the breeding of horses and oversee the running of the stable who would think themselves degraded — strange want of sense and feeling! — by paying any attention to the nursery. Yet how many children are absolutely murdered by the ignorance of women! And when they escape, and are destroyed neither by unnatural negligence nor by blind fondness, how few are properly managed as far as the infant mind goes! So that a child which has been allowed to grow vicious at home is sent to school to have its spirit broken; and the methods used there, which must be used to keep a number of children in order, scatter the seeds of nearly every vice in soil that has just been forcibly torn up.

I have sometimes compared the struggles of these poor children, who ought never to have felt restraint and would not have needed to if they had always been held with an even hand, to the despairing plunges of a spirited filly I once watched being broken on a beach: its feet sinking deeper into the sand every time it tried to throw its rider, until at last it sullenly submitted.

I have always found horses, an animal I am attached to, very tractable when they are treated with humanity and steadiness; so I doubt whether the violent methods used to break them do not do them real harm. I am certain, at any rate, that a child should never be forcibly tamed like that after being injudiciously allowed to run wild; for every violation of justice and reason in the treatment of children weakens their reason. And children take on a character so early that the base of the moral character is fixed, experience leads me to infer, before their seventh year — the very period during which women are allowed the sole management of them. Afterwards it too often happens that half the business of education is correcting, and very imperfectly if it is done in a hurry, the faults they would never have acquired if their mothers had had more understanding.

One striking instance of women's folly must not be left out: the way they treat servants in front of children, letting them suppose that servants ought to wait on them and put up with their moods. A child should always be made to accept help from a man or a woman as a favour; and as the first lesson in independence, children should be taught by their mother's practical example not to demand the personal attendance that it is an insult to humanity to demand when one is in health. Instead of being led to assume airs of consequence, a sense of their own weakness should first make them feel the natural equality of all people. Yet how often have I heard with indignation servants imperiously called to put children to bed, and sent away again and again because master or miss was hanging about mamma to stay a little longer. Made to dance slavish attendance on the little idol in this way, they were treated to all the disgusting moods that characterize a spoiled child.

In short, speaking of most mothers, they either leave their children entirely to the care of servants, or else treat them, because they are their children, as if they were little demigods — though I have always observed that the women who idolize their children in this way seldom show common humanity to a servant, or feel the least tenderness for any children but their own.
